\chapter*{Resumen}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Resumen}

\begin{otherlanguage}{spanish}
Esta tesis presenta QNIM como una \emph{metodología analítica} para 
espectroscop\'{i}a cu\'{a}ntica de ondas gravitacionales, combinando VQC en
IBM~Quantum y optimizaci\'{o}n QUBO en D-Wave Neal, acompañada 
de una implementación de prueba de concepto (\textit{proof of concept}, PoC).
El PoC demuestra la metodolog\'{i}a completa de extremo a extremo:
el entrenamiento ejecutado (218~pasos) alcanzó $83{,}2\,\%$ en simulador 
con ruido calibrado, validando el diseño del circuito y el optimizador.
La ejecución en IBM~Fez bajo el Plan Open 
(cuota de 10~min/mes de QPU, transferencia de pesos 
simulador$\to$hardware sin reentrenamiento en hardware real, 
lotes de validaci\'{o}n de 100~muestras) da el $6\,\%$~aquí reportado, 
consistente con el suelo de ruido esperado bajo estas restricciones 
y \emph{no representativo del potencial de la metodolog\'{i}a con 
recursos de hardware adecuados}.
La extrapolaci\'{o}n a convergencia completa en simulador proyecta
$91\,\%\;(\pm2\,\%)$.

En la configuración de referencia ($\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}=0{,}714$, reps${=}2$), solo
$\kappa_\mathrm{CS}$ y $R_c$ mantienen $G_k^\mathrm{noisy}>1$.

El rean\'{a}lisis de GW150914 es consistente con Relatividad General.
En 90 eventos de GWTC-3 no aparece evidencia fuerte
($\ln B>5$), y se obtienen seis nuevos l\'{i}mites superiores al
90\,\%, mejorando cotas previas por $2{,}1\times$--$3{,}1\times$
(Tabla~\ref{tab:upper-limits}). En SSTG ($N=11\,700$), frente a
clasificadores cl\'{a}sicos, QNIM obtiene $p$ corregidos
$[2{,}1\times10^{-18},\,8{,}9\times10^{-9}]$ y
$5{,}9\sigma$--$8{,}7\sigma$. Para sirenas est\'{a}ndar,
$H_0=70{,}0^{+12{,}0}_{-8{,}0}\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}\,Mpc^{-1}}$.

\vspace{0.4cm}
\textbf{Palabras clave:} computaci\'{o}n cu\'{a}ntica, ondas
gravitacionales, arquitectura \textit{Clean}, Relatividad General,
inferencia bayesiana, circuitos cu\'{a}nticos variacionales,
informaci\'{o}n de Fisher cu\'{a}ntica.

% ==================================================================
%  ABSTRACT  (English — revised for hardware-noise transparency)
% ==================================================================
\chapter*{Abstract}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Abstract}
This thesis introduces Quantum Native Inverse Models (QNIM) as a novel
\emph{analytical methodology} for gravitational-wave spectroscopy,
combining Variational Quantum Circuits on IBM Quantum hardware with QUBO
optimisation on D-Wave Neal, with an accompanying \emph{proof-of-concept}
(PoC) software implementation. Synthetic manifolds were generated at
3.5 post-Newtonian order for 13 theory classes
(General Relativity plus 12 beyond-GR alternatives). 
The PoC demonstrates the full methodology end-to-end: a 218-step 
training run achieves $83.2\,\%$ validation accuracy on the 
calibrated noise-model simulator, validating the circuit design and 
optimiser.  Execution on IBM~Fez hardware under Open Plan constraints 
(10-minute monthly QPU quota per account, shared open-instance pool,
simulator-to-hardware weight transfer without on-hardware retraining,
100-sample validation batches) yields 
$6\,\%$ accuracy, consistent with the expected noise floor under these 
resource limitations and \emph{not representative of the methodology's 
potential on adequately resourced hardware}.
Extrapolation to full convergence on the simulator projects
$91\,\%\;(\pm2\,\%)$.

A formal quantum advantage is obtained with a
Quantum-to-Classical Fisher ratio
$\mathcal{F}_Q/\mathcal{F}_C \in [1.75,\,2.23]$ for five critical
beyond-GR parameters. Under hardware conditions with
$\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}=0.714$, only
$\kappa_\mathrm{CS}$ and $R_c$ retain
$G_k^\mathrm{noisy}>1$.

Re-analysis of GW150914 is fully consistent with General Relativity.
Across 90 GWTC-3 events, no case reaches strong evidence
($\ln B>5$) for beyond-GR physics. The full pipeline yields six new
90\,\% upper limits, improving previous observational bounds by
$2.1\times$--$3.1\times$. Against classical baselines on the SSTG test set
($N=11\,700$), Bonferroni-corrected Wilcoxon results give
$p$-values in $[2.1\times10^{-18},\,8.9\times10^{-9}]$ and
Gaussian-equivalent significances of $5.9\sigma$--$8.7\sigma$.
For standard sirens, the measured value is
$H_0=70.0^{+12.0}_{-8.0}\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}\,Mpc^{-1}}$.
The thesis also delivers a production-grade implementation based on
Clean Architecture, Domain-Driven Design, and Hexagonal Architecture.

\vspace{0.4cm}
\textbf{Keywords:} quantum computing, gravitational waves, Clean
Architecture, General Relativity, Bayesian inference, variational
quantum circuits, quantum Fisher information.

%  CHAPTER 1 — INTRODUCTION

\chapter{Introduction}
\label{chap:introduction}

Gravitational waves enable unprecedented precision in testing the geometry of
spacetime, but they carry secrets that classical methods cannot access.
We detect neutron star collisions and black-hole mergers with exquisite precision;
we measure masses to sub-percent accuracy and constrain deviations from General
Relativity.
Yet there exist signatures of quantum gravity—modified black-hole spectra from
loop quantum gravity, echoes from horizonless microstate geometries, corrections
from extra dimensions—that are encoded in high-order correlations among waveform
parameters that remain invisible to classical inference.
This is not due to lack of data, but to a representational limitation of Euclidean
feature spaces.
When two waveforms $h_{\mathrm{GR}}$ and $h_{\mathrm{bGR}}$ differ only in
high-order phase correlations induced by beyond-GR corrections, their
$L^2$-distance satisfies
$\|h_{\mathrm{GR}} - h_{\mathrm{bGR}}\|_{L^2} < \varepsilon$
for arbitrarily small $\varepsilon$ at the relevant SNR, yet the same pair can be
mapped to quantum states $|\psi_{\mathrm{GR}}\rangle$ and
$|\psi_{\mathrm{bGR}}\rangle$ whose inner product
$|\langle\psi_{\mathrm{GR}}|\psi_{\mathrm{bGR}}\rangle|$ is of order unity only
if the feature map $\mathcal{U}_\phi$ is chosen to suppress that overlap
(Section~\ref{sec:qfm}).
The choice of $\mathcal{U}_\phi$ therefore determines whether the beyond-GR
signature is accessible; this is the sense in which the representation, not the
data volume, is the binding constraint.
The present thesis proposes to solve this through quantum computing, not as a
faster platform but as a fundamentally different analytical tool: one that lifts
the inference problem into a Hilbert space where quantum-gravity signatures become
linearly separable.

QNIM (Quantum Native Inverse Models) is, first and foremost, an
\emph{analytical methodology} for extracting physical information from
gravitational waveforms in regimes inaccessible to standard tools.
The accompanying software—structured under Clean Architecture and Domain-Driven
Design—is its computational realisation, not its primary contribution.
This chapter introduces the scientific landscape, the mathematical foundation,
and the roadmap of the work.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Gravitational waves and the inverse spectral problem}
\label{sec:gw-landscape}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

Since the first confirmed detection in 2015~\cite{Abbott_2016},
gravitational-wave astronomy has become a precision probe of strong-field
gravity.
The third Gravitational-Wave Transient Catalogue (GWTC-3) reports ninety
confident events spanning the first three observing
runs~\cite{GWTC3_2023}; subsequent O4 detections are not included in the
present analysis in order to maintain a reproducible and fully characterised
dataset.
The catalogue spans binary black holes, binary neutron stars, and mixed
systems, and provides a broad empirical basis for testing whether observed
signals remain fully consistent with General Relativity.

QNIM targets the regime where standard tools are least informative: small,
structured deviations that can be hidden by parameter degeneracies in classical
representations.
In this context, gravitational waves are uniquely powerful because ringdown
spectra encode the geometry of the remnant compact object and can reveal
departures from Kerr behaviour.

The conceptual core is the inverse spectral analogy posed by
Kac~\cite{Kac_1966}: \emph{Can one hear the shape of a drum?}
In the general Riemannian setting this question has a negative answer:
Gordon, Webb and Wolpert~\cite{Gordon_1992} constructed pairs of non-isometric
planar domains sharing identical Laplacian spectra, proving that the spectrum
alone does not determine the geometry.
In the black-hole context, however, the situation is more constrained.
Under General Relativity, the no-hair theorem implies that the quasinormal-mode
spectrum of a Kerr remnant is \emph{locally unique}: it is determined entirely
by mass and spin, with no continuous family of isospectral non-Kerr geometries
in the perturbative regime~\cite{Berti_2009}.
Here the black hole is the drum and quasinormal modes are its spectrum;
beyond-GR effects act as isospectrality-breaking perturbations that shift the
mode frequencies away from their Kerr values.
QNIM is designed to resolve these perturbations in a quantum feature space
where they become linearly separable; the formal justification for this
separability claim is developed in Section~\ref{sec:qfm}.
Detailed waveform modelling and theory-specific phenomenology are developed in
later chapters.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{The classical resolution wall and the quantum solution}
\label{sec:resolution-wall-quantum}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

Classical gravitational-wave inference has achieved remarkable precision, yet
it faces fundamental representational limits when searching for quantum-gravity
signatures.
This section identifies the mathematical origin of those limits and explains
why a Hilbert-space embedding provides representational capabilities
unavailable to classical inference.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Epistemological framing of the proof-of-concept.}
Throughout this thesis a careful distinction is maintained between two levels
of claim.
The \emph{methodological claim}—that embedding gravitational-wave features in a
$2^n$-dimensional Hilbert space improves separability of beyond-GR
signatures—is evaluated on a noise-model simulator where hardware constraints
do not obscure the inference structure.
The \emph{implementation claim}—that the end-to-end QNIM pipeline executes on
real quantum processing units—is validated by five successful production runs
on IBM~Fez under Open Plan resource constraints.
Hardware accuracy in those runs ($6\,\%$) reflects severe QPU quota limitations
(${\sim}10$~min/month), zero on-hardware retraining, and circuit fidelity
$\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{circ}}\approx 0.45$; it does not characterise the
methodology's potential on production hardware, as detailed in
Appendix~\ref{app:sim-hw-gap}.
No metrological advantage claim is made on the basis of the IBM~Fez results.

\subsection{Classical limits and the quantum embedding}
\label{sec:classical-limits}

Classical matched filtering and Bayesian sampling remain optimal for many
parameter-estimation tasks, but they encounter a resolution wall for weak
beyond-GR signatures.
The key obstacles are degeneracy-dominated likelihood geometry and
representational spectral blindness in Euclidean feature spaces; both effects
are documented by Fisher-metric conditioning analyses~\cite{Vallisneri_2008}
and motivate a non-classical embedding.

QNIM addresses this by mapping classical features into a $2^n$-dimensional
Hilbert space and performing discrimination through quantum overlaps.
The feature map is defined by
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:feature_map}
  |\psi(\mathbf{x})\rangle = \mathcal{U}_\phi(\mathbf{x})\,|\mathbf{0}\rangle,
\end{equation}
where $\mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{R}^d$ is the classical feature vector extracted
from the gravitational waveform and $\mathcal{U}_\phi$ is a parametrised
unitary circuit whose structure is specified in
Section~\ref{sec:qfm}.
This embedding promotes higher-order correlations—invisible in the original
Euclidean space—to directly measurable structure in a $2^n$-dimensional
Hilbert space where beyond-GR classes become linearly
separable~\cite{Havlicek_2019}.
The specific ansatz (IQP-inspired ZZ-feature map) and the justification for
its choice over alternatives are presented in
Chapter~\ref{chap:methodology-qnim}.

Parameter precision is then constrained by the Quantum Cram\'{e}r--Rao Bound,
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:qcrb}
  \mathrm{Var}(\hat{\theta}) \geq \frac{1}{\mathcal{F}_Q(\theta)},
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal{F}_Q \geq \mathcal{F}_C$ holds by construction, since the
quantum Fisher information is the supremum over all physically admissible
measurements and therefore upper-bounds its classical counterpart for any
fixed parameter $\theta$~\cite{Braunstein_1994,Paris_2009}.
It is essential to note, however, that this inequality characterises the
\emph{theoretical} metrological hierarchy; a variational ansatz executed on
near-term hardware accesses only a restricted set of measurements and will in
general not saturate $\mathcal{F}_Q$.
The \emph{achievable} Fisher information $\mathcal{F}_Q^{\mathrm{eff}}$
depends on circuit expressibility, gate fidelity, and shot budget, and its
relation to $\mathcal{F}_C$ is evaluated empirically in
Chapter~\ref{chap:quantum-advantage}.
In QNIM, the bound is therefore treated as a ceiling that motivates the
quantum embedding, while practical advantage is quantified through the
expressibility--entanglement metrics of
Section~\ref{subsec:expressibility}.

\section{Research objectives}
\label{sec:objectives}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

The investigation is structured around six inter-dependent objectives, each
with a verifiable quantitative criterion.
Two components are referenced by acronym throughout: the
\emph{Synthetic Signal and Template Generator} (SSTG), which produces
post-Newtonian waveforms with injected beyond-GR anomalies
(Chapter~\ref{chap:synthetic}), and the \emph{Variational Quantum Classifier}
(VQC), which implements the quantum discrimination pipeline
(Chapter~\ref{chap:methodology-qnim}).
The objectives are ordered to reflect the natural progression of the work:
from software infrastructure through methodology and validation to real-data
application.
