% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Structure of the thesis}
\label{sec:structure}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

The thesis follows a natural arc: first establishing the theoretical and
physical landscape, then building the analytical machinery, then implementing
and validating it.
Chapter~\ref{chap:theoretical-framework} lays the foundations—the spectral
geometry of quasinormal modes, the post-Newtonian waveform model to 3.5PN
order, the twelve beyond-GR theory classes (Loop Quantum Gravity, Generalised
Uncertainty Principle, extra dimensions, Chern--Simons, etc.), and the broader
context of standard sirens and cosmological inference.
Chapter~\ref{chap:methodology-qnim} introduces the QNIM methodology in full:
the quantum feature map, the variational ansatz, the QNSPSA-EML-Feynman
optimiser, the QUBO reformulation, and the metrological framework based on
Quantum Fisher Information.
Chapter~\ref{chap:software} describes the implementation: the Domain-Driven
Design ontology, the seven-layer Clean Architecture, the Hexagonal pattern,
and the hybrid quantum-classical orchestrator that makes the abstract
methodology operational.
Chapter~\ref{chap:synthetic} covers synthetic data generation—the multi-regime
engine spanning post-Newtonian through Effective One Body, the
anomaly-injection protocols for all thirteen theory classes, and the
validation methodology against real LIGO distributions.
Chapter~\ref{chap:quantum-advantage} provides the formal analysis: QFI versus
CFI benchmarks, expressibility metrics, barren-plateau diagnostics, realistic
NISQ error mitigation, and the $5\sigma$ significance pipeline.
Chapter~\ref{chap:results} presents the experimental results: training
convergence on simulators and hardware, classification of all ninety GWTC-3
events with Bayes factors and reliability tiers, new upper limits on beyond-GR
parameters, cosmological inference from standard sirens, and computational
performance analysis.
Chapter~\ref{chap:discussion} synthesises implications, current limitations,
and scalability projections; Chapter~\ref{chap:conclusions} outlines a roadmap
for future development.
Five appendices supply supporting derivations, deployment guides, and the
complete QNIM Planck Reliability Catalogue.

% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
%   END OF CHAPTER 1
% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\chapter{Theoretical framework: spectral geometry, post-Newtonian dynamics,
         and quantum-gravity signatures}
\label{chap:theoretical-framework}

This chapter builds the physical vocabulary that the QNIM quantum classifier requires. Rather than a comprehensive review of all relativistic effects, it strategically selects the elements essential to interpreting gravitational-wave data through the lens of quantum-gravity testing. The material divides into three interconnected blocks: \emph{(1)~the physics of the signal background}, which includes the quasinormal mode spectrum that dominates the ringdown and the post-Newtonian/Effective One Body formalism that describes the inspiral and merger; \emph{(2)~the hierarchy of beyond-GR signatures}, spanning thirteen distinct theory classes from modified dispersion relations to spacetime quantisation, organised into seven physical layers (Layers~1--4 cover 
standard GR observables and are described in 
Appendix~\ref{app:bms-superad}; Layers~5--7, the 
operationally active beyond-GR regime, are detailed below); and \emph{(3)~observational connections}, particularly the use of gravitational waves as standard sirens and cosmological probes. Each of these blocks is essential: the first two establish what the quantum feature map must discriminate, and the third anchors those discriminations to real astrophysical questions. Sections on the infrared structure of gravity (BMS symmetry, gravitational memory) and on superradiance, while beautiful and physically relevant, are deferred to Appendix~\ref{app:bms-superad} because they do not directly modulate the signal classification pipeline of QNIM.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Black-hole perturbation theory and the quasinormal mode spectrum}
\label{sec:qnm}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------
The gravitational-wave signal emitted during the final stages of a binary merger encodes the geometry of the resulting black hole through its quasinormal mode spectrum. This section develops the perturbation theory framework needed to understand that spectrum, from the Schwarzschild case through the Kerr geometry, and establishes the inverse spectral problem that QNIM is designed to solve.
\subsection{The inverse spectral problem}
\label{subsec:inverse-spectral}

The mathematical problem at the heart of gravitational-wave spectroscopy
was described evocatively by Mark Kac in 1966 through the question:
``\emph{Can one hear the shape of a drum?}''.  The drum membrane, like
the perturbed spacetime exterior to a black hole, vibrates at a discrete
set of characteristic frequencies; the inverse problem asks whether the
geometry of the resonator can be uniquely reconstructed from its spectrum.

For a black hole the answer is positive under General Relativity: the
\emph{No-Hair theorem} (discussed in Section~\ref{subsec:no-hair}) guarantees
that a stationary black hole is characterised completely by its mass $M$,
angular momentum $J$, and electric charge $Q_e$.  The quasinormal mode (QNM)
spectrum depends only on these three quantities, providing a unique acoustic
fingerprint of the final state.  The QNIM methodology leverages this
uniqueness in two ways: to extract $(M_f, J_f)$ from the ringdown phase of
a detected event, and to detect spectral anomalies that would signal
deviations from the Kerr geometry predicted by GR.

To make this analogy precise and operationally useful, we first need to establish
the mathematics governing how a black hole perturbs its surrounding spacetime.
The simplest case—a non-rotating Schwarzschild black hole—already captures the
essential physics and provides the spectral equations that QNIM's feature map
is designed to invert.

\subsection{Perturbations of the Schwarzschild geometry}
\label{subsec:rw-equation}

Consider a Schwarzschild black hole with metric:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:schwarzschild}
  ds^2 = -f(r)\,dt^2 + f(r)^{-1}\,dr^2 + r^2\,d\Omega^2,
  \qquad f(r) = 1 - \frac{2M}{r}.
\end{equation}
A general perturbation $h_{\mu\nu}$ can be decomposed into scalar spherical
harmonics $Y_{lm}(\theta,\phi)$ and categorised by parity.  Axial (odd-parity)
perturbations are governed by the \emph{ReggeWheeler equation}
\cite{Regge_1957}:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:regge_wheeler}
  \left[ \frac{d^2}{dr^{*2}} + \omega^2 - V_s(r) \right] \Psi_{lm} = 0,
\end{equation}
where the tortoise coordinate $r^*$ maps the exterior region $r \in (2M,\infty)$
to the full real line via:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:tortoise}
  \frac{dr^*}{dr} = f(r)^{-1}
  \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
  r^* = r + 2M\ln\!\left(\frac{r}{2M}-1\right),
\end{equation}
and the ReggeWheeler potential for spin-$s$ fields is:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:rw_potential}
  V_s(r) = f(r)\left[\frac{l(l+1)}{r^2} - \frac{2M(1-s^2)}{r^3}\right].
\end{equation}
For gravitational perturbations $s=2$; for electromagnetic $s=1$; for scalar
$s=0$.  Polar (even-parity) gravitational perturbations are governed by the
\emph{Zerilli equation}, which shares the same spectrum as the ReggeWheeler
equation by isospectrality  a non-trivial identity that is broken in most
modified-gravity theories and therefore serves as a consistency test within
the QNIM framework.

\subsubsection{Quasinormal mode boundary conditions}

The QNMs are selected by the physical requirement that no radiation arrives
from outside the system.  The boundary conditions in the tortoise coordinate
are:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:qnm_bc}
  \Psi_{lm} \sim
  \begin{cases}
    e^{-i\omega r^*} & r^* \to -\infty \quad (\text{ingoing at horizon}),\\
    e^{+i\omega r^*} & r^* \to +\infty \quad (\text{outgoing at infinity}).
  \end{cases}
\end{equation}
These conditions admit solutions only for a discrete set of complex
frequencies $\omega_{nls} = \omega_R + i\omega_I$, where $\omega_R$ is the
oscillation frequency and $\omega_I < 0$ encodes the exponential damping
rate.  The quality factor $Q = \omega_R / (2|\omega_I|)$ measures the number
of oscillation cycles before the mode damps by a factor $1/e$.

These boundary conditions select a discrete, countably infinite family of
complex frequencies. Two complementary techniques allow their practical
computation with the accuracy required for QNIM's injection and validation
protocols.

\subsubsection{WKB approximation and Leaver's continued-fraction method}

The WKB approximation and Berti--Cardoso--Will fitting 
coefficients for computing QNM frequencies are collected in 
Appendix~\ref{app:math-tools} (Table~\ref{tab:bcw-coeffs}).
The Schwarzschild analysis, however, is incomplete for the astrophysically
relevant case: almost all binary black hole mergers produce a spinning remnant.
Rotation couples the axial and polar perturbation sectors and requires a more
powerful equation of motion.

\subsection{The Teukolsky equation for Kerr black holes}
\label{subsec:teukolsky}

The Kerr metric in Boyer--Lindquist form is given in 
Appendix~\ref{app:math-tools}; here we proceed directly 
to the Teukolsky radial equation for spin-$s$ perturbations:

\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:teukolsky}
  \Delta^{-s}\frac{d}{dr}\!\left(\Delta^{s+1}\frac{dR}{dr}\right)
  + \left[\frac{K^2 - 2is(r-M)K}{\Delta}
    + 4is\omega r - \lambda\right]R = 0,
\end{equation}
with $K = (r^2+a^2)\omega - am$ and angular eigenvalue $\lambda$. The coupled angular sector is solved with spin-weighted spheroidal harmonics to recover the QNM spectrum.

\subsection{The No-Hair theorem and its quantum-gravity challenge}
\label{subsec:no-hair}

The No-Hair theorem, formalised by Carter \cite{Carter_1971}, states that stationary electrovacuum black holes are fully specified by $(M,J,Q_e)$, with multipoles fixed by the Hansen relations:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:hansen}
  M_l + iS_l = M\,(ia)^l,
\end{equation}
where $M_l$ and $S_l$ are mass and current moments. The quadrupole-deviation test used by QNIM is defined by 
Eq.~\eqref{eq:nohair_deviation} (introduced in 
Section~\ref{subsec:layer6} below).
In explicit form, the leading correction is $\delta\omega_R/\omega_R \approx -0.15\,\delta_Q\,\chi_f^2 + \mathcal{O}(\delta_Q^2)$.
The information-geometric development and Fisher-metric 
formalism are treated rigorously in 
Chapter~\ref{chap:methodology-qnim}.
With the perturbative spectrum and Kerr consistency conditions established, we now summarise the inspiral-merger dynamics that generate the waveform manifold used in QNIM.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Binary dynamics: post-Newtonian theory and EOB resummation}
\label{sec:pn-eob}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------
Before the merger, the binary evolves through thousands of orbital cycles whose dynamics are captured by the post-Newtonian expansion. This section presents the waveform model that QNIM uses as a foundation for synthetic signal generation and parameter estimation, from the inspiral phase governed by the post-Newtonian series through the Effective One Body resummation that extends it to the strong-field regime.
\subsection{The post-Newtonian expansion}
\label{subsec:pn}

Away from the merger, the dynamics of a compact binary with component masses
$m_1$, $m_2$, total mass $M = m_1 + m_2$, chirp mass
$\mathcal{M}_c = (m_1 m_2)^{3/5}/M^{1/5}$, and symmetric mass ratio
$\eta = m_1 m_2/M^2$ are governed by the post-Newtonian expansion
\cite{Blanchet_2014}.  The characteristic expansion parameter is
$v/c = (G M\omega / c^3)^{1/3}$, where $\omega$ is the orbital angular
frequency.

In the frequency domain, the gravitational-wave phase under the
Stationary Phase Approximation (SPA) takes the form:
\begin{align}
  \label{eq:spa_phase}
  \Psi_\mathrm{SPA}(f) &= \frac{3}{128\eta}\,v^{-5}\Bigg[
    1
    + \frac{20}{9}\!\left(\frac{743}{336}+\frac{11\eta}{4}\right)v^2
    + \left(-16\pi + \beta_\mathrm{SO}\right)v^3
    \nonumber\\
    &\quad
    + \left(\frac{15293365}{508032}+\frac{27145\eta}{504}
      +\frac{3085\eta^2}{72} - 10\sigma_\mathrm{SS}\right)v^4
    \nonumber\\
    &\quad
    + \pi\!\left(\frac{38645}{756}-\frac{65\eta}{9}\right)
      \left(1 + 3\ln v\right)v^5
    \nonumber\\
    &\quad
    + \left(\frac{11583231236531}{4694215680}
      - \frac{640\pi^2}{3}
      - \frac{6848\gamma_E}{21} + \eta\,\Xi_6\right)v^6
    \nonumber\\
    &\quad
    + \pi\!\left(\frac{77096675}{254016}+\frac{378515\eta}{1512}
      -\frac{74045\eta^2}{756}\right)v^7
    - \frac{3\tilde{\Lambda}}{128\eta}\,v^{10}
  \Bigg],
\end{align}
where $v = (\pi G M f / c^3)^{1/3}$, $\beta_\mathrm{SO}$ captures spin-orbit
coupling, $\sigma_\mathrm{SS}$ encodes spin-spin interactions,
$\gamma_E \approx 0.5772$ is the EulerMascheroni constant,$\tilde{\Lambda}$ is the binary tidal deformability and $\Xi_6 = \frac{15293365}{508032} - \frac{640\pi^2}{3} 
- \frac{6848\gamma_E}{21}$ collects the 3PN 
non-spinning terms \cite{Blanchet_2014}.  The ``tail'' logarithm
at 2.5PN (the $v^5\ln v$ term) arises from the back-scattering of gravitational
waves off the Coulomb-like gravitational potential of the source, representing
a non-linear memory effect of the radiation.

The phase~\eqref{eq:spa_phase} is valid up to the Innermost Stable Circular
Orbit (ISCO) frequency:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:isco}
  f_\mathrm{ISCO} = \frac{c^3}{6\sqrt{6}\,\pi G M},
\end{equation}
which for a $60\,M_\odot$ system lies at approximately $44$\,Hz,
well within the Advanced LIGO sensitive band.

The spin-orbit coefficient $\beta_\mathrm{SO}$ and binary tidal 
deformability $\tilde{\Lambda}$ are derived in 
Appendix~\ref{app:math-tools} 
(Eqs.~\ref{eq:beta_so}--\ref{eq:tidal_lambda}); their operative 
forms enter the phase~\eqref{eq:spa_phase} at 1.5PN and 5PN order 
respectively.

\subsection{Effective One Body resummation}
\label{subsec:eob}

As the binary approaches merger, the PN series diverges.  The Effective One
Body (EOB) formalism \cite{Buonanno_1999} provides a systematic resummation
by mapping the two-body dynamics onto the motion of an effective test particle
with mass $\mu = m_1 m_2/M$ in a deformed Schwarzschild-like metric:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:eob_metric}
  ds^2_\mathrm{eff} = -A(r)\,dt^2
    + A(r)^{-1}D(r)\,dr^2 + r^2\,d\Omega^2,
\end{equation}
where the potentials $A(r)$ and $D(r)$ incorporate PN corrections resummed
via Pad\'e approximants (full construction in 
Appendix~\ref{app:math-tools}, Eq.~\ref{eq:pade_A}), 
enforcing an effective horizon/ISCO structure for the two-body problem.

The EOB Hamiltonian, calibrated to numerical-relativity waveforms, provides
the most accurate inspiral-merger-ringdown (IMR) waveform models currently
available (\textsc{SEOBNRv5}, \textsc{IMRPhenomXPHM}) and is used as the
reference model against which QNIM's beyond-GR injections are validated.

Having now mapped the complete gravitational-wave signal—from inspiral through merger to ringdown—under General Relativity, we turn to the central question: \emph{where} and \emph{how} do quantum-gravity corrections enter this picture? The following section catalogs the thirteen beyond-GR theory classes that QNIM's quantum classifier must discriminate, organised into a hierarchy of physical scales and degrees of speculative boldness.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{The hierarchy of quantum-gravity signatures}
\label{sec:qg-hierarchy}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

Not all quantum-gravity signatures are created equal. Some arise from
modifications to graviton propagation that accumulate over cosmological distances
and are accessible at moderate signal-to-noise ratio; others require resolving
the near-horizon geometry and demand exceptional event quality. The following
hierarchy organises the thirteen beyond-GR theory classes addressed by QNIM into
three observational layers, ordered by the minimum network SNR at which each
becomes detectable.
The QNIM framework operationally focuses on Layers~5--7 as the observable beyond-GR regime. Table~\ref{tab:qg-layers} summarises the hierarchy used for injections.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\caption[QNIM physical-layer hierarchy]{The QNIM physical-layer hierarchy for beyond-GR signatures in
         gravitational-wave data.}
\label{tab:qg-layers}
\begin{tabular}{@{}clll@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Layer} & \textbf{Name} & \textbf{Observable signature} &
\textbf{Min.\ SNR} \\
\midrule
5 & Beyond-GR propagation & Modified dispersion, dipolar radiation & 12 \\
6 & Horizon quantum topology & Late-time echoes, QNM deviations & 25 \\
7 & Deep quantum spacetime & Phase diffusion, spectral broadening & 40 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsection{Layer 5: beyond-GR propagation effects}
\label{subsec:layer5}

Layer~5 captures propagation-level departures that accumulate over distance and are therefore accessible at moderate SNR. In QNIM, these signatures are implemented as phase/amplitude perturbations in inspiral-dominated signals.

\subsubsection{Brans-Dicke scalar-tensor gravity}

The simplest extension of GR couples a massless scalar field $\phi$ to
the Ricci scalar through the BransDicke action:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:bd_action}
  S_\mathrm{BD} = \frac{1}{16\pi}\int d^4x\sqrt{-g}
    \left[\phi R - \frac{\omega_\mathrm{BD}}{\phi}(\nabla\phi)^2\right]
    + S_m[g_{\mu\nu}, \Psi_m],
\end{equation}
where $\omega_\mathrm{BD}$ is the coupling constant.  In this theory,
compact objects acquire a ``scalar charge'' $s_i = -\partial\ln m_i/
\partial\ln G_\mathrm{eff}$ that depends on their internal compactness.
A binary with unequal scalar charges emits dipolar radiation at $-1$PN order, yielding \cite{Damour_1993}:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:bd_phase}
  \delta\Psi_\mathrm{BD}(f) =
    -\frac{5\Delta s^2}{84(\omega_\mathrm{BD}+2)\eta}
    \cdot (\pi G\mathcal{M}_c f/c^3)^{-7/3},
\end{equation}
where $\Delta s = s_1 - s_2$.

\subsubsection{Massive graviton and modified dispersion}

If the graviton carries a non-zero rest mass $m_g$, the dispersion relation
becomes $E^2 = p^2c^2 + m_g^2c^4$, leading to a frequency-dependent phase
velocity.  The resulting phase shift in the gravitational waveform is
\cite{Will_1998}:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:mg_phase}
  \delta\Psi_{m_g}(f) = \frac{\pi^2 d_L m_g^2 c^3}{h_\mathrm{Pl} f(1+z)},
\end{equation}
where $d_L$ is the luminosity distance and $z$ is the redshift.  Current
LIGO/Virgo bounds constrain $m_g < 1.27\times10^{-23}\,\mathrm{eV}/c^2$
\cite{Abbott_2021_GWTC3_TGR}; QNIM uses this bound as the Layer~5 calibration baseline.

\subsubsection{Extra dimensions and the Randall--Sundrum model}

In the RandallSundrum braneworld model \cite{Randall_1999}, our observable
universe is a four-dimensional brane embedded in a five-dimensional anti-de
Sitter bulk.  Gravitational waves can leak into the bulk on scales smaller
than the crossover radius $R_\mathrm{cross}$.  For sources at
$d_L > R_\mathrm{cross}$, the strain amplitude damps as:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:rs_correction}
  h \propto d_L^{-(1+n_\mathrm{extra}/2)},
\end{equation}
where $n_\mathrm{extra}$ is the number of large extra dimensions.  The
ADD variant with $n_\mathrm{extra}=2$ and $R_\mathrm{cross}\sim\mathcal{O}(10\,\mathrm{Mpc})$ is the reference case for standard-siren anomalies.

\subsubsection{Lorentz invariance violation and birefringence}

More general modifications to the graviton dispersion relation take the form:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:liv_dispersion}
  E^2 = p^2c^2\left[1 + A_\alpha\!\left(\frac{pc}{E_\mathrm{QG}}\right)^{\alpha-2}
    \right] + m_g^2c^4,
\end{equation}
where $E_\mathrm{QG}$ is the quantum-gravity energy scale and $\alpha$ is
the power-law index.  For $\alpha = 3$ (linear LIV), $E_\mathrm{QG}$ is
already constrained to exceed $10^{18}$\,GeV by GW170817. QNIM scans $\alpha\in[2,4]$ and includes parity-violating birefringence in the same layer.

\subsection{Layer 6: horizon quantum topology}
\label{subsec:layer6}

Layer~6 targets near-horizon structure through delayed ringdown components and quadrupole consistency tests. The objective is to separate echo-like signatures from genuine Kerr-deviation effects under realistic SNR.

\subsubsection{The Fuzzball paradigm}

In string-theoretic approaches to black holes, the event horizon is
dissolved into a ``Fuzzball'' microstate geometry \cite{Mathur_2005}: a
horizonless configuration in which the mass is supported by string-scale
structure rather than a singularity.  The Fuzzball acts as a partially
reflecting wall.  Radiation that would classically be absorbed by the horizon
is instead reflected, reprocessed, and re-emitted with a time delay:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:fuzzball_delay}
  \Delta t_\mathrm{echo} = 2\int_{r_s}^{r_+}
    \frac{dr}{c\,f(r)} \approx \frac{2r_+}{c}\ln\!\left(\frac{r_+}{r_s}\right),
\end{equation}
where $r_s$ is the Fuzzball surface radius (slightly outside $r_+$).  The
resulting signal is modeled as a discrete echo train:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:echo_train}
  h_\mathrm{ringdown}(t) = h_\mathrm{QNM}(t)
    + \sum_{n=1}^{N} \mathcal{R}^n \cdot h_\mathrm{QNM}(t - n\Delta t),
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal{R}$ is the complex reflectivity ($|\mathcal{R}|\in[0,1]$).

\subsubsection{LQG area quantisation and the Barbero--Immirzi parameter}

In Loop Quantum Gravity \cite{Ashtekar_2004}, the area of the event horizon
is quantised in units of the Planck area:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:lqg_area}
  A_n = 8\pi\gamma\,l_P^2\sum_k\sqrt{j_k(j_k+1)},
\end{equation}
where $\gamma$ is the BarberoImmirzi parameter and $j_k$ are spin-network
labels.  For the physical value $\gamma_\mathrm{Meissner} = \ln 2/(\pi\sqrt{3})$
\cite{Meissner_2004}, the minimum area quantum is $A_\mathrm{min} \approx
4\ln2\,l_P^2$. The induced line-structure correction is represented through the delay model:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:lqg_correction}
  \Delta t_\mathrm{LQG} =
    \frac{2r_+(a)}{c}\ln\!\left(\frac{M_f}{m_P\sqrt{\gamma}}\right),
\end{equation}
which is separable from Fuzzball scaling through its $(\gamma,M_f)$ dependence.

\subsubsection{Quadrupole deviations and the generalised No-Hair test}

A model-independent test of the Kerr geometry is provided by parameterising
the quadrupole moment as $Q = -J^2(1+\delta_Q)/M$, where $\delta_Q$ is a
free parameter.  A non-zero $\delta_Q$ modifies the QNM frequencies at
leading order through the relation:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:nohair_deviation}
  \frac{\delta\omega_R}{\omega_R} \approx -0.15\,\delta_Q\,\chi_f^2
    + \mathcal{O}(\delta_Q^2).
\end{equation}
QNIM's Layer~6 injection simultaneously varies $\delta_Q$, $|\mathcal{R}|$,
and $\Delta t$ as independent parameters.

\subsection{Layer 7: deep quantum spacetime}
\label{subsec:layer7}

Layer~7 collects speculative high-curvature effects as effective stochastic or vacuum corrections. These signatures are weak, but they define the upper boundary of the QNIM hypothesis space.

\subsubsection{Hawking radiation and the information paradox}

The thermal radiation predicted by Hawking \cite{Hawking_1975} to be emitted
by black holes at temperature $T_H = \hbar c^3/(8\pi G M k_B)$ has never
been directly observed, as the temperature is $\sim 6\times10^{-8}\,(M_\odot/M)$
K for stellar-mass objects. The effective vacuum contribution is modeled with zeta-function regularisation \cite{Hawking_1975}:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:casimir_zeta}
  E_0^\mathrm{reg} = \frac{\hbar\omega_c}{2}\,\zeta(-1)
    = -\frac{\hbar\omega_c}{24},
\end{equation}
where $\zeta(-1)=-1/12$.

\subsubsection{Spacetime foam and Ornstein--Uhlenbeck phase diffusion}

Wheeler's spacetime foam hypothesis posits that at the Planck length
$l_P = \sqrt{\hbar G/c^3} \approx 1.6\times10^{-35}$\,m, the metric
undergoes random topological fluctuations analogous to quantum foam.
At the macroscopic scale accessible to LIGO, these fluctuations manifest
as a stochastic phase noise $\delta\varphi(t)$ in the gravitational-wave
signal.  QNIM models this noise as an OrnsteinUhlenbeck process:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:ou_process}
  d(\delta\varphi_t) = -\gamma_\mathrm{foam}\,\delta\varphi_t\,dt
    + \sqrt{2\gamma_\mathrm{foam}\,T_\mathrm{foam}}\,dW_t,
\end{equation}
where $\gamma_\mathrm{foam}$ is the relaxation rate, $T_\mathrm{foam}$ an effective amplitude scale, and $W_t$ a Wiener process; the corresponding PSD is
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:ou_spectrum}
  S_\mathrm{foam}(f) =
    \frac{4\gamma_\mathrm{foam}\,T_\mathrm{foam}}
         {\gamma_\mathrm{foam}^2 + (2\pi f)^2},
\end{equation}
with knee frequency $f_\mathrm{knee}=\gamma_\mathrm{foam}/(2\pi)$.

These seven layers constitute the \emph{complete signal hierarchy} that QNIM's quantum classifier must discriminate: from the standard-siren and post-Newtonian regime 
(Layers~1--4, deferred to Appendix~\ref{app:bms-superad}) 
through the quantum-topology scenarios of Layer~6, to the deepest quantum-foam corrections of Layer~7. With this hierarchy in place, we now turn to the observational tools that connect these signatures to real astrophysical data. The infrared structure of gravity—the BMS symmetry group, the soft graviton theorem, and gravitational memory—while intimately related to the quantum structure of spacetime, forms a separate theoretical layer deferred to Appendix~\ref{app:bms-superad}. The following sections focus on the observables that are \emph{directly encoded in the strain time series} accessible to QNIM's feature map.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Standard sirens and the Hubble tension}
\label{sec:standard-sirens}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------
Gravitational waves from compact binaries provide a self-calibrating distance measurement that is independent of the cosmic distance ladder. This section describes how that measurement is made, its current contribution to the Hubble constant debate, and how the QNIM quantum metrological gain translates into a concrete improvement in cosmological inference.
\subsection{The luminosity-distance measurement}
\label{subsec:siren-distance}

As first recognised by Schutz \cite{Schutz_1986}, the gravitational-wave
signal of a compact binary is a ``self-calibrating'' distance indicator:
the chirp mass extracted from the phasing, combined with the observed
amplitude, determines the luminosity distance $d_L$ without any external
calibration (i.e., without the cosmic distance ladder):
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:siren_amplitude}
  h \propto \frac{G\mathcal{M}_c}{c^2 d_L}
    \left(\frac{G\mathcal{M}_c \pi f}{c^3}\right)^{2/3}.
\end{equation}
By combining $d_L$ with a redshift $z$ obtained from an electromagnetic
counterpart (or from statistical galaxy catalogues), the Hubble constant
follows directly:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:siren_h0}
  H_0 \approx \frac{c\,z}{d_L}
    \quad (z \ll 1).
\end{equation}
At higher redshift, a full cosmological model (e.g., flat $\Lambda$CDM) is
required to relate $d_L$ to $z$ and $H_0$.

\subsection{Current tension and GW contribution}
\label{subsec:hubble-tension}

Modern cosmology faces a tension between the ``early-universe'' estimate
$H_0 = 67.4 \pm 0.5$\,km\,s$^{-1}$\,Mpc$^{-1}$ from Planck CMB
measurements \cite{Planck_2020} and late-universe distance-ladder values,
whose severity depends strongly on the calibration method.  The SH0ES
programme, incorporating Small Magellanic Cloud Cepheids as a fourth
geometric anchor observed with HST, yields
$H_0 = 73.17 \pm 0.86$\,km\,s$^{-1}$\,Mpc$^{-1}$ \cite{Breuval_2024},
updating the earlier \cite{Riess_2022} value and implying a
$5.8\sigma$ tension with Planck.  By contrast, the Chicago--Carnegie
Hubble Programme (CCHP), calibrating via JWST observations of the tip
of the red giant branch (TRGB), obtains
$H_0 = 70.39 \pm 1.22\,(\mathrm{stat}) \pm 1.33\,(\mathrm{sys})$\,km\,s$^{-1}$\,Mpc$^{-1}$
\cite{Freedman_2024}; using JWST data alone the CCHP TRGB anchor gives
$H_0 = 68.81 \pm 2.21$\,km\,s$^{-1}$\,Mpc$^{-1}$, consistent with the CMB.
Considering the full ensemble of independent late-universe probes, the
tension currently stands at approximately $3.7$--$5.8\sigma$ depending
on the distance calibration adopted.  Whether this reflects new physics
(early dark energy, modified gravity, interacting dark matter) or
unresolved systematics in one or more rungs of the distance ladder remains
one of the most pressing open questions in cosmology.

The GW170817 standard-siren measurement yielded
$H_0 = 70.0^{+12.0}_{-8.0}$\,km\,s$^{-1}$\,Mpc$^{-1}$ \cite{Abbott_2017c},
consistent with both extremes but with too large an uncertainty to adjudicate.
Reaching a sub-percent measurement of $H_0$ via GWs requires
$\mathcal{O}(10^3)$ events with electromagnetic counterparts or large
galaxy catalogue datasets.  The QNIM pipeline reduces the systematic floor in luminosity-distance
estimation through the quantum metrological gain $G_{d_L} = 1.09$
(Table~\ref{tab:qmg-parameters}), which corresponds to a
$1 - 1/\sqrt{G_{d_L}} \approx 4.3\,\%$ reduction in $\sigma_{d_L}$
relative to classical matched filtering at the same SNR\@. This modest
but systematic improvement propagates directly into the $H_0$ posterior:
for a fixed set of $N$ standard-siren events, it is equivalent to
adding approximately $N(G_{d_L} - 1) \approx 0.09N$ additional
events to the catalogue. For the 13-event GWTC-3 subset used in
Section~\ref{sec:cosmological-results}, this corresponds to roughly
one additional effective event. The non-linear memory effect
\cite{Christodoulou_1991} contributes to this gain by encoding
an additional observable in the late-time ringdown tail that is
captured by the quantum feature map but not by the standard
matched-filter statistic; a dedicated analysis of the memory
contribution is reserved for future work.

\subsection{Dark energy and the speed of gravitational waves}
\label{subsec:dark-energy}

The detection of GW170817 and its associated gamma-ray burst GRB~170817A
with a time delay of only $1.74 \pm 0.05$\,s over $40$\,Mpc constrained
the gravitational-wave propagation speed to \cite{Abbott_2017b}:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:speed_constraint}
  \left|\frac{c_\mathrm{GW}}{c} - 1\right| < 5\times10^{-16}.
\end{equation}
This single measurement ruled out a large class of scalar-tensor and
vector-tensor dark energy models  in particular, most k-essence models
\cite{Armendariz_2001} in which the graviton propagation speed deviates
from $c$.  In the QNIM framework, this constraint is implemented as a hard
prior on the Layer~5 parameter space, drastically reducing the volume of
viable modified-gravity theories.

With this landscape of standard sirens and cosmological observables in place, the present chapter's task is complete: we have established the full physical vocabulary needed to interpret QNIM's results. The analysis of superradiance and axion clouds, while relevant to future searches for ultralight bosons through spin-gap constraints, do not directly enter the thirteen-class classification pipeline presented in Chapter~\ref{chap:results}. These topics are consequently deferred to Appendix~\ref{app:bms-superad}, together with the infrared structure of gravity and the complete treatment of zeta-function regularisation.

The following section provides a consolidated reference table of all parameters and their roles, serving as the bridge from theoretical physics to computational methodology.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Summary of theoretical inputs to QNIM}
\label{sec:theory-summary}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

Table~\ref{tab:theory-summary} provides a consolidated reference of all
physical parameters, their theoretical origin, and their role in the QNIM
classification pipeline.  This table will be referenced throughout the
methodology and results chapters.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\small
\caption[Summary of physical parameters in QNIM]{Summary of physical parameters entering the QNIM framework across
         all modelled theories.}
\label{tab:theory-summary}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lllc@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Theory} & \textbf{Observable effect} &
\textbf{Layer} \\
\midrule
$\omega_{BD}$ & BransDicke & Dipolar phase at $-1$PN & 5 \\
$m_g$         & Massive graviton & Phase dispersion, group delay & 5 \\
$n_\mathrm{extra}$ & RandallSundrum ADD & $d_L$ anomaly & 5 \\
$A_\alpha, \alpha$ & LIV modified dispersion & Arrival-time shifts & 5 \\
$\theta_\mathrm{CS}$ & ChernSimons & Birefringence $h_+ \leftrightarrow h_\times$ & 5 \\
$\delta Q$ & No-Hair violation & QNM frequency deviation & 6 \\
$|\mathcal{R}|, \Delta t$ & Fuzzball echoes & Echo train amplitude & 6 \\
$\gamma_\mathrm{BI}$ & LQG area quanta & QNM spectral broadening & 6 \\
$T_\mathrm{foam}, \gamma_\mathrm{foam}$ & Spacetime foam & Phase diffusion PSD & 7 \\
$m_b, \alpha_\mathrm{SR}$ & Axion cloud & Monochromatic CW signal & 5 \\
$c_\mathrm{GW}/c$ & Modified propagation & Multi-messenger time delay & 5 \\
$\tilde{\Lambda}$ & Tidal EOS & 5PN phase correction & 4 \\
$\Delta h_\mathrm{mem}$ & BMS memory & DC offset in ringdown tail & 6 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The thirteen theory classes represented in this table span the full range of
physical complexity probed by the QNIM methodology, from well-constrained
perturbative corrections (massive graviton, BransDicke) to highly
speculative deep-quantum signatures (spacetime foam, LQG echoes).  A
central result of Chapter~\ref{chap:results} is that the quantum feature
map separates all thirteen classes with a pairwise KL-divergence exceeding
$0.3$\,nats, meeting Objective~O2 stated in
Section~\ref{sec:objectives}.

% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
%   END OF CHAPTER 2 
%  Chapter 3 will be appended in the next session.
% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
% CHAPTER 3  THE QNIM METHODOLOGY
% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\chapter{The QNIM analytical methodology}
\label{chap:methodology-qnim}

This chapter describes the analytical core of QNIM. We begin with raw gravitational-wave
strain data—already conditioned and whitened—and project it into a $2^{12}$-dimensional
quantum Hilbert space where beyond-GR signatures become linearly separable.
The seven-step methodology is: \emph{(1)~Classical encoding} (Section~\ref{sec:encoding})
converts strain into 12 classical feature vectors via STFT and PCA; 
\emph{(2)~quantum feature map} (Section~\ref{sec:qfm}) lifts these into quantum 
amplitudes inaccessible to classical representations; \emph{(3)~variational ansatz} 
(Section~\ref{sec:vqc}) applies a trainable quantum circuit to entangle and rotate 
the state; \emph{(4)~Bayesian model ranking} (Section~\ref{sec:bayes-factor-formalism}) 
compares the thirteen beyond-GR theories against General Relativity using Bayes factors; 
\emph{(5)~QUBO parameter estimation} (Section~\ref{sec:qubo}) reformulates parameter 
refinement as a binary optimisation problem; \emph{(6)~stochastic natural-gradient optimisation} 
(Section~\ref{sec:optimizer}) tunes the VQC using 
quantum natural gradients with zero-noise extrapolation; and \emph{(7)~end-to-end pipeline} (Section~\ref{sec:pipeline}) 
orchestrates all stages. The chapter justifies why quantum computing provides advantage 
at each step and how each component integrates into a unified system.

Throughout, we use the following notation conventions:
\begin{itemize}
  \item $n$: number of physical qubits.
  \item $d$: number of classical input features (after PCA).
  \item $N_\theta$: number of variational parameters.
  \item $\boldsymbol{\theta} \in \mathbb{R}^p$: vector of trainable circuit
        parameters.
  \item $\mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{R}^d$: classical feature vector for a
        single gravitational-wave event.
  \item $|\psi(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\theta})\rangle$: parameterised
        quantum state on $n$ qubits.
\end{itemize}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{From gravitational-wave strain to classical feature vectors}
\label{sec:encoding}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

Before any quantum operation, the raw detector strain must be converted
into a compact, noise-whitened feature vector of fixed dimension $d$.
This pre-processing pipeline is entirely classical and is implemented as
a set of Value Objects within the QNIM Domain model
(Section~\ref{app:sec:domain-model}).

The preprocessing pipeline is entirely classical and consists of four sequential
operations that transform raw detector output into the compact, noise-whitened
vector that the quantum circuit will encode.

\subsection{Data acquisition and conditioning}
\label{subsec:conditioning}

The input to the pipeline is the calibrated, frequency-domain strain
$\tilde{s}(f)$ downloaded from the Gravitational-Wave Open Science Center
(GWOSC).  The conditioning steps are:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Noise power estimation.}  The one-sided power spectral
        density $S_n(f)$ is estimated using a Welch periodogram on an
        off-source stretch of 256\,s surrounding the event.

  \item \textbf{Whitening and bandpassing.}  The strain is whitened
        by dividing by $\sqrt{S_n(f)}$ and bandpassed to the sensitive
        frequency range $[f_\mathrm{low}, f_\mathrm{high}]$.  For the
        QNIM training dataset, $f_\mathrm{low} = 20$\,Hz and
        $f_\mathrm{high} = 1024$\,Hz.

  \item \textbf{Time-frequency representation.}  The whitened strain is
        mapped to a time-frequency spectrogram using a short-time Fourier
        transform (STFT) with a Tukey window of length 0.5\,s and
        50\,\% overlap.

  \item \textbf{Matched-filter score vector.}  For theory classification,
        the spectrogram is supplemented by the matched-filter log-likelihood
        $\ln\mathcal{L}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_\mathrm{MAP})$ and its
        first-order phase-space gradient, evaluated at the Maximum A
        Posteriori (MAP) estimate returned by a fast Bayesian sampler.
\end{enumerate}

The conditioned feature matrix typically contains thousands of frequency-domain samples.
Before any quantum encoding, this dimensionality must be reduced to match the number
of available qubits while preserving the signal variance that distinguishes the
thirteen theory classes.

\subsection{Dimensionality reduction via PCA}
\label{subsec:pca}

The concatenated feature matrix (spectrogram plus matched-filter
statistics) typically has $\mathcal{O}(10^3)$ dimensions.  QNIM applies
Principal Component Analysis (PCA) to reduce this to $d = 12$ principal
components, retaining $\geq 95\,\%$ of the total explained variance
across the training dataset.  The choice $d = 12$ matches the number of
qubits $n = 12$, enabling a one-to-one assignment of principal components
to qubit rotation angles in the encoding layer.

The PCA basis is fitted once on the training set and applied without
retraining to all subsequent events, ensuring that the quantum feature
map operates in a fixed, physically meaningful subspace of signal
variation. The 12 components retain 95.2\% variance (Table~\ref{tab:pca-variance-app}).

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{The quantum feature map}
\label{sec:qfm}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

The quantum feature map is the foundational building block of the QNIM
methodology.  It converts a classical feature vector $\mathbf{x} \in
\mathbb{R}^{d}$ into a quantum state in the $2^n$-dimensional Hilbert space
$\mathcal{H} = (\mathbb{C}^2)^{\otimes n}$, encoding the information in
quantum amplitudes that are inaccessible to any classical representation
of the same dimension.

\subsection{Formal definition}
\label{subsec:qfm-formal}

The feature map is a parameterised unitary operator:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:feature_unitary}
  \mathcal{U}_\phi(\mathbf{x}) =
    \prod_{k=1}^{r_\phi}
    \left[ {H}^{\otimes n} \cdot
    \exp\!\left(-i\sum_{S \subseteq [n]}\phi_S(\mathbf{x})\,\bigotimes_{j\in S}Z_j
    \right) \right],
\end{equation}
where $r_\phi$ is the number of repetitions, $Z_j$ is the Pauli-$Z$
operator on qubit $j$, $[n] = \{1,\ldots,n\}$, and the data-dependent
angles are:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:phi_angles}
  \phi_S(\mathbf{x}) =
  \begin{cases}
    x_j & |S| = 1, \\
    \prod_{i \in S}(\pi - x_i) & |S| > 1.
  \end{cases}
\end{equation}
\noindent\textbf{Normalisation convention:} All input features $x_i$ must be
normalised to the interval $[0, \pi]$ before being passed to the feature map.
This ensures that the angles $(\pi - x_i)$ are real-valued and bounded in
$[0, \pi]$. In the QNIM pipeline, raw PCA-projected features (which are
unbounded) are rescaled via min-max normalisation to $[0,\pi]$ as part of
the preprocessing layer. This is performed in the infrastructure layer
(see Chapter~\ref{chap:software} for the full software architecture).

The Hadamard layer $\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}$ prepares a uniform
superposition, while the entangling diagonal unitary creates non-linear
correlations between features through the product terms $\prod_i(\pi - x_i)$.
This is the \emph{ZZFeatureMap} introduced by Havlcek et al.
\cite{Havlicek_2019}, which provides a provable separation from classical
kernel methods.

The ZZFeatureMap in its standard form already provides provable separation from
classical kernels, but the QNIM application demands additional spectral resolution
in the high-frequency oscillation content of gravitational waveforms. A Chebyshev
pre-processing layer achieves this without adding qubits.

\subsection{Chebyshev enhancement}
\label{subsec:chebyshev}

For the QNIM application, the standard ZZFeatureMap is augmented with a
Chebyshev encoding layer to improve the resolution of high-frequency
oscillations in the waveform spectrum.  Each feature $x_j$ is first
transformed via the Chebyshev-$T$ polynomial of degree $m$:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:chebyshev}
  \tilde{x}_j^{(m)} = \arccos\!\left(T_m(2x_j - 1)\right),
  \qquad T_m(\xi) = \cos(m\arccos\xi),
\end{equation}
producing $m$ harmonically related copies of each feature that collectively
resolve sub-features at $m$ times the native resolution.  In the QNIM
baseline configuration, $m = 2$ is used, effectively doubling the spectral
resolution of the quantum embedding without increasing the qubit count.

The feature map encodes each event into a quantum state. The natural similarity
measure between two such states—and the mathematical object that determines the
classifier's discriminative power—is the quantum kernel.
In the QNIM pipeline, the Chebyshev-transformed features 
$\tilde{x}_j^{(2)}$ \emph{replace} the raw PCA components 
$x_j$ as inputs to the ZZFeatureMap angles 
(Eq.~\ref{eq:phi_angles}), so that the data-dependent 
angles become $\phi_{\{j\}}(\mathbf{x}) = \tilde{x}_j^{(2)}$ 
for $|S|=1$.
\subsection{Quantum kernel and reproducing Hilbert space}
\label{subsec:quantum_kernel}

The inner product between two encoded states defines the quantum kernel:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:qk_inner}
  k_Q(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}') =
    \left|\langle\mathbf{0}|\,
    \mathcal{U}_\phi^\dagger(\mathbf{x}')\,
    \mathcal{U}_\phi(\mathbf{x})\,
    |\mathbf{0}\rangle\right|^2.
\end{equation}
This kernel is positive semi-definite and defines a unique Reproducing
Kernel Hilbert Space (RKHS) $\mathcal{F}$ in which classification is
performed.  The key metrological property is that the Gram matrix
$K_{ij} = k_Q(\mathbf{x}_i, \mathbf{x}_j)$ can distinguish signal
pairs that share identical values of all classical matched-filter
statistics but differ in their quantum-geometric structure  precisely
the degeneracy that renders classical inference blind to Layer~6 and
Layer~7 signatures.

The computational cost of evaluating $k_Q$ on a classical simulator is
$\mathcal{O}(r_\phi n \cdot 2^n)$ per kernel element via statevector
simulation, or $\mathcal{O}(n\chi^3)$ via MPS at bond dimension
$\chi = \chi_\mathrm{crit}$. A formal cost comparison is deferred to Appendix~\ref{app:kernel-cost}.

\subsection{The qubit count constraint: five design drivers for $n = 12$}
\label{subsec:qubit-justification}

The full design-driver justification is moved to Appendix~\ref{app:math-tools}. Operationally, $n=12$ is fixed by five simultaneous constraints:
\begin{itemize}
  \item PCA dimensionality matching ($d=12$).
  \item NISQ coherence and circuit-depth limits.
  \item Trainability under barren-plateau constraints.
  \item IBM hardware topology and routing overhead.
  \item Diminishing marginal variance beyond the twelfth component.
\end{itemize}

On NISQ hardware, the evaluation reduces to two circuit executions of
depth proportional to $r_\phi \cdot n$, constituting the primary source
of quantum advantage in the classification branch of QNIM.

The quantum feature map encodes the input data; the variational ansatz learns to
classify it. The following section describes the parameterised circuit that sits
downstream of the feature map, its expressibility properties, and how its output
is decoded into theory probabilities.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Variational quantum circuit architecture}
\label{sec:vqc}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------
Once the quantum feature map has encoded the input data into Hilbert space, a parameterised variational circuit learns to separate the thirteen theory classes. This section describes the circuit architecture, its expressibility and entanglement properties, and how its output is decoded into theory probabilities.
\subsection{The EfficientSU2 ansatz}
\label{subsec:efficient_su2}

The classification task is performed by a parameterised quantum circuit
(Variational Quantum Classifier, VQC) of the form:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:vqc}
  |\psi(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\theta})\rangle =
    W(\boldsymbol{\theta})\,\mathcal{U}_\phi(\mathbf{x})\,|\mathbf{0}\rangle,
\end{equation}
where $W(\boldsymbol{\theta})$ is the \emph{trainable ansatz} and
$\mathcal{U}_\phi(\mathbf{x})$ is the feature map of
Section~\ref{sec:qfm}.  The QNIM ansatz is built from the
\textsc{EfficientSU2} template, which alternates single-qubit rotation
layers with a linear entanglement structure:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:eff_su2}
  W(\boldsymbol{\theta}) =
    \prod_{r=1}^{r_W}
    \left[
      \bigotimes_{j=1}^{n} R_y(\theta_{j,r}^{(y)})\,R_z(\theta_{j,r}^{(z)})
      \cdot \mathrm{CNOT}_{\mathrm{linear}}
    \right],
\end{equation}
where $R_y(\theta) = e^{-i\theta Y/2}$ and $R_z(\theta) = e^{-i\theta Z/2}$
are single-qubit rotations around the $Y$ and $Z$ Bloch-sphere axes, and
$\mathrm{CNOT}_\mathrm{linear}$ is a layer of CNOT gates coupling qubit $j$
to qubit $j+1$ for $j = 1,\ldots,n-1$.

For $n = 12$ qubits and $r_W = 2$ repetitions, the total parameter count is:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:param_count}
  N_\theta = 2 \cdot n \cdot r_W = 2 \times 12 \times 2 = 48,
\end{equation}
and the circuit depth (in units of two-qubit gate layers) is
$D = r_W \cdot (n-1) = 22$, which is within the coherence budget of
current NISQ processors.
Here $D$ counts only the two-qubit (CNOT) gate layers; 
the total circuit depth including single-qubit rotations 
is $D_\mathrm{total} = r_W(2n + n - 1) = 2 \times (24 + 11) = 70$ 
native gates, consistent with the IBM~Fez compilation 
depth of $D_\mathrm{native} = 31$ two-qubit-equivalent layers 
reported in Appendix~\ref{app:sec:adapters}.

\subsection{Expressibility and entanglement metrics}
\label{subsec:expressibility}

Choosing an ansatz is not merely an architectural decision: a circuit that spans
too small a region of Hilbert space will underfit, while one that is too expressive
will exhibit barren plateaus. Two quantitative metrics—expressibility and entanglement
capacity—characterise where EfficientSU2 sits on this spectrum.

The ability of the ansatz to approximate arbitrary unitary operations is
quantified by the \emph{expressibility} $\mathcal{E}$, defined as the
KullbackLeibler divergence between the distribution of states generated
by random sampling of $\boldsymbol{\theta}$ from the ansatz and the
Haar-random distribution over the full $SU(2^n)$ group \cite{Sim_2019}:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:expressibility}
  \mathcal{E} = D_\mathrm{KL}\!\left(
    P_\mathrm{ansatz}(|\psi\rangle)\,\|\,P_\mathrm{Haar}(|\psi\rangle)
  \right).
\end{equation}
For the QNIM \textsc{EfficientSU2} configuration ($n=12$, $r_W=2$),
numerical estimation via Monte Carlo sampling over $10^4$ parameter
draws yields $\mathcal{E} = 0.043 \pm 0.008$\,nats.  Since a perfect
Haar-random circuit would give $\mathcal{E} = 0$, this value indicates
that the ansatz spans approximately $96\,\%$ of the relevant state space.

The entanglement capacity is quantified by the average bipartite
von Neumann entropy~\eqref{eq:vne}; the measured value 
$\bar{S}_\mathrm{vN} = 3.82 \pm 0.12$\,bits out of a maximum 
$6$\,bits confirms the multi-qubit entanglement needed for 
Layer~6--7 discrimination.

Once the variational circuit has processed the input state, a classical readout
layer translates the quantum expectation values into a probability vector over
the thirteen theory classes.

\subsection{Measurement and classification head}
\label{subsec:measurement}

The circuit output is the expectation value of a product of single-qubit
Pauli-$Z$ observables:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:measurement}
  \hat{y}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\theta}) =
    \langle\psi(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\theta})|\,
\bigotimes_{j=1}^{4} Z_j \,|
    \psi(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\theta})\rangle
    \in [-1, +1].
\end{equation}
For the multi-class extension to thirteen theory classes, the single readout
is replaced by an output register of $\lceil\log_2 13\rceil = 4$ qubits
whose joint Pauli expectation values are decoded by a softmax layer into
a probability simplex over the thirteen classes.  The predicted
class is:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:predicted_class}
  \hat{c} = \arg\max_{c \in \{0,\ldots,12\}}
    \mathrm{softmax}\!\left(\mathbf{W}_\mathrm{out}\,
    \langle\boldsymbol{Z}\rangle + \mathbf{b}_\mathrm{out}\right)_c,
\end{equation}
where $\mathbf{W}_\mathrm{out}$ and $\mathbf{b}_\mathrm{out}$ are small
classical weight matrices trained jointly with the quantum parameters.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Bayesian model ranking and the Bayes factor}
\label{sec:bayes-factor-formalism}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

Once the quantum circuit $W(\boldsymbol{\theta}^\star)$ has classified a 
gravitational-wave event into one of the thirteen beyond-GR theory classes, 
a critical next question arises: \emph{how strong is the statistical evidence 
for that classification?} The VQC output $\mathbf{p}_\mathrm{theory}$ provides 
a probability ranking of theories, but does not quantify the absolute strength 
of the evidence for the top-ranked theory relative to General Relativity (the 
null hypothesis).  This quantification is provided by the \emph{Bayes factor}, 
which formalises Bayesian model comparison through a rigorous likelihood ratio 
(Equation~\ref{eq:bayes-factor-definition}).  Bayes factors serve as the 
bridge between the quantum classification (continuous probability outputs) 
and the scientific inference layer (discrete tier assignments in the Planck 
Reliability Catalogue).

A key aspect of QNIM is the comparison of the thirteen theory hypotheses
against each other and against the null hypothesis (General Relativity).
This comparison is formally quantified through the \emph{Bayes factor}, which
is defined as the ratio of marginal likelihoods (also called the evidence):

\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:bayes-factor-definition}
  B_{H_1/H_0} = \frac{\mathcal{Z}_{H_1}}{\mathcal{Z}_{H_0}} =
    \frac{\int_{\Theta_1} \mathcal{L}(\mathbf{d}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_1, H_1)\,\pi(\boldsymbol{\theta}_1|H_1)\,d\boldsymbol{\theta}_1}
    {\int_{\Theta_0} \mathcal{L}(\mathbf{d}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_0, H_0)\,\pi(\boldsymbol{\theta}_0|H_0)\,d\boldsymbol{\theta}_0},
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal{Z}_{H_i}$ is the marginal likelihood (or ``evidence'') for
hypothesis $H_i$, $\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{d}|\boldsymbol{\theta}_i, H_i)$ is the
likelihood of the data $\mathbf{d}$ under hypothesis $H_i$ with parameters
$\boldsymbol{\theta}_i$, and $\pi(\boldsymbol{\theta}_i|H_i)$ is the prior
distribution over parameters under $H_i$.  In QNIM, $H_0$ is General Relativity
(the ``null'' hypothesis) and $H_1$ represents each of the twelve beyond-GR
theory classes in turn (scalar-tensor, LQG, Chern-Simons, etc.).

The calculation of the evidence $\mathcal{Z}_{H_i}$ is performed via
Laplace approximation around the Maximum A Posteriori (MAP) estimate:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:evidence_laplace}
  \ln\mathcal{Z}_{H_i} \approx \ln\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{d}|\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_i, H_i)
    + \ln\pi(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_i|H_i)
    + \frac{k_i}{2}\ln(2\pi)
    - \frac{1}{2}\ln|\mathbf{H}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_i)|,
\end{equation}
where $\mathbf{H}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_i) = 
-\nabla^2\ln p(\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_i|\mathbf{d},H_i)$ 
is the \emph{negative} Hessian of the log-posterior 
(positive definite at a maximum), so that 
$\ln|\mathbf{H}| > 0$ penalises broad posteriors.  For
GW150914 and the GWTC-3 catalogue, $k_0 = 15$ (GR parameters) and
$k_1 \approx 16$--$18$ (beyond-GR theories, which add 1--3 parameters
to the base set).

\begin{quote}
\noindent\textbf{Note.} Laplace evidence is accurate mainly for unimodal, near-Gaussian posteriors. Compact-binary posteriors can be multimodal, so Bayes factors here are approximate evidence ratios rather than exact marginal likelihoods. A fully rigorous treatment would require nested sampling or thermodynamic integration on the full posterior.
\end{quote}

The \emph{natural logarithm} of the Bayes factor is reported throughout
this thesis as $\ln B_{H_1/H_0}$, or in base-10 form as $\log_{10}B_{H_1/H_0}$
for compatibility with Jeffreys' scale of evidence strength:
\begin{itemize}
  \item $|\ln B| < 2.3$ (i.e., $|\log_{10}B| < 1$): anecdotal evidence.
  \item $2.3 < |\ln B| < 5.1$: weak evidence.
  \item $5.1 < |\ln B| < 9.2$: strong evidence.
  \item $|\ln B| > 9.2$: decisive evidence.
\end{itemize}
A positive $\ln B_{H_1/H_0}$ indicates evidence in favour of the beyond-GR
hypothesis; a negative value favours GR.  In QNIM, most GWTC-3 events exhibit
$\ln B < 0$, confirming GR consistency (see Chapter~\ref{chap:results} for
the full catalogue).

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{QUBO formulation of parameter estimation}
\label{sec:qubo}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------
Theory classification identifies the most probable beyond-GR model; precise extraction of the coupling constants within that model requires solving a high-dimensional, non-convex optimisation problem. This section reformulates that problem as a Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimisation instance suitable for quantum annealing, and describes the hybrid classical post-processing that refines the annealer output.
\subsection{Motivation: the landscape of the likelihood}
\label{subsec:qubo-motivation}

Theory classification is only one half of the QNIM pipeline.  Equally
important is the precise extraction of the physical parameters
$\boldsymbol{\theta} = (m_1, m_2, \chi_\mathrm{eff}, d_L, \ldots)$ within
the framework identified as most likely by the VQC.  This parameter
estimation task is formulated as an optimisation problem over the
log-posterior:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:log_posterior}
  \hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}} = \arg\max_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathcal{P}}
    \left[\ln\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\theta}) + \ln\pi(\boldsymbol{\theta})\right].
\end{equation}
The log-likelihood surface in $\mathcal{P}$ is highly non-convex,
exhibiting hundreds of local maxima in the 15-dimensional space, all
of which yield similar matched-filter scores but correspond to physically
distinct parameter combinations.  Gradient-based optimisers invariably
converge to a local maximum that depends strongly on the initialisation,
introducing systematic biases.

Quantum annealers operate on discrete binary variables, not continuous real-valued
parameters. The first step in constructing the QUBO is therefore to represent each
of the fifteen physical parameters as a string of bits, choosing an encoding that
preserves the physical adjacency structure of the parameter space.

\subsection{Binary discretisation and QUBO Hamiltonian}
\label{subsec:binary-discretisation}

Each continuous parameter $\theta_k \in [\theta_k^\mathrm{min}, \theta_k^\mathrm{max}]$
is discretised into $b_k = 8$ bits using Gray encoding (Appendix~\ref{app:qubo-embedding}),
yielding $N_q = 15 \times 8 = 120$ binary variables. The optimisation
problem is cast as:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:qubo}
  H_\mathrm{QUBO}(\mathbf{q}) = \mathbf{q}^\top Q\,\mathbf{q},
  \qquad \mathbf{q} \in \{0,1\}^{120},
\end{equation}
where $Q$ is constructed from the second-order Taylor expansion of
$-\ln\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\mathbf{q}))$ around the MAP estimate;
diagonal elements encode likelihood gradients and off-diagonal elements
encode FIM parameter correlations. Constraint penalties
($\lambda_\mathrm{penalty} = 10\times\max|Q_{ij}|$) enforce physicality
conditions such as $m_2 \leq m_1$ and $|\chi_i|\leq 1$. Full index-map
conventions are in Appendix~\ref{app:qubo-embedding}.

\subsection{D-Wave annealing and hybrid post-processing}
\label{subsec:annealing}

\noindent\textbf{Scope note.} All annealing results in this thesis use
\texttt{neal.SimulatedAnnealingSampler} (D-Wave Ocean SDK), a classical
algorithm. Physical QPU tunnelling dynamics motivate this design but are
\emph{not} empirically demonstrated here; results are interpreted as
optimisation evidence under a noise-perturbed surrogate model (full protocol
in Appendix~\ref{app:noisy-ensemble-detail}).

The sampler is executed with Gaussian perturbations injected into QUBO
coefficients ($\sigma_Q = 0.03$, 1000 reads per event). The returned
ensemble of low-energy bitstrings is decoded via Gray decoding into
continuous parameter vectors $\{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(k)}\}$, which seed
an L-BFGS-B refinement step. The final QNIM estimate is the candidate with
the highest polished log-posterior; 68\,\% credible intervals follow from
bootstrap resampling of the ensemble.


% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{The QNSPSA-EML-Feynman optimiser}
\label{sec:optimizer}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

Optimising the 48 variational parameters of the VQC on NISQ hardware
requires an algorithm that is simultaneously:
\begin{itemize}
  \item \emph{Gradient-free}: exact gradients are cost-prohibitive on
        hardware due to shot noise, requiring stochastic approximations.
  \item \emph{Geometry-aware}: the cost landscape of the VQC is not
        Euclidean but Riemannian (FubiniStudy metric), necessitating
        a quantum natural gradient step.
  \item \emph{Noise-resilient}: hardware noise introduces biases that
        corrupt gradient estimates, requiring active error mitigation
        at the optimisation level.
\end{itemize}
The QNSPSA-EML-Feynman algorithm addresses all three requirements
simultaneously.

The natural gradient step provides the geometric awareness; SPSA provides the
shot-efficient gradient estimation; their combination constitutes the QNSPSA kernel
of the optimiser.

\subsection{Quantum Natural SPSA (QNSPSA)}
\label{subsec:qnspsa}

The Quantum Natural Simultaneous Perturbation Stochastic Approximation
(QNSPSA) combines the Quantum Natural Gradient (QNG) \cite{Stokes_2020}
with the SPSA finite-difference gradient estimator \cite{Spall_1998}.

At iteration $t$, a random Rademacher perturbation vector
$\boldsymbol{\Delta} \in \{-1,+1\}^p$ is drawn uniformly.  The stochastic
gradient and Quantum Geometric Tensor (QGT) estimates are obtained from
four circuit evaluations:
\begin{align}
  \label{eq:qnspsa_gradient}
  \hat{g}_t &= \frac{f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_t + c_t\boldsymbol{\Delta}_t)
                     - f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_t - c_t\boldsymbol{\Delta}_t)}
                    {2c_t\boldsymbol{\Delta}_t}, \\
  \label{eq:qgt_estimate}
  \hat{F}_t &= \frac{1}{2}\!\left(
    \hat{g}_t \otimes \hat{g}_t
    + \hat{g}'_t \otimes \hat{g}'_t\right),
\end{align}
where $\hat{g}'_t$ uses an independent Rademacher draw $\boldsymbol{\Delta}'_t$.
Note that $\hat{F}_t$ is an \emph{approximation} of the 
Quantum Geometric Tensor based on the outer product of 
stochastic gradient estimates, following the QNSPSA 
construction of~\cite{Gacon_2021}. This approximation 
is computationally tractable on NISQ hardware but 
underestimates the off-diagonal elements of the true 
QGT; the resulting preconditioned step is nonetheless 
superior to Euclidean gradient descent 
(Section~\ref{app:qnspsa-convergence}).
The inverse QGT approximation $\hat{H}^{-1}$ is updated using the
rank-two ShermanMorrisonWoodbury identity:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:smw}
  \hat{H}^{-1}_{t+1} = \hat{H}^{-1}_t
    - \frac{\hat{H}^{-1}_t\hat{F}_t\hat{H}^{-1}_t}
           {1 + \mathrm{Tr}(\hat{H}^{-1}_t\hat{F}_t)},
\end{equation}
which avoids the $\mathcal{O}(p^3)$ cost of direct matrix inversion while
maintaining a numerically stable approximation of the FubiniStudy metric.
The natural gradient step is:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:ng_step}
  \boldsymbol{\theta}_{t+1} = \boldsymbol{\theta}_t
    - a_t\,\hat{H}^{-1}_t\,\hat{g}_t,
\end{equation}
with the learning rate schedule $a_t = a_0/(t + 1)^{0.602}$ following the
RobbinsMonro conditions for stochastic approximation convergence.

Even with natural-gradient preconditioning, hardware noise biases every gradient
estimate. The Error Mitigation Layer corrects this bias at the gradient level,
before it propagates into the parameter update.

\subsection{Error Mitigation Layer (EML)}
\label{subsec:eml}

Hardware noise induces a systematic bias in the loss function:
$f_\mathrm{noisy}(\boldsymbol{\theta}) = f_\mathrm{ideal}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
+ \epsilon_\mathrm{hw}(\boldsymbol{\theta})$.  The EML applies Zero Noise
Extrapolation (ZNE) at the gradient level by evaluating the circuit at
three noise amplification factors $\{\lambda_k\} = \{1, 2, 3\}$ via
unitary gate folding (replacing a gate $U$ by $U(U^\dagger U)^k$):
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:zne_gradient}
  \hat{g}^\mathrm{ZNE}_t = \sum_{k=1}^{K} c_k\,\hat{g}(\lambda_k),
  \qquad
  \sum_k c_k = 1,\; \sum_k c_k\lambda_k^j = 0 \; (j=1,\ldots,K-1),
\end{equation}
where the Richardson extrapolation coefficients $\{c_k\}$ ensure that the
extrapolated gradient is unbiased to order $K-1$ in the noise.  For
$K=3$ noise amplification factors $\{\lambda_1=1, \lambda_2=2, \lambda_3=3\}$,
the coefficients are $c_1 = 3$, $c_2 = -3$, $c_3 = 1$,
reducing the gradient bias by a factor of $\sim\!\!10$ at the cost of
tripling the number of circuit evaluations per step.

ZNE removes hardware-induced bias, but it does not prevent the optimiser from
drifting into flat regions of parameter space where the quantum Fisher information
vanishes—the barren plateau problem. The Feynman regularisation addresses this by
steering the optimiser toward high-curvature regions of Hilbert space.

\subsection{Feynman physical regularisation}
\label{subsec:feynman}

The ``Feynman'' component of the optimiser incorporates physical
conservation laws as a regularisation term in the gradient:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:feynman_reg}
  \hat{g}^\mathrm{total}_t =
    \hat{g}^\mathrm{ZNE}_t + \lambda_R\,\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
    \mathcal{R}_\mathrm{curv}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_t),
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal{R}_\mathrm{curv}$ is a curvature regulariser derived from
the FubiniStudy metric:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:curvature_reg}
  \mathcal{R}_\mathrm{curv}(\boldsymbol{\theta}) =
    \sum_{k} \left(
      \langle\partial_k\psi|\partial_k\psi\rangle
      - |\langle\psi|\partial_k\psi\rangle|^2
    \right)
    = \frac{1}{4}\sum_k \mathcal{F}_{Q,k},
\end{equation}
which is, up to a constant, the sum of the QFI over all parameters.

\noindent\textbf{Implementation note:} The regularisation coefficient
$\lambda_R$ in Eq.~(\ref{eq:feynman_reg}) has a sign and magnitude tuned
such that the gradient direction encourages exploration of high-curvature
regions (high QFI, avoiding barren plateaus).
In the reported training run, $\lambda_R = +5.0 \times 10^{-3}$ (dimensionless), tuned by grid search over $\lambda_R \in \{10^{-4}, 10^{-3}, 5\times10^{-3}, 10^{-2}, 10^{-1}\}$ on the validation set after 50 warm-up steps; this value produced the highest validation accuracy at step 50 while keeping gradient variance above $10^{-3}$.
In the Adam optimizer step $\boldsymbol{\theta}_{t+1} = \boldsymbol{\theta}_t - a_t \hat{g}^\mathrm{total}_t$,
the effective sign of the regularisation term is determined by the sign of
$\lambda_R$. Maximising $\mathcal{R}_\mathrm{curv}$ is equivalent to pushing the
quantum state toward regions of Hilbert space with high parameter
sensitivity, counteracting the \emph{barren plateau} phenomenon
discussed in Section~\ref{subsec:barren-plateaus}.

The Feynman regularisation also imposes physical priors through a
penalty on non-physical states:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:physical_prior}
  \mathcal{P}_\mathrm{phys}(\boldsymbol{\theta}) =
    \sum_{\alpha}\max\!\left(0,\,
    C_\alpha(\boldsymbol{\theta}(\mathbf{q})) \right)^2,
\end{equation}
where $C_\alpha \leq 0$ encodes physical constraints such as
$m_2 \leq m_1$, $0 \leq \chi_i \leq 1$, and
$d_L \geq d_L^\mathrm{min}(\mathrm{SNR})$.

\subsection{Convergence analysis and barren plateaus}
\label{subsec:barren-plateaus}

A fundamental obstacle in training deep VQCs is the \emph{barren plateau}
phenomenon \cite{Cerezo_2021}: for random parameter initialisations in
circuits of depth $D \propto n$, the variance of the gradient vanishes
exponentially:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:barren_plateau}
  \mathrm{Var}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\!\left[
    \frac{\partial f}{\partial\theta_k}
  \right] \propto 2^{-n}.
\end{equation}
For $n=12$ qubits this gives $\mathrm{Var} \approx 2.4\times10^{-4}$,
which is marginally trainable.  The QNSPSA-EML-Feynman algorithm mitigates
this in three ways:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Layer-by-layer initialisation}: parameters are trained
        one layer at a time, avoiding the deep-circuit regime where the
        barren plateau is most severe.
  \item \textbf{QNG step}: the natural gradient rescales the step size by
        the inverse QGT, amplifying gradient components in flat directions
        of the cost landscape.
  \item \textbf{Feynman curvature regularisation}: pushing the state toward
        high-QFI regions maintains a non-negligible gradient variance
        throughout training.
\end{enumerate}

Table~\ref{tab:barren-compare} documents the measured gradient variance
at selected qubit scales; the convergence theorem and full comparison table are moved to Appendix~\ref{app:qnspsa-convergence}.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Quantum Fisher information and the metrological advantage}
\label{sec:qfi_methodology}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

The quantum metrological advantage is not merely a theoretical statement about
the hierarchy $\mathcal{F}_Q \geq \mathcal{F}_C$: it must be numerically measured
on the QNIM VQC state for each of the fifteen physical parameters. The parameter-shift
rule provides an exact, hardware-executable method for this measurement.

\subsection{Computing the QFI via the parameter-shift rule}
\label{subsec:parameter_shift}

The Quantum Fisher Information for parameter $\theta_k$ of the QNIM VQC
is:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:qfi_vqc}
  \mathcal{F}_{Q,k}(\boldsymbol{\theta}) = 4\left[
    \langle\partial_k\psi|\partial_k\psi\rangle
    - \left|\langle\psi|\partial_k\psi\rangle\right|^2
  \right].
\end{equation}
On NISQ hardware, The expected value of any observable differs exactly by \cite{Mitarai_2018,Schuld_2019}:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:param_shift}
  \frac{\partial\langle O\rangle}{\partial\theta_k} =
  \frac{\langle O\rangle(\boldsymbol{\theta} + \frac{\pi}{2}\mathbf{e}_k)
        - \langle O\rangle(\boldsymbol{\theta} - \frac{\pi}{2}\mathbf{e}_k)}
       {2},
\end{equation}
where $\mathbf{e}_k$ is the unit vector in direction $k$.  This requires
two additional circuit evaluations per parameter, for a total of
$2p = 96$ evaluations of the QFI gradient per optimisation step.

With the quantum Fisher information numerically accessible, we can directly compare
it to the classical bound achievable by matched filtering, quantifying the advantage
that QNIM delivers per parameter.

\subsection{QFI versus classical Fisher information}
\label{subsec:qfi_vs_cfi}

The classical Fisher Information for parameter $\theta_k$ in the
gravitational-wave context is \cite{Veitch_2015}:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:cfi_gw}
  \mathcal{F}_{C,k} = 4\,\mathrm{Re}
    \int_0^\infty \frac{|\tilde{h}_{,k}(f)|^2}{S_n(f)}\,df.
\end{equation}
The ratio $\mathcal{F}_{Q,k}/\mathcal{F}_{C,k}$ quantifies the quantum
metrological advantage for each parameter.  As demonstrated in
Chapter~\ref{chap:quantum-advantage}, this ratio lies in the range
$[1.75, 2.23]$ for the five critical beyond-GR parameters, meaning
that QNIM can achieve a parameter uncertainty between $\sqrt{1/1.75}
\approx 76\,\%$ and $\sqrt{1/2.23} \approx 67\,\%$ of the classical
CramrRao bound  a factor of $1.3$--$1.5$ improvement in precision.

The physical reason for this advantage is the non-commutativity of
the quantum state with respect to the beyond-GR parameters.  Whereas
the classical FIM treats all parameters as commuting variables in the
real Hilbert space $\mathbb{R}^d$, the quantum FIM captures off-diagonal
correlations between parameters through the full complex amplitude
$\langle\psi|\partial_k\psi\rangle$, which has no classical analogue.

\subsection{Mapping from circuit QFI to physical-parameter QFI}
\label{subsec:qfi_chain_rule}

The physical-parameter QFI is obtained via the chain rule through the
preprocessing pipeline
$\theta_k \!\to\! \tilde{h}(f;\theta_k) \!\to\! \mathrm{STFT} \!\to\! \mathrm{PCA}
\!\to\! \mathbf{x}_\mathrm{PCA}(\theta_k)$:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:qfi_physical}
  \mathcal{F}_{Q,k}^\mathrm{phys} = \sum_{i,j=1}^{12}
    \mathcal{F}_{Q,ij}^\mathrm{VQC}
    \frac{\partial x_i}{\partial \theta_k}
    \frac{\partial x_j}{\partial \theta_k},
\end{equation}
with finite-difference Jacobians at step $\delta\theta_k = 0.05\,\theta_k^\mathrm{MAP}$
(Eq.~\ref{eq:jacobian_fd} in Appendix~\ref{app:math-tools}). The full
$15\times15$ QGT and numerical values at GW150914 MAP are provided in
Table~\ref{tab:qfi-jacobian} and Figure~\ref{fig:qgt-matrix} of
Appendix~\ref{app:math-tools}. The key result is that the two highest-gain
beyond-GR parameters are $\kappa_\mathrm{CS}$ ($G_k = 2.23$,
$|\nabla x|_\infty = 0.198$) and $R_c$ ($G_k = 2.23$,
$|\nabla x|_\infty = 0.156$), whose frequency-quadratic and
frequency-linear phase modulations concentrate strongly onto the leading
PCA components. Standard GR parameters yield modest gains
($G_k \in [1.01, 1.10]$) because matched filtering is already near-optimal
for linearly encoded phase corrections. Positive-semidefiniteness checks
are reported in Appendix~\ref{app:math-tools}.

\section{The unified inference pipeline}
\label{sec:pipeline}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

The seven components described in this chapter—encoding, feature map, variational
circuit, Bayesian ranking, QUBO estimation, gradient optimisation, and metrological
quantification—operate as a unified pipeline. The following workflow describes how
a single gravitational-wave event traverses each stage from raw strain to Planck
Reliability tier.

\subsection{End-to-end workflow}
\label{subsec:workflow}

The complete QNIM analytical workflow for a single gravitational-wave
event proceeds through the following stages, which are orchestrated by
the \texttt{HybridInferenceOrchestrator} domain service described in Chapter~\ref{chap:software}:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Acquisition and conditioning} (classical):
        download GWOSC data, estimate $S_n(f)$, whiten, STFT.

  \item \textbf{Feature extraction} (classical):
        compute matched-filter statistics at MAP; apply PCA to
        obtain $\mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{R}^{12}$.

  \item \textbf{Quantum feature encoding} (quantum):
        apply $\mathcal{U}_\phi(\mathbf{x})$ to produce
        $|\phi(\mathbf{x})\rangle \in \mathcal{H}$.

  \item \textbf{Theory classification} (quantum + classical):
        evaluate $W(\boldsymbol{\theta}^\star)|\phi(\mathbf{x})\rangle$,
        measure, decode via softmax to obtain theory probabilities
        $\mathbf{p}_\mathrm{theory}$.

  \item \textbf{Parameter estimation} (quantum annealing):
        construct $H_\mathrm{QUBO}$ for the top-ranked theory, submit
        to D-Wave, decode ensemble, refine via L-BFGS-B.

  \item \textbf{Statistical ranking} (classical):
        compute Bayes factors $\ln B_{H_1/H_0}$, apply Bonferroni
        correction, generate Planck Reliability Sheet.

  \item \textbf{Cosmological inference} (classical):
        if $d_L$ precision $< 10\,\%$, infer $H_0$ contribution.
\end{enumerate}
