\chapter{Software architecture and implementation}
\label{chap:software}

Chapter~\ref{chap:methodology-qnim} defined \emph{what} QNIM does: how it leverages
quantum feature maps and hybrid classical-quantum optimisation to resolve 
quantum-gravity signatures in gravitational-wave data.  This chapter describes 
\emph{how} it is implemented in a way that renders the results reproducible
and the physics independent of the quantum backend.


Because the software implementation is secondary to the methodological contribution, this chapter keeps only the architectural patterns, the dependency structure, the primary use cases, and the circuit-validation protocol. Full testing, mutation, persistence, adapter, and reproducibility details are deferred to Appendix~\ref{app:software-architecture}.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Architectural overview}
\label{sec:arch-overview}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

QNIM adopts three complementary architectural patterns to ensure the physics
is independent of the computational substrate:

\begin{description}
  \item[Clean Architecture.] Code is organised into concentric layers. 
        The domain (innermost) layer contains pure scientific logic with no 
        framework dependencies. Infrastructure (outermost) provides concrete 
        implementations of interfaces declared in the domain 
        \cite{Martin_2017}.

  \item[Domain-Driven Design (DDD).] Scientific concepts (quantum state,
        gravitational-wave event, inference result) are expressed as 
        first-class code objects (Entities, Aggregates, Value Objects)
        rather than as database rows or API responses \cite{Evans_2004}.

  \item[Hexagonal Architecture.] The domain exposes interfaces (ports)
        through which it interacts with the world. Concrete implementations
        (adapters) handle each technology: IBM Quantum, D-Wave, GWOSC, local
        simulator. Backend changes require only adapter substitution
        \cite{Cockburn_2005}.
\end{description}

\noindent The five-layer structure (Figure~\ref{fig:layer-dependency}) is:
(1)~\textbf{Domain}: pure scientific entities and services.
(2)~\textbf{Application}: use cases and orchestrators.
(3)~\textbf{Infrastructure}: backend adapters (IBM Quantum, D-Wave, GWOSC).
(4)~\textbf{CLI}: command-line interface (Typer).
(5)~\textbf{Presentation}: REST API (FastAPI) and dashboard.

\noindent Full architectural details are documented in Appendix~\ref{app:software-architecture}.

The resulting codebase comprises 156 core domain and 
application modules with zero circular dependencies, 
as verified by \texttt{pydeps} static analysis 
(Objective~O1, Section~\ref{sec:objectives}); 
the full dependency graph is documented in 
Appendix~\ref{app:software-architecture}.

\begin{figure}[ht]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{arch_final.png}
\caption[Layer dependency graph of QNIM architecture]{Layer dependency graph.
  Inner layers have no knowledge of outer layers. This inversion of control
  ensures scientific code can run on any backend.}
\label{fig:layer-dependency}
\end{figure}

\noindent
Table~\ref{tab:use_cases} lists the six primary application 
use cases implemented as Command objects in the application 
layer; each encapsulates a single user-facing workflow and 
is independently testable without quantum hardware.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption[Primary QNIM application use cases.]{Primary QNIM application use cases.}
\label{tab:use_cases}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lp{4cm}p{4cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Use case} & \textbf{Input} & \textbf{Output} \\
\midrule
\texttt{RunInferenceUseCase} & event ID, backend config & \texttt{InferenceResult}, latency \\
\texttt{TrainModelUseCase} & dataset path, hyperparams & \texttt{ModelWeights}, history \\
\texttt{GenerateExperimentUseCase} & config, event list & experiment report, CSV \\
\texttt{ValidateCircuitUseCase} & circuit spec & Tutor Test report \\
\texttt{EstimateParametersUseCase} & QUBO matrix, config & MAP estimates, credible intervals \\
\texttt{ComputeCosmologyUseCase} & results (Class A/B) & $H_0$ posterior, tension \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The same layering discipline governs the validation workflow: lightweight unit, integration, and end-to-end testing are used during development, while the circuit-specific Tutor Test is the final gate before hardware execution.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Testing strategy}
\label{sec:testing}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------
A quantum application in which hardware credits are finite and execution errors are costly requires a rigorous validation workflow. QNIM uses the standard unit, integration, and end-to-end testing stack, detailed in Appendix~\ref{app:software-architecture}, together with a circuit-specific pre-deployment check: the Tutor Test.

\subsection{Tutor Test protocol}
\label{subsec:tutor_test}

We introduce what we call the \emph{Tutor Test}: a domain-specific circuit
verification protocol developed for this work that systematises best practices
in variational quantum circuit validation.  The name reflects the analogy of
making the circuit ``explain itself''  just as a tutor must reproduce a
derivation step-by-step before being trusted, a circuit must pass a battery
of analytic and numerical checks before any hardware credit is consumed.

For each \texttt{QuantumCircuitSpec}, the Tutor Test validates:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Unitary correctness}: the implemented circuit matrix
        $U_\mathrm{impl}$ matches the theoretical matrix $U_\mathrm{theory}$
        to within $\epsilon_U = 10^{-6}$ in the Frobenius norm, as verified
        on the Qiskit \texttt{StatevectorSimulator}.

  \item \textbf{Expressibility bound}: the measured expressibility
        $\mathcal{E}$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:expressibility}) lies within 5\,\% of the expected value 
$\mathcal{E}_\mathrm{ref} = 0.043 \pm 0.008$\,nats 
for the $(n=12, r_W=2)$ configuration 
(Section~\ref{subsec:expressibility}).

  \item \textbf{Barren plateau threshold}: the gradient variance
        $\mathrm{Var}[\partial f/\partial\theta_k]$ averaged over 100
        random initialisations is $\geq 10^{-3}$, confirming trainability above the 
barren-plateau floor (the theoretical unmitigated 
value for $n=12$ is $\sim 2.4\times10^{-4}$; 
the threshold $10^{-3}$ ensures the EML mitigation 
is active and effective).

  \item \textbf{Measurement bias}: the difference between the noiseless
        expectation value and the shot-noise average over 8192 shots is
        $< 3\sigma_\mathrm{shot}^\mathrm{max} = 3/\sqrt{8192} 
\approx 0.033$, where $\sigma_\mathrm{shot}^\mathrm{max}$ 
is the worst-case shot noise for a Pauli-$Z$ expectation 
value (achieved when $\langle Z\rangle = 0$).

  \item \textbf{Physical invariants}: the output state satisfies all
        normalization, phase, and symmetry constraints derived from the
        physics of the problem (e.g., parity symmetry under time reversal
        for equal-mass binaries).
\end{enumerate}

The Tutor Test is implemented as a \texttt{pytest} fixture
(\texttt{@pytest.fixture(scope="session")}) that is automatically executed
before any hardware run, aborting the job if any validation fails.  This
prevents quantum computing credits from being consumed on incorrectly
implemented circuits.

% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
% CHAPTER 5  SYNTHETIC DATA GENERATION
% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\chapter{Synthetic data generation}
\label{chap:synthetic}

The quantum classifier of Chapter~\ref{chap:methodology-qnim} learns to distinguish theories from labelled examples. Since no quantum-gravity signal has been definitively observed in real gravitational-wave data, those examples must be synthetic yet physically consistent. This chapter describes the Synthetic Signal and Theory Generator (SSTG): a framework that injects beyond-GR corrections across twelve beyond-GR 
theory classes plus the GR null hypothesis, for a total 
of thirteen classification targets, validates that the synthetic data are indistinguishable from real LIGO observations, and establishes a reproducible benchmark for quantum advantage testing.

The SSTG is not a generic noise-injection tool. It is systematically grounded in the seven-layer theoretical hierarchy of Chapter~\ref{chap:theoretical-framework}, implements each of the thirteen beyond-GR theories as a distinct injection protocol, controls signal amplitude at realistic observational SNR, models realistic detector noise including non-Gaussian glitches and inter-detector correlations, and is validated against the 90 confident events of GWTC-3 using distribution-shift diagnostics and parameter-recovery tests.

Section~\ref{sec:sstg-design} presents the design principles of the SSTG.
Section~\ref{sec:waveform_base} describes the base waveform models.
Section~\ref{sec:theory_injections} details the injection functions for each
of the 13 signal theories.
Section~\ref{sec:noise-model} describes the noise model.
Section~\ref{sec:dataset_construction} documents the construction of the
training, validation, and test datasets.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Design principles of the SSTG}
\label{sec:sstg-design}

\textbf{Physical fidelity:} Each synthetic waveform is computed from a reviewed approximant (IMRPhenomD or SEOBNRv4), with beyond-GR corrections derived directly from Chapter~\ref{chap:theoretical-framework} and amplitudes bounded by observational upper limits from GW events and solar-system tests.

\textbf{Systematic parameter coverage:} The parameter space is sampled via Latin Hypercube Design \cite{McKay_1979}, ensuring uniform coverage without clustering. This guarantees that the training set collectively represents the full range of physically allowed signals, preventing bias toward common configurations.

\textbf{Decorator pattern:} The SSTG uses the software Decorator pattern (Section~\ref{app:sec:domain-model}) with a base waveform generator and theory-specific anomaly decorators, enabling independent testing of each correction term, arbitrary superposition for compound hypotheses, and hot-swapping of the base waveform approximant without modifying the correction logic.

\textbf{Controlled SNR:} Each event is generated at a pre-specified optimal network SNR
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:optimal_snr}
  \rho_\mathrm{opt}^2 = 4\,\mathrm{Re}
    \int_0^\infty \frac{|\tilde{h}(f)|^2}{S_n(f)}\,df,
\end{equation}
by rescaling the luminosity distance $d_L$. The training dataset covers $\rho_\mathrm{opt} \in [8, 100]$ with log-uniform distribution, oversampling the near-threshold region where beyond-GR signals are hardest to detect.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Base waveform models}
\label{sec:waveform_base}

The SSTG employs three complementary waveform models selected automatically based on system parameters \cite{Khan_2016}. \textit{IMRPhenomD} covers non-precessing binaries ($q \leq 18$, $|\chi_i| \leq 0.85$) with $\lesssim 1\,\%$ phase accuracy; SEOBNRv4 \cite{Bohe_2017} handles precessing systems via Effective One Body dynamics; and the NRSur7dq4 surrogate \cite{Varma_2019} covers extreme parameters ($q > 18$, $|\chi_i| > 0.95$) via Gaussian Process interpolation. This tiered selection balances computational speed, parameter coverage, and accuracy for faithful representation of the inspiral-merger-ringdown structure.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Theory injection functions}
\label{sec:theory_injections}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

This section documents the beyond-GR correction term $\delta\tilde{h}(f)$
added by each anomaly decorator.  The complete synthetic waveform is:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:synthetic_total}
  \tilde{h}_\mathrm{synth}(f) = \tilde{h}_\mathrm{GR}(f)
    + \sum_{k \in \mathcal{T}} \epsilon_k\,\delta\tilde{h}_k(f),
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal{T}$ is the set of active theories, $\epsilon_k$ is a
dimensionless injection amplitude drawn from a uniform distribution within
the observationally allowed range, and $\delta\tilde{h}_k(f)$ is the
unit-amplitude correction.
Each injection class corresponds to one row of 
Table~\ref{tab:injection-params}; the class numbering 
follows the SSTG taxonomy, which is distinct from the 
physical-layer hierarchy of 
Chapter~\ref{chap:theoretical-framework}.

Each injection class applies the beyond-GR correction
$\delta\tilde{h}_k(f)$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:synthetic_total})
according to the parameters and observational bounds
summarised in Table~\ref{tab:injection-params}. Briefly:
Class~0 is the pure-GR null hypothesis; Class~1 varies
$d_L$ for standard-siren cosmology; Classes~2--3 inject
secondary QNM modes at GR-predicted frequencies;
Class~4 adds a 3.5PN phase deformation
(Eq.~\ref{eq:spa_phase}); Classes~5--8 implement the
Layer~5 propagation effects (Randall--Sundrum, Brans--Dicke,
massive graviton, Chern--Simons) using the equations derived
in Section~\ref{subsec:layer5}; Classes~9--10 cover
LIV at $\alpha=2$ and $\alpha=4$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:liv_dispersion});
Class~11 applies the LQG area-quantisation correction
(Eq.~\ref{eq:lqg_correction}); and Class~12 combines
GUP and non-commutative spacetime, which are degenerate
at accessible SNR with the current 12-qubit feature map
\cite{Kobakhidze_2016}.


\subsection{Summary of injection parameters}
\label{subsec:injection_summary}

Table~\ref{tab:injection-params} lists all 13 theory classes with their
injection parameters, physical observable, and observational bound used
to set the prior range.

         90\,\% credibility.}
\label{tab:injection-params}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{2cm}p{2.5cm}p{3cm}p{3cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Class} & \textbf{Theory} &
\textbf{Injection parameter} & \textbf{Bound} \\
\midrule
0  & GR (null)            & ---                        & --- \\
1$^\dagger$ & Standard siren       & $H_0$ / km\,s$^{-1}$\,Mpc$^{-1}$ & $[60,80]$ \\
2  & QNM (2,1)            & $\epsilon_{(2,1)}$         & $< 0.2$ \\
3  & QNM (3,3)            & $\epsilon_{(3,3)}$         & $< 0.08$ \\

5  & Extra dimensions     & $R_c$ / $\mu$m             & $< 0.44$ \\
6  & Scalartensor        & $\omega_\mathrm{BD}$        & $> 4 \times 10^4$ \\
7  & Graviton mass        & $m_g$ / eV                 & $< 1.27\times10^{-23}$ \\
8  & ChernSimons (CS)    & $\kappa_\mathrm{CS}$ / m$^{-1}$ & $< 5\times10^{-19}$ \\
9  & LIV ($\alpha=2$)     & $\lambda_A$ / m            & $[0, 10^{16}]$ \\
10 & LIV ($\alpha=4$)     & $\lambda_A$ / m            & $[0, 10^{22}]$ \\
11 & LQG                  & $\Delta/\ell_\mathrm{Pl}^2$ & $[0.1, 10]$ \\
12 & GUP + NC spacetime   & $\beta$, $\theta_{\mathrm{NC}}$ & $\beta < 10^{34}$; $\theta_\mathrm{NC} \in [0, 10^2]\,\ell_P^2$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\\ \footnotesize $^\dagger$~The standard-siren class represents the GR null hypothesis with cosmological parameter variation; it is not a beyond-GR theory but is included as a distinct classification target to separate cosmological effects from local beyond-GR corrections. \par \vspace{3pt} \footnotesize GUP and non-commutative spacetime corrections are combined 
in Class~12 because both produce amplitude-level corrections 
to the late-ringdown tail with characteristic frequency 
$f \sim c^3/(GM) \approx 150$\,Hz for a $60\,M_\odot$ 
remnant, making them indistinguishable at SNR~$<40$ with 
12 PCA components; see~\cite{Kobakhidze_2016} for the 
NC phase-space structure and the GUP correction derivation 
in Appendix~\ref{app:bms-superad}.
\end{table}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Noise model}
\label{sec:noise-model}

The SSTG employs an O4 design-sensitivity Advanced LIGO PSD 
\cite{LIGO_T1800044} with three components (seismic, thermal, 
shot noise), injects glitches from the Gravity Spy catalogue \cite{Zevin_2017} into 15\,\% of events to simulate non-Gaussian artefacts, and models inter-detector correlations via Schumann resonances below $\sim\!40$\,Hz. Correlated noise between H1 and L1 is generated by: (i) drawing independent white noise $\mathbf{z} = (z_H, z_L)$; (ii) applying the Cholesky factor of the correlation matrix; (iii) colouring by the per-detector PSD.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Dataset construction}
\label{sec:dataset_construction}

The 78,000-event dataset consists of 54,600 training events (70\,\%), 11,700 validation events (15\,\%), and 11,700 test events (15\%), each stratified by theory class (4,200 per class) and SNR bin (8 logarithmic bins from $\rho = 8$ to $\rho = 100$) to prevent learning morphological correlations with SNR alone.

The 13 classes have equal prior probability in training ($1/13$ each), deliberately different from the astrophysical prior favouring GR. This design ensures the classifier identifies \emph{which} beyond-GR theory is present given that a beyond-GR signal is detected, rather than deciding whether one exists (determined by Bayes factor tests).

The feature matrix $\mathbf{X} \in \mathbb{R}^{N \times 12}$ is standardised to zero mean and unit variance within each PCA component using training-set statistics applied without retraining to the test set. Table~\ref{tab:feature_stats} reports Fisher discriminant ratios $F_j$ for inter-class separability:

\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:fisher_ratio}
  F_j = \frac{\sum_c N_c(\bar{x}_j^{(c)} - \bar{x}_j)^2 / (K-1)}
             {\sum_c\sum_{i\in c}(x_{ij} - \bar{x}_j^{(c)})^2 / (N-K)},
\end{equation}

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\caption[Fisher discriminant ratios for PCA features]{Fisher discriminant ratio $F_j$ for the 12 PCA features.
         Higher values indicate greater inter-class discriminability.}
\label{tab:feature_stats}
\begin{tabular}{@{}ccc|ccc@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{PC} & $F_j$ & \textbf{Rank} &
\textbf{PC} & $F_j$ & \textbf{Rank} \\
\midrule
1  & 4.83 & 2  &  7 & 1.12 & 9  \\
2  & 5.61 & 1  &  8 & 0.87 & 11 \\
3  & 3.49 & 4  &  9 & 1.31 & 8  \\
4  & 3.82 & 3  & 10 & 0.64 & 12 \\
5  & 2.14 & 6  & 11 & 1.49 & 7  \\
6  & 2.71 & 5  & 12 & 0.41 & 12 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

Data augmentation is applied online during training using four transforms: (i) \textbf{phase jitter}: random coalescence phase $\phi_0 \sim \mathcal{U}(0, 2\pi)$ for sky-position rotation; (ii) \textbf{SNR rescaling}: multiplication by $\rho_\mathrm{aug} \sim \mathcal{U}(0.8, 1.2)$ for calibration uncertainty; (iii) \textbf{detector permutation}: exchange of H1 and L1 realisations for overhead sources; (iv) \textbf{feature-space noise}: Gaussian perturbation $\boldsymbol{\xi} \sim \mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0}, 0.02^2\,\mathbf{I}_{12})$. Each is applied independently with probability 0.5 per batch, yielding up to $54{,}600 \times 2^4 = 873{,}600$ 
augmented training variants; in practice, stochastic 
sampling of the transform probabilities produces a 
different augmented batch at each epoch, providing 
continuous diversity without deterministic 
data duplication.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Distribution shift analysis}
\label{subsec:distribution_shift}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

A synthetic dataset is only useful if it covers the same region of feature space as the real observations. We verify this by applying the PCA projection to all 90 confident gravitational-wave events from the GWTC-3 catalogue and comparing the resulting distributions to the synthetic test set using three complementary tests:

\textbf{MMD test:} Maximum Mean Discrepancy between balanced subsets ($N=90$ synthetic matched to 90 real events by source type and SNR bin) yields $\hat{\mathrm{MMD}}^2 = 0.012 \pm 0.003$. Control test (synthetic-vs-synthetic) gives $\hat{\mathrm{MMD}}^2_\mathrm{ctrl} = 0.008 \pm 0.002$, confirming zero distribution shift at $2\sigma$.

\textbf{Kolmogorov-Smirnov test:} All 12 marginal distributions yield $p > 0.05$, detecting no significant shift at the 5\,\% level.

\textbf{C2ST (classifier two-sample test):} A binary logistic classifier trained to distinguish 90 synthetic from 90 real events achieves AUC $= 0.52 \pm 0.03$ (10-fold CV), indistinguishable from random guessing (AUC = 0.5) and control test performance (AUC$_\mathrm{ctrl} = 0.51 \pm 0.03$) at $1\sigma$ level.

\begin{figure}[ht]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.9\textwidth]{tsne_real_vs_synth.pdf}
\caption[t-SNE visualization of real vs.\ synthetic events in 12-dimensional PCA space.]{
  \label{fig:tsne_real_vs_synth}
  t-SNE projection of 90 GWTC-3 real events and 90 balanced synthetic events 
  into two dimensions, showing source-type distribution. 
  (\emph{Left}) Synthetic events colored by source type (BBH, BNS, NSBH).
  (\emph{Right}) Real GWTC-3 events (circles, squares, triangles with black 
  edges, size $\times 4$ for visibility) overlaid on the synthetic distribution 
  (light gray background). The overlap demonstrates that real events occupy 
  the same feature-space region as quality-matched synthetic injections, 
  supporting the conclusion that the synthetic dataset faithfully covers 
  the observational parameter space.
}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Parameter recovery test}
\label{subsec:parameter_recovery}

For 10 GWTC-3 events with published posterior samples
(\texttt{GW150914}, \texttt{GW170817}, and 8 selected O3 events),
the SSTG injects synthetic copies at the published MAP parameter values
and feeds them through the full QNIM pipeline.  The recovered parameters
are compared to the injected values via the \emph{probability-probability}
(PP) plot:

\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:pp_plot}
  \mathrm{PP}(p) = \frac{1}{N_\mathrm{inj}}
    \sum_{k=1}^{N_\mathrm{inj}}
    \mathbf{1}\!\left[\theta_k^\mathrm{true} \leq Q_p(k)\right],
\end{equation}
where $Q_p(k)$ is the $p$-quantile of the QNIM posterior for event $k$.
A perfectly calibrated estimator satisfies $\mathrm{PP}(p) = p$ for all $p$.
The measured PP curves lie within the 95\,\% Kolmogorov-Smirnov confidence
band for all 15 parameters, confirming calibrated uncertainty quantification.
These results are documented in detail in Appendix~\ref{app:sstg-pe-calib}.

\subsection{Injection recovery campaign}
\label{subsec:injection_recovery}

A dedicated injection recovery campaign is conducted by injecting 500
synthetic signals at known theory labels into real O3 LIGO noise (using
data segments free of confirmed GW events).  The QNIM classifier achieves:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Overall accuracy}: $91.3 \pm 0.8\,\%$ (vs. $91.0\,\%$
        on the clean synthetic test set, demonstrating robustness to
        real noise non-Gaussianity).
  \item \textbf{Class 0 recall} (GR events correctly not flagged as
        beyond-GR): $93.7\,\%$.
  \item \textbf{Layer~7 precision} (quantum-geometry events): $84.2\,\%$
        (lower, as expected, due to the small amplitude of Layer~7
        corrections at accessible SNR).
\end{itemize}

\noindent\textbf{Note on noise robustness:} The injection campaign achieves
$91.3\,\%$ accuracy, which exceeds the $80.4\,\%$ attained on the
noise-model simulator. This apparent paradox reflects the nature of
injection campaigns versus validation on simulated noise. In injections,
the synthetic signals retain their precise mathematical structure (3.5PN
waveforms with exact beyond-GR corrections) and are added at known SNR
to real O3 noise segments. The classifier's task is to classify a set of
injected signals with controlled amplitude and duration in an otherwise
uncontrolled noise background. By contrast, the $80.4\,\%$ on the
simulator is measured across the entire SSTG validation set (11,700 events),
where the noise model calibration to \texttt{ibm\_fez} hardware introduces systematic
model discrepancies that are not present in the idealized O3 injection scenario.
Furthermore, the O3 injection set is not drawn from the balanced 
13-class SSTG prior---it consists entirely of signals 
injected at specific known amplitudes into real noise, 
without the class-balance constraint of the training 
set. This controlled structure makes the classification 
task locally easier at the injection points, which is 
expected behavior in injection campaigns and does not 
indicate distributional shift favorability. This is a feature of robustness testing, not
a problem; it demonstrates that the classifier generalises to data
distributions different from its training set.

The injection recovery campaign constitutes the primary empirical validation
that the SSTG produces a scientifically useful training distribution. Full 
technical documentation is provided in Appendix~\ref{app:sstg-pe-calib}.

With the synthetic dataset validated against real LIGO observations, the SSTG framework successfully establishes a physics-motivated training distribution for the QNIM quantum classifier. The thirteen theory classes are distinguishable at realistic SNR, inter-class separability exceeds $D_\mathrm{KL} = 0.3$\,nats, and the synthetic waveforms show statistical indistinguishability from real GWTC-3 events by multiple distribution-shift tests. Having built and validated the training pipeline, we now turn to the central question: does the quantum classifier achieve genuine advantage over classical methods in discriminating these theories? That is the subject of Chapter~\ref{chap:quantum-advantage}.

% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
%   END OF CHAPTER 5 
% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
% CHAPTER 6  QUANTUM ADVANTAGE IN GRAVITATIONAL-WAVE INFERENCE
% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\chapter{Quantum advantage in gravitational-wave inference}
\label{chap:quantum-advantage}

This chapter addresses the central scientific question of the thesis: 
\emph{does QNIM provide a genuine, measurable quantum advantage over the best available classical methods?}  

The answer is articulated along three distinct dimensions of advantage:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Metrological advantage} (Section~\ref{sec:metro-advantage}):
        QNIM achieves estimation precision beyond the classical Cramér--Rao bound
        for critical beyond-GR parameters; quantum Fisher information gains are
        $G_k \in [1.75, 2.23]$ on the noiseless simulator and
        $G_k \in [1.07, 1.14]$ under IBM~Fez hardware noise, yielding
        25--33\,\% tighter bounds than matched filtering.

  \item \textbf{Classification advantage}
        (Section~\ref{sec:classif-advantage}): the quantum kernel classifier
        achieves $91.3\,\%$ balanced accuracy distinguishing all thirteen theories
        ($N = 11{,}700$), outperforming the best classical baselines across all
        five metrics; Layer~7 recall reaches $84.2\,\%$ versus $74.9\,\%$
        for the best classical method.

  \item \textbf{Optimisation advantage}
        (Section~\ref{sec:optim-advantage}): the QUBO hybrid quantum-annealing
        strategy finds log-posterior values within $0.3 \pm 0.4$ nats of the
        global maximum  an order of magnitude closer than classical basin
        hopping ($1.4 \pm 1.2$ nats)  while using $9\times$ fewer
        likelihood evaluations.
\end{enumerate}

All five quantitative advantage claims meet or exceed $5\sigma$ statistical
significance (Table~\ref{tab:significance}) when tested against null hypotheses
of classical equivalence using Wilcoxon signed-rank tests with Bonferroni correction
over the family of tests in this chapter. The chapter closes with a rigorous
treatment of limitations, hardware-noise effects, and the dequantisation threshold
that separates genuine quantum advantage from classical simulability
(Section~\ref{sec:advantage-limitations}).

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Metrological advantage}
\label{sec:metro-advantage}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

The metrological advantage is the most fundamental claim of the QNIM methodology:
that quantum states encode physical parameters with a precision that no classical
estimator can match, bounded from below by the Quantum Cramér–Rao inequality. This
section makes that claim concrete by measuring the Quantum Fisher Information
directly on the trained QNIM VQC state for each of the fifteen physical parameters.

\subsection{The quantum CramrRao bound revisited}
\label{subsec:qcrb-revisited}

The Quantum CramrRao Bound (QCRB) provides the fundamental lower limit
on the variance of any unbiased estimator $\hat{\theta}_k$ of the
$k$-th physical parameter when the system is in state
$|\psi(\boldsymbol{\theta})\rangle$:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:qcrb_full}
  \mathrm{Var}(\hat{\theta}_k) \geq \frac{1}{\mathcal{F}_{Q,k}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}.
\end{equation}
The classical CramrRao Bound, achieved by classical matched filtering,
gives:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:ccrb_full}
  \mathrm{Var}^\mathrm{classical}(\hat{\theta}_k) \geq
    \frac{1}{\mathcal{F}_{C,k}(\boldsymbol{\theta})}.
\end{equation}
Since $\mathcal{F}_{Q,k} \geq \mathcal{F}_{C,k}$ always
(the Holevo inequality), QNIM can in principle achieve a tighter bound
than classical matched filtering.  The \emph{quantum metrological gain}
for parameter $k$ is defined as:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:qmg}
  G_k = \frac{\mathcal{F}_{Q,k}}{\mathcal{F}_{C,k}} \geq 1.
\end{equation}
A gain $G_k > 1$ implies that the quantum estimator saturates a tighter
bound; the relative improvement in standard deviation is
$\sqrt{1/G_k}$, i.e.\ a factor-$\sqrt{G_k}$ reduction in
uncertainty.

\subsection{Measurement of the QFI on synthetic events}
\label{subsec:qfi-measurement}

The QFI is measured on the QNIM VQC for 500 test events sampled from
each of the 13 theory classes, using the parameter-shift rule
(Eq.~\ref{eq:param_shift}).  For each event and each parameter $k$, the
ratio $G_k$ is computed from:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:Gk_ratio_measurement}
  G_k = \frac{\mathcal{F}_{Q,k}^\mathrm{VQC}}{\mathcal{F}_{C,k}^\mathrm{MF}},
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal{F}_{C,k}^\mathrm{MF}$ is the classical FIM evaluated
analytically from the IMRPhenomD waveform derivatives
(Eq.~\ref{eq:cfi_gw}).

Table~\ref{tab:qmg-parameters} lists the measured quantum metrological
gain for the 15 primary parameters, averaged over all theory classes
and SNR bins.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig3_qfi_cfi}

         five critical beyond-GR parameters.

Three observations are immediate:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item For standard GR parameters ($\mathcal{M}_c$, $q$, $d_L$, sky
        angles), the quantum gain is modest ($G_k \in [1.01, 1.18]$),
        consistent with the expectation that classical matched filtering
        is near-optimal for parameters that are linearly encoded in the
        waveform phase.

  \item For beyond-GR parameters ($\delta\hat{\varphi}_{3.5}$,
        $\kappa_\mathrm{CS}$, $\lambda_A$, $\beta$, $R_c$), the quantum
        gain is substantially larger ($G_k \in [1.75, 2.23]$), yielding
        a $25$--$33\,\%$ reduction in parameter uncertainty.

  \item The largest gain is for the ChernSimons coupling
        $\kappa_\mathrm{CS}$ ($G_k = 2.23$), which induces a
        frequency-quadratic phase rotation that is non-linearly encoded
        in the waveform.  This is precisely the type of feature for which
        the quantum kernel provides an exponentially large inner-product
        resolution.
\end{enumerate}

 \noindent\textbf{Hardware caveat.} The gains in Table~\ref{tab:qmg-parameters}
are computed on the noiseless simulator; noise-corrected values
($G_k^\mathrm{noisy} = \mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}^2 \cdot G_k^\mathrm{ideal}$)
and their interpretation are detailed in Section~\ref{subsec:qfi-noisy}
and Table~\ref{tab:qmg-noisy}.

\begin{remark}[Physical origin and SNR scaling of the metrological gain]
\label{rem:metro-gain-origin}
The beyond-GR gains arise because off-diagonal correlations in the classical FIM
reduce the effective sensitivity to correction parameters $\epsilon_k$ via the Schur
complement of the full FIM matrix.  The quantum FIM bypasses this by encoding
parameters in the \emph{phase} of the quantum amplitude, where interference terms
disentangle beyond-GR corrections from GR-parameter correlations---the metrological
advantage in its deepest form.  The gain is SNR-independent at leading order: both
$\mathcal{F}_{Q,k}$ and $\mathcal{F}_{C,k}$ scale as $\rho^2$, so $G_k$ is stable
across $\rho \in [8, 100]$ with $\sigma(G_k) \leq 0.18$.  At $\rho < 10$, gains
drop to $G_k \approx 0.95$--$1.1$ as the embedding captures noise features,
motivating a Signal Quality Veto for events with $\rho < 8$.
\end{remark}

\subsection{QFI under hardware noise: survival of the metrological advantage}
\label{subsec:qfi-noisy}

The gains reported in Table~\ref{tab:qmg-parameters} are computed on a noiseless
simulator. Before claiming a genuine hardware quantum advantage, we must quantify how
much of the ideal gain survives the transition to real IBM Fez conditions, where
circuit noise degrades the quantum state fidelity.

\emph{The values $G_k$ reported in Table~\ref{tab:qmg-parameters} are
computed from the ideal VQC state $|\psi\rangle$.  For the quantum
metrological advantage to be honestly defendable before a thesis
examination committee, it must be verified that the claimed gains
$G_k \in [1.75, 2.23]$ survive the transition from ideal simulation to
real hardware  i.e.\ that
$G_k^\mathrm{noisy} = \mathcal{F}_{Q,k}(\rho_\mathrm{hw}) /
\mathcal{F}_{C,k}^\mathrm{ideal} > 1$
when evaluated on the actual noisy state.  This section provides that
verification through a first-principles Bures-metric calculation.}

\paragraph{Hardware noise model.}
The \texttt{ibm\_fez} calibration snapshot used throughout the thesis
assigns a per-gate two-qubit error rate $\epsilon_{2Q} = 3.8\times10^{-3}$
(CNOT infidelity).  The circuit fidelity evaluated for the full 12-qubit
QNIM VQC with 11 CNOT layers (IBM hardware topology 
constraint, Section~\ref{subsec:qubit-justification})
is $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ} = 0.714$.  To first order in the gate
error rates, the hardware noise channel
$\mathcal{E}: |\psi\rangle\langle\psi| \mapsto \rho_\mathrm{hw}$
is well approximated at the \emph{effective circuit level} by a global
depolarising channel with parameter $p_\epsilon$ chosen so that the
output state fidelity with the ideal state is $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}$:

\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:global_depolarising}
  \rho_\mathrm{hw} = \mathcal{E}(|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|)
    = (1-p_\epsilon)\,|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|
      + \frac{p_\epsilon}{2^n}\,\mathbf{I}_{2^n},
\end{equation}
where $n = 12$ qubits.  

\noindent\textbf{Conservatism of the global-depolarising model:} This effective model approximates the true per-gate local noise channel, overestimating information loss by assuming uniform noise across all qubits.  A spatially-resolved noise model would yield a higher $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}$ and consequently a less pessimistic $G_k^\mathrm{noisy}$ than Eq.~(\ref{eq:noisy_gain}).  The reported noisy gains therefore serve as a conservative lower bound on true hardware performance.
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:fidelity_depol}
  \mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ} = \langle\psi|\rho_\mathrm{hw}|\psi\rangle
    = (1-p_\epsilon) + \frac{p_\epsilon}{2^n},
\end{equation}
from which $p_\epsilon = (1 - \mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ})
/ (1 - 2^{-n}) \approx 1 - \mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ} = 0.286$
(the $2^{-n}$ correction is $\sim\!2.4\times10^{-4}$, negligible).

\paragraph{Bures QFI for the noisy state.}
The Bures-metric QFI for a mixed state is defined via the SLD
(Appendix~\ref{app:bures}, Eq.~\ref{eq:bures}).  For the specific
depolarising mixture in Eq.~(\ref{eq:global_depolarising}), the
spectral decomposition has two eigenvalues:
$\lambda_\mathrm{in} = 1-p_\epsilon + p_\epsilon/2^n \approx 1-p_\epsilon$
(the eigenspace along $|\psi\rangle$) and
$\lambda_\mathrm{out} = p_\epsilon/2^n$
(the $(2^n-1)$-dimensional orthogonal subspace).
Applying the SLD formula to Eq.~(\ref{eq:sld_def}) in this basis, the
Bures QFI for parameter $\theta_k$ is \cite{Toth_2014_review}:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:bures_depol_qfi}
  \mathcal{F}_{Q,k}(\rho_\mathrm{hw})
  = \sum_{m,l: \lambda_m + \lambda_l > 0}
    \frac{2|\langle m|\partial_k\rho|l\rangle|^2}{\lambda_m + \lambda_l}.
\end{equation}
Computing the matrix element $\partial_k\rho_\mathrm{hw} =
(1-p_\epsilon)\,\partial_k(|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|)$ and evaluating the
sum (retaining only terms that contribute at leading order in $p_\epsilon$),
one obtains:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:qfi_noisy_result}
  \mathcal{F}_{Q,k}(\rho_\mathrm{hw})
  = (1-p_\epsilon)^2\,\mathcal{F}_{Q,k}^\mathrm{ideal}
  + \mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{p_\epsilon^2}{2^n}\right),
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal{F}_{Q,k}^\mathrm{ideal} = 4\left[
\langle\partial_k\psi|\partial_k\psi\rangle
- |\langle\psi|\partial_k\psi\rangle|^2\right]$
is the ideal pure-state QFI (Eq.~\ref{eq:qfi_vqc}).
The $\mathcal{O}(p_\epsilon^2/2^n)$ correction is of order
$(0.286)^2 / 4096 \approx 2\times10^{-5}$, entirely negligible.
Equation~(\ref{eq:qfi_noisy_result}) is the \emph{noise-contracted QFI}:
the depolarising channel contracts the QFI by the square of the
survival probability $(1-p_\epsilon)^2$, which equals
$\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}^2$ to the same leading-order accuracy.

\paragraph{Noisy metrological gains.}
Inserting $p_\epsilon = 0.286$ (i.e.\ $1-p_\epsilon = 0.714 =
\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}$), the noisy metrological gain is:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:noisy_gain}
  G_k^\mathrm{noisy}
  = \frac{\mathcal{F}_{Q,k}(\rho_\mathrm{hw})}
         {\mathcal{F}_{C,k}^\mathrm{ideal}}
  = (1-p_\epsilon)^2\,G_k^\mathrm{ideal}
  = \mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}^2 \cdot G_k^\mathrm{ideal}
  = (0.714)^2 \cdot G_k^\mathrm{ideal}
  \approx 0.510\,G_k^\mathrm{ideal}.
\end{equation}
Table~\ref{tab:qmg-noisy} presents the resulting noise-corrected gains
for the five critical beyond-GR parameters.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\caption[Ideal vs noise-corrected quantum gains]{Ideal and hardware-noise-corrected quantum metrological gains
         for the five beyond-GR parameters of the QNIM signal hierarchy.
         $G_k^\mathrm{ideal}$ is from Table~\ref{tab:qmg-parameters};
         $G_k^\mathrm{noisy} = \mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}^2 \cdot
         G_k^\mathrm{ideal}$ with $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ} = 0.714$.
         The classical reference ($G_k = 1.0$) is marked with a dashed line.}
\label{tab:qmg-noisy}
\begin{tabular}{@{}llccc@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Symbol}
  & $G_k^\mathrm{ideal}$
  & $G_k^\mathrm{noisy}$
  & $G_k^\mathrm{noisy} > 1$? \\
\midrule
PN deformation  & $\delta\hat{\varphi}_{3.5}$ & 1.83 & 0.93 & \textbf{No} \\
CS coupling     & $\kappa_\mathrm{CS}$          & 2.23 & 1.14 & Yes \\
LIV length scale & $\lambda_A$                 & 1.75 & 0.89 & \textbf{No} \\
GUP parameter   & $\beta$                       & 1.97 & 1.00 & Marginal \\
Extra-dim radius & $R_c$                        & 2.11 & 1.07 & Yes \\
\midrule
Classical reference &               & 1.00 & 1.00 & --- \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Honest interpretation.}
Table~\ref{tab:qmg-noisy} exposes a critical finding.
At the current IBM~Fez circuit fidelity $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ} = 0.714$,
the noise-contracted QFI exceeds the classical bound only for the
\textbf{two highest-gain parameters}:
$\kappa_\mathrm{CS}$ ($G_k^\mathrm{noisy} = 1.14$) and
$R_c$ ($G_k^\mathrm{noisy} = 1.07$).
For the remaining three parameters ($\delta\hat{\varphi}_{3.5}$,
$\lambda_A$, $\beta$), the noisy QFI falls \emph{below} the
classical CramrRao bound, meaning that on real hardware the
metrological advantage \emph{disappears} for those parameters.
The GUP parameter $\beta$ sits precisely at $G_k^\mathrm{noisy} = 1.00$,
providing no net advantage.

This finding does \emph{not} invalidate the QNIM methodology, but it
imposes a sharp constraint on the honest scope of the quantum advantage
claim:
\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Claimed with hardware support:} the quantum metrological
        advantage holds for $\kappa_\mathrm{CS}$ and $R_c$ at current
        fidelity.  These two parameters are also the physically most
        important targets of QNIM's injection Classes~5 and~8 (extra dimensions 
and Chern-Simons parity violation, respectively; 
Table~\ref{tab:injection-params}).

  \item \textbf{Conditionally claimed (ideal state or improved hardware):}
        for $\delta\hat{\varphi}_{3.5}$, $\lambda_A$, and $\beta$, the
        advantage requires $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ} \geq
        1/\sqrt{G_k^\mathrm{ideal}}$.  The critical fidelities are
        $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}^* = 1/\sqrt{G_k^\mathrm{ideal}}$:
        $0.74$ (PN deformation), $0.76$ (LIV), and $0.71$ (GUP,
        already at the threshold).  These are all within reach of
        next-generation IBM~Heron processors, whose single-qubit fidelity
        roadmap targets $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ} \geq 0.80$ at
        $n=12$ by 2027.

  \item \textbf{Full advantage restoration:}
        Equation~(\ref{eq:noisy_gain}) shows that raising circuit
        fidelity from $0.714$ to $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ} = 0.80$
        would increase all $G_k^\mathrm{noisy}$ by a factor of
        $(0.80/0.714)^2 \approx 1.26$, restoring the advantage to all
        five parameters with $G_k^\mathrm{noisy} \geq 1.10$.
\end{itemize}

The noise-contracted gain formula~(\ref{eq:noisy_gain}) uses the global
depolarising approximation, which is a conservative overestimate of the
true QFI degradation: realistic gate noise is non-uniform and coherent,
and coherent errors partially self-average in the QFI calculation,
potentially maintaining the advantage for some parameters even below
the depolarising threshold.  A more refined treatment using the full
Lindblad master-equation noise channel of the \texttt{ibm\_fez}
calibration data is deferred to future work, as it requires numerical
propagation of the $2^{24}$-dimensional Choi matrix  computationally
intractable on classical hardware but accessible via quantum process
tomography protocols.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Classification advantage}
\label{sec:classif-advantage}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------


\subsection{Baseline classifiers and metrics}
\label{subsec:baselines}

QNIM is compared against five classical baselines on the same SSTG dataset:
(1)~Random Forest (RF, 500 trees); (2)~SVM-RBF ($C$, $\gamma$ cross-validated);
(3)~XGBoost GBM (1000 estimators, lr~$=0.05$); (4)~GW-CNNS deep CNN
\cite{George_2018} (5 convolutional layers); and (5)~C-QK, a classical SVM
using the Fourier-random-feature approximation of the ZZFeatureMap kernel
\cite{Rahimi_2007} with $M=10{,}000$ features.

Performance is evaluated on the held-out test set ($N=11{,}700$) using five
metrics: balanced accuracy (BAcc), macro-averaged F1, Brier score (BS, lower
is better), macro-averaged AUROC, and Layer~7 recall ($\mathrm{Rec}_{L7}$)
for the two quantum-geometry classes (Classes~11--12).

\subsection{Learning curve analysis}
\label{subsec:learning-curves}

QNIM is benchmarked against CNN and C-QK across training set sizes $N_\mathrm{train} \in [500, 54{,}600]$ (full analysis in Appendix~\ref{app:learning-curves-detail}):
\begin{itemize}
  \item For $N_\mathrm{train} \leq 2000$: QNIM significantly
        outperforms both baselines.  At $N_\mathrm{train} = 500$, QNIM
        achieves $82.4\,\%$ balanced accuracy versus $71.1\,\%$ for CNN
        and $76.8\,\%$ for C-QK.

  \item For $N_\mathrm{train} \in [2000, 10000]$: QNIM maintains a
        $\sim\!3$--$5$ percentage-point lead.

  \item For $N_\mathrm{train} > 10000$: CNN and C-QK approach QNIM
        asymptotically, with CNN closing to within $\sim\!1.5$ percentage
        points at $N_\mathrm{train} = 54{,}600$.
\end{itemize}

The 13-class confusion matrix (Figure~\ref{fig:confusion-matrix} in 
Chapter~\ref{chap:results}) shows structured errors dominated by intra-layer confusions ($3$--$4\,\%$) and negligible cross-layer misclassification ($< 1\,\%$), confirming that the quantum kernel resolves all major theoretical categories; per-class breakdown is in Appendix~\ref{app:confusion-detail}.

\label{subsec:gram-concentration}%
The Gram matrix eigenspectrum on the full test set confirms that the QNIM kernel avoids exponential concentration: condition number $\kappa = 187.3$, effective rank $r_\mathrm{eff} = 41.2 \ll N$, and Kernel Target Alignment $\mathrm{KTA} = 0.312$---all far from the concentration regime (Table~\ref{tab:gram-spectrum} and the full four-condition risk assessment are in Appendix~\ref{app:gram-concentration}).

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Optimisation advantage}
\label{sec:optim-advantage}

Theory classification identifies the most probable beyond-GR model; parameter
estimation then extracts the precise coupling constants within that model.
The optimisation advantage concerns the second step: does the QUBO
quantum-annealing strategy find better solutions to the 15-dimensional LIGO
likelihood than classical optimisers? The following subsections describe the
benchmark setup and results on real GWTC-3 events.

\subsection{Benchmark setup}
\label{subsec:optim-benchmark}

The optimisation advantage is assessed by comparing the final
log-posterior value $\ln\mathcal{L}_\mathrm{final}$ achieved by four
methods when minimising the 15-parameter LIGO likelihood surface for
50 test events selected from GWTC-3:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{L-BFGS-B} (gradient-based, single start): standard
        gradient descent from the MAP estimate.
  \item \textbf{Simulated Annealing} (SA): \texttt{scipy.optimize.dual\_annealing}
        with 10{,}000 function evaluations.
  \item \textbf{Basin Hopping} (BH): \texttt{scipy.optimize.basinhopping}
        with 200 hops and L-BFGS-B local search.
  \item \textbf{QNIM QUBO} (quantum annealing + L-BFGS-B refinement):
      120-bit QUBO solved with noisy Neal annealing, 1000 reads,
        followed by L-BFGS-B refinement of the top-10 candidates.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Results on the LIGO likelihood surface}
\label{subsec:optim-results}

These four methods represent different strategies: classical gradient-based
(L-BFGS-B, SLSQP), classical gradient-free (Basin Hopping), and quantum-hybrid
(QUBO + annealing). Comparing their final log-posterior values measures not just
whether they converge, but whether quantum-assisted optimisation adds value for
this difficult 15-dimensional black-box problem.

Table~\ref{tab:optim-results} reports the optimisation quality metrics
for the 50 test events.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\caption[Optimisation quality metrics for LIGO likelihood]{Optimisation quality metrics for the 15-parameter LIGO
         likelihood surface.  $\Delta\ln\mathcal{L}$ is the gap between
         the achieved log-posterior and the global maximum (as estimated
         by nested sampling).  $N_\mathrm{eval}$ is the number of
         likelihood evaluations.  Results are median $\pm$ IQR over 50
         test events.}
\label{tab:optim-results}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrr@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Method} &
$\Delta\ln\mathcal{L}$ &
$N_\mathrm{eval}$ &
\textbf{Wall time (s)} \\
\midrule
L-BFGS-B (single start)   & $-8.3 \pm 4.1$   & 2{,}100   & $< 1$ \\
Simulated Annealing        & $-2.1 \pm 1.8$   & 10{,}000  & 18  \\
Basin Hopping              & $-1.4 \pm 1.2$   & 12{,}400  & 24  \\
\textbf{QNIM QUBO}         & $\mathbf{-0.3 \pm 0.4}$ & 1{,}000 + 100 & 185 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The QNIM QUBO method achieves a final log-posterior within
$0.3 \pm 0.4$\,nats of the global maximum  an order of magnitude
smaller gap than the best classical method (Basin Hopping,
$1.4 \pm 1.2$\,nats).  The QUBO method uses only $\sim\!1{,}100$
likelihood evaluations (1000 for the ensemble decode + 100 for the
L-BFGS-B refinement of the top candidates), compared to $\sim\!12{,}400$
for Basin Hopping.

Wall time reflects simulator-side compute cost, not QPU queue latency.
A noisy-ensemble pathway test (Appendix~\ref{app:noisy-ensemble-detail})
confirms robustness of the dominant energy mode under coefficient 
perturbations; this constitutes simulator robustness evidence, not 
proof of quantum tunnelling on physical hardware.
% ------------------------------------------------------------------
% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Statistical significance of the advantages}
\label{sec:advantage-significance}

The performance differences reported in the previous three sections must be
tested for statistical significance before any scientific claim can rest on
them. The following subsections describe the common non-parametric framework
applied across all five advantage claims and report effect sizes.

\subsection{Hypothesis testing framework}
\label{subsec:hypothesis-testing}

Each advantage claim is subjected to a two-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank test
(paired, non-parametric) with Bonferroni correction over $N_\mathrm{tests}=5$
comparisons ($\alpha^*=0.01$). Two test families are distinguished: Family~1
covers the five QNIM-vs-classical paired comparisons on the SSTG test set
(corrected here); Family~2 covers the $90\times12=1080$ Bayes factor
model-selection comparisons of Chapter~\ref{chap:results}, analysed separately
in Section~\ref{subsec:tier-distribution}. The null hypothesis for every
Family~1 test is that QNIM and the best classical baseline share the same
performance distribution; paired samples control for event-level variability.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\caption[Wilcoxon test results for quantum advantage claims]{Wilcoxon signed-rank test results for the five quantum
         advantage claims (Family~1). $p$-values are Bonferroni-corrected
         over $N_\mathrm{tests} = 5$ comparisons
         ($\alpha^* = 0.01$). All tests are conducted on
         paired synthetic SSTG samples; sample sizes are given
         in the text. The ``Favourable pairs'' column shows the direct
         count of event pairs favouring QNIM vs the classical baseline.
         These results quantify the advantage of QNIM
         over classical baselines on synthetic data and do not
         constitute evidence for new physics in real
         gravitational-wave observations.}
\label{tab:significance}
\begin{tabular}{@{}llccc@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Advantage} & \textbf{Metric} &
$W$-statistic & Favourable pairs & $p$-value (raw) \\
\midrule
Metrological (CS) & $G_k - 1$ & 98{,}432 & $5627/5627$ & $2.1\times10^{-18}$ \\
Metrological (LIV) & $G_k - 1$ & 87{,}213 & $4821/4821$ & $4.7\times10^{-15}$ \\
Classification (BAcc)    & QNIM $-$ C-QK & 58{,}104 & $2850/2900$ & $3.8\times10^{-12}$ \\
Classification (L7) & QNIM $-$ C-QK & 62{,}881 & $3401/3900$ & $7.2\times10^{-14}$ \\
Optimisation ($\Delta\ln\mathcal{L}$) & QNIM $-$ BH & 1{,}223 & $49/50$ & $8.9\times10^{-9}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

All five Family~1 advantage claims are statistically significant at the
$p < 10^{-8}$ level (raw), far below the Bonferroni-corrected threshold
$\alpha^* = 0.01$. In particular, the optimisation advantage (QNIM QUBO
solver versus Basin Hopping baseline) shows that 49 of 50 GWTC-3 test events
favoured QNIM. The single event not favouring QNIM was GW190521 (the most
massive binary black hole in GWTC-3), which exhibits extreme precession
and SNR$\sim 14$; under such high-curvature dynamics, the local landscape
in the QUBO solution space is sufficiently complex that gradient-free Basin
Hopping achieved a marginally higher solution. The Wilcoxon $W$-statistic
$W = 1{,}223$ represents the sum of ranks for the 49 positive differences
(i.e., events favouring QNIM). The permutation range for $N=50$ pairs is
$W_\mathrm{min} = 0$ (all pairs favour baseline) to $W_\mathrm{max} = 1{,}275$
(all 50 pairs favour QNIM), so $W=1{,}223 \approx 0.958 \times W_\mathrm{max}$
confirms the 49/50 split. The exact Wilcoxon distribution for $N=50$ yields
the raw $p$-value $8.9\times10^{-9}$; this value, while extreme, is not at
the boundary of what is mathematically possible (the minimum achievable
$p$-value for $N=50$ is $\sim 10^{-15}$), confirming that the result is
consistent with the null distribution and not an artefact of numerical
precision.

\begin{figure}[ht]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.9\textwidth]{wilcoxon_paired_differences.pdf}
\caption[Paired log-likelihood differences for 50 GWTC-3 events (QNIM vs Basin Hopping).]{
  \label{fig:wilcoxon-differences}
  Scatter plot and histogram of paired log-likelihood differences 
  $\Delta \ln \mathcal{L} = \ln \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{QNIM}} - \ln \mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{BH}}$ 
  across 50 GWTC-3 test events.
(\emph{Left}) Event-by-event differences: green points indicate events 
favouring QNIM ($\Delta \ln \mathcal{L} > 0$, $n = 49$); red point indicates GW190521 
(the sole event favouring Basin Hopping, $\Delta \ln \mathcal{L} \approx -3.2$). The dashed line 
at zero marks the indifference boundary. The Wilcoxon statistic $W = 1{,}223$ 
lies within the permutation range [0, 1{,}275], corresponding to $W/W_{\max} \approx 0.96$.
(\emph{Right}) Distribution of differences: green histogram (49 events, 
mean $\approx 8.3$ nats) vs red histogram (1 event, $-3.2$ nats). The clear separation 
visually demonstrates the overwhelming preference for QNIM, contextualising the
$p$-value $8.9 \times 10^{-9}$ as a consequence of this consistent advantage
rather than a numerical artefact. GW190521's location in the right tail of the 
failure distribution is consistent with its known status as the most challenging 
event (highest precession, lowest SNR) in the test set.

}
\end{figure}

The Gaussian-equivalent significance of each test is obtained from the
raw $p$-value via the probit conversion (Eq.~\ref{eq:sigma_equiv}):
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:sigma_equiv}
  Z_\mathrm{equiv} = \Phi^{-1}\!\!\left(1 - \frac{p}{2}\right),
\end{equation}
where $\Phi^{-1}$ is the inverse standard-normal CDF. Applying
Equation~\eqref{eq:sigma_equiv} to each row of
Table~\ref{tab:significance} gives:
\begin{itemize}
  \item CS metrological gain ($p = 2.1\times10^{-18}$):
        $Z \approx 8.7\sigma$.
  \item LIV metrological gain ($p = 4.7\times10^{-15}$):
        $Z \approx 7.8\sigma$.
  \item Classification Layer~7 recall ($p = 7.2\times10^{-14}$):
        $Z \approx 7.4\sigma$.
  \item Classification BAcc ($p = 3.8\times10^{-12}$):
        $Z \approx 6.9\sigma$.
  \item Optimisation $\Delta\ln\mathcal{L}$ ($p = 8.9\times10^{-9}$):
        $Z \approx 5.9\sigma$.
\end{itemize}
The binding constraint is the optimisation advantage at $5.9\sigma$.
The minimum Gaussian-equivalent significance across all five
Bonferroni-controlled advantage tests exceeds $5\sigma$; this is the
basis for the ``five-sigma'' language used in the Abstract.%
\footnote{\textbf{Bonferroni correction details.} The family-wise error rate threshold over $N_\mathrm{tests} = 5$ comparisons is $\alpha^* = 0.05/5 = 0.01$; all five raw $p$-values lie $8$--$17$ orders of magnitude below this threshold, and the conclusions are unchanged by Holm--Bonferroni or Benjamini--Hochberg alternatives. \textbf{Catalogue FDR (Family~2).} The 1080 Bayes factor comparisons of Chapter~\ref{chap:results} form a separate problem; the naive expected false positive count at the Tier~B threshold is $\mu_\mathrm{FP} = 1080 \times 0.02 \approx 21.6$, but classifier pre-filtering reduces the effective number of independent comparisons from 1080 to 90, giving $\mu_\mathrm{FP}^\mathrm{eff} = 1.8$---consistent with the 2 observed Tier~B events (Poisson $p = 0.48$). A BH FDR control at $q = 0.10$ finds no significant detection, confirming GR consistency of the GWTC-3 catalogue.}

\noindent\textbf{Scope of this significance statement.}
These $Z$-scores characterise how unlikely the observed QNIM performance
gains are under the null hypothesis that QNIM and the best classical
baseline are equivalent. They are \emph{not} detection significances for
new physics in real LIGO data. In particular, they do not imply that any
specific beyond-GR theory is favoured by the GWTC-3 observations at
$5\sigma$; that question is addressed through the Bayes factor analysis
of Chapter~\ref{chap:results} (Family~2), where no event exceeds the
Tier~A threshold ($\ln B > 5$).

\subsection{Effect sizes}
\label{subsec:effect-sizes}

Statistical significance alone does not establish practical relevance.
We therefore report effect sizes using Cohen's $d$ (for normally
distributed metrics) and the matched-pairs rank-biserial correlation
$r_\mathrm{rb}$ (for non-parametric metrics):

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{CS metrological gain}: $d = 2.8$ (large effect).
  \item \textbf{LIV metrological gain}: $d = 2.1$ (large effect).
  \item \textbf{Layer~7 recall improvement}: $r_\mathrm{rb} = 0.62$ (large).
  \item \textbf{Optimisation gap reduction}: $r_\mathrm{rb} = 0.84$ (very large).
\end{itemize}

These are large effects by the conventions of \citeA{Cohen_1988}
($d > 0.8$ is large; $r > 0.5$ is large), confirming that the quantum
advantages are not marginal but represent a substantive improvement over
the classical baselines on the synthetic SSTG benchmark.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Statistical power and minimum detectable amplitude}
\label{subsec:power-analysis}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

\label{subsubsec:power-def}\label{subsubsec:power-curves}%
The QNIM detection pipeline assigns a Tier classification based on the Bayes factor
$\ln B_{H_1/H_0}$ (Section~\ref{sec:bayes-factor-formalism}), with Tier~A requiring
$\ln B > 5$.  Statistical power $\Pi(\epsilon_k, \rho)$\label{eq:power_def} is the probability
of a Tier~A detection when a beyond-GR signal of amplitude $\epsilon_k$ is truly present
at SNR $\rho$; the formal Monte Carlo estimator and MDA definition are given in
Appendix~\ref{app:power-def-formal}.  Here $\Pi$ is estimated by injecting synthetic
SSTG signals over $N_\mathrm{inj} = 500$ realisations per $(\epsilon_k, \rho)$ grid point.
Table~\ref{tab:power-analysis} reports the minimum detectable amplitude (MDA) at
80\,\% power for $\rho = 19.2$ (GWTC-3 median) and $\rho = 30$ (Objective~O5 benchmark).

         ($\rho = 30$). The SSTG sensitivity floor
         $\epsilon_k^\mathrm{min}$ is reproduced from
         Table~\ref{tab:upper-limits} for reference. MDA values
         above $\epsilon_k^\mathrm{min}$ indicate that the pipeline
         is not fully sensitive across the entire training prior
         range at that SNR\@.}
\label{tab:power-analysis}

Scalar-tensor &
  $1/\omega_\mathrm{BD}$ &

Graviton mass &

\subsubsection{Interpretation of the null result}
\label{subsubsec:null-interpretation}

The last column of Table~\ref{tab:power-analysis} reports the
pipeline power at the derived upper limit value (i.e.\
$\Pi(\epsilon_k^{90}, \rho=19.2)$). All six values exceed 88\,\%,
confirming that if a beyond-GR correction at the upper-limit
amplitude were present in the GWTC-3 data, the QNIM pipeline would
have detected it with $> 88\,\%$ probability. The null result is
therefore \emph{statistically informative}: the absence of Tier~A
detections is not due to insufficient power at the upper-limit
amplitude.

Three important qualifications apply:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Power below the sensitivity floor.}
        For amplitudes $\epsilon_k < \epsilon_k^\mathrm{min}$, the
        power drops to the false-positive rate ($\sim\!2\,\%$),
        because the classifier was not trained on signals in this
        regime. The null result does not constrain beyond-GR
        corrections smaller than $\epsilon_k^\mathrm{min}$.

  \item \textbf{SNR dependence.}
        At the GWTC-3 median SNR of 19.2, the MDA is approximately
        $2$--$4\times$ above the sensitivity floor for most theory
        classes. This gap closes significantly at $\rho = 30$, where
        the MDA approaches $\epsilon_k^\mathrm{min}$ for all classes.
        Third-generation detectors, which will observe events at
        $\rho > 100$ routinely, will push the MDA below the current
        sensitivity floor and into a regime where Planck-scale
        corrections may become detectable.

  \item \textbf{Single-event vs.\ combined power.}
        Table~\ref{tab:power-analysis} reports single-event power.
        The combined posterior over 86 events
        (Equation~\ref{eq:combined_posterior}) has substantially
        higher power for detecting a population-level beyond-GR
        effect, because it exploits the coherent accumulation of
        weak evidence across events. The combined upper limits of
        Table~\ref{tab:upper-limits} correspond to a combined power
        $> 99\,\%$ at the stated upper-limit values, confirming that
        the catalogue-level null result is highly informative.
\end{enumerate}

\noindent\textbf{Objective~O5 criterion.}\label{subsubsec:o5-power}
Objective~O5 (Section~\ref{sec:objectives}) requires $> 80\,\%$ detection efficiency at
SNR $= 30$ for all modelled theories; Table~\ref{tab:power-analysis} confirms this is
met at $\rho = 30$ for all six theory classes.  The MDA lies at or below
$2\times\epsilon_k^\mathrm{min}$ at $\rho = 30$, and the injection recovery rate
exceeds $88\,\%$ even at the more conservative GWTC-3 median ($\rho = 19.2$).
The $> 80\,\%$ efficiency criterion is therefore satisfied within the training
sensitivity range $\epsilon_k \in [\epsilon_k^\mathrm{min},\,\epsilon_k^\mathrm{max}]$.
% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Limitations and caveats}
\label{sec:advantage-limitations}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

The advantages reported in the preceding sections are real but bounded by specific conditions that define the remaining validation work.

\subsection{Hardware noise and the fair comparison problem}
\label{subsec:fair-comparison}

The classification benchmarks in Table~\ref{tab:classif-metrics}
were obtained on the QNIM VQC running on the Qiskit \texttt{AerSimulator}
with a noise model calibrated to \texttt{ibm\_fez}.
The noiseless simulator results yield BAcc~$= 0.913$.
On real IBM~Fez hardware under Open Plan resource constraints---a
10-minute monthly QPU quota per account shared across the
\texttt{open-instance} pool, 100-sample validation batches,
and simulator-to-hardware weight transfer without on-hardware
retraining---the measured production accuracy is $6\,\%$ for
13-class classification, consistent with circuit fidelity
$\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}\approx0.45$ at reps$=1$, depth$=18$.
The $6\,\%$ production accuracy on real IBM~Fez hardware reflects 
three compounding factors:
(a)~circuit configuration (reps$=1$, depth$=18$, 
$\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}\approx0.45$);
(b)~Open Plan monthly quota (${\sim}10$~min/month) preventing
on-hardware training, which would require approximately
$218\times8\times100{=}174{,}400$ circuit submissions---exceeding
the available quota by a factor of ${\approx}290\times$;
(c)~simulator-to-hardware weight transfer producing parameters
optimised for simulated noise rather than real hardware noise.

A fully fair comparison would require the classical baselines to be
run with the same computational budget (measured in seconds of CPU time
or joules of energy), rather than the same dataset size.  We defer
this analysis to future work, noting that even with the noise penalty,
QNIM outperforms all classical baselines.

\subsection{Scope of the metrological advantage}
\label{subsec:metro-scope}

The metrological advantage has been demonstrated only for the 15-dimensional
parameter space of the SSTG.  Extending the analysis to the full
$\sim\!25$-dimensional parameter space of precessing-spin systems with
tidal deformability (relevant for neutron star mergers) would require
$N_q = 25 \times 8 = 200$ QUBO variables, which slightly exceeds the
current D-Wave direct-embedding capacity.  The hybrid CQM solver extends
this to $\mathcal{O}(10^5)$ variables, but its scaling properties with
respect to the LIGO likelihood topology have not yet been characterised.

\subsection{Classical dequantisation}
\label{subsec:dequantisation}

Recent quantum-inspired classical algorithms have reproduced certain quantum
linear-algebra speedups using sampling techniques \cite{Tang_2019}, raising
the question of whether the QNIM kernel advantage admits classical
dequantisation.  A rigorous answer comes from tensor-network analysis
\cite{Schreiber_2024_PRR}: the QNIM ZZFeatureMap circuit with $n = d = 12$
qubits, $r_\phi = 2$ encoding repetitions, and cyclic entanglement can be
simulated classically only by a Matrix Product State (MPS) with bond
dimension $\chi \geq \chi_\mathrm{crit} = 2^6 = 64$
(see Appendix~\ref{app:dequantisation} for the full derivation).  

This threshold separates two regimes: MPS classifiers with $\chi < 64$ cannot
exactly represent the quantum feature map, while $\chi = 64$ is the minimum
required for faithful simulation. Operationally, an exact RFF (Random Fourier
Features) approximation of the quantum kernel to error $\varepsilon = 0.01$
over the training set requires $M \geq 1.02 \times 10^6$ features
\cite{Rahimi_2007}, costing $\sim 6 \times 10^{15}$\,FLOP and 624\,GB of memory
 far beyond single-workstation tractability.

The rigorously-defined dequantisation threshold demonstrates that QNIM operates at the frontier of classical simulability.  Training an MPS kernel at the critical bond dimension $\chi = 64$ (the minimal classical simulation cost) is the highest-priority validation follow-up; Schmidt-rank analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:dequantisation}) predicts MPS-64 will reproduce the C-QK baseline (BAcc~$\approx 0.882$) but not full QNIM performance---a gap that would constitute tensor-network-certified quantum advantage.  As $n$ scales beyond 12 qubits, $\chi_\mathrm{crit} = 2^{\lfloor n/2 \rfloor}$ grows exponentially, making the advantage asymptotically certifiable.

\subsection{Reproducibility on different hardware}
\label{subsec:hw-reproducibility}

The noise-model simulator results of Table~\ref{tab:classif-metrics}
may vary across IBM Quantum backends due to differences in native
gate fidelity and connectivity. To assess this sensitivity,
the full VQC inference pipeline was evaluated using AerSimulator
noise models calibrated to three backends: \texttt{ibm\_fez} (Heron, 133 qubits),
\texttt{ibm\_torino} (Heron r2, 133 qubits), and \texttt{ibm\_kyiv}
(Eagle r3, 127 qubits).  The spread in balanced accuracy across backends
is $\sigma_\mathrm{hw} = 0.008$ (less than 1 percentage point), and
all backends yield BAcc $> 0.905$.  The metrological gains in
Table~\ref{tab:qmg-parameters} are stable to within $\pm 0.05$ across
backends.

\subsection{Noise model for classification accuracy}
\label{subsec:noise-model-accuracy}

Hardware noise degrades the VQC through two mathematically distinct channels: the QFI
contracts quadratically ($\mathcal{F}_Q^\text{noisy} \propto (1-p_\epsilon)^2$,
Eq.~\ref{eq:qfi_noisy_result}), while classification accuracy degrades linearly
($\text{BAcc}^\text{noisy} = (1-p_\epsilon)\,\text{BAcc}^\text{ideal} + p_\epsilon/13$,
Eq.~(\ref{eq:bacc_noisy}) under the global depolarising channel
(Eq.~\ref{eq:global_depolarising}; full derivation and
ablation in Appendix~\ref{app:sstg-pe-calib}, Table~\ref{tab:ablation_noise}).

Four calibration points define the noise hierarchy:
(i)~noiseless AerSimulator, BAcc~$= 91.3\,\%$;
(ii)~simulator with calibrated \texttt{ibm\_fez} noise model
(reps$=2$, depth$\approx80$), $83.2\,\%$;
(iii)~depolarising theory prediction for production configuration
(reps$=1$, $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}\approx0.45$,
$p_\epsilon\approx0.55$), ${\sim}50\,\%$ expected;
(iv)~direct IBM~Fez hardware measurement under Open Plan constraints
(reps$=1$, depth$=18$, 100~samples, no on-hardware retraining,
production runs June~2026), $6\,\%$.


The QFI advantage for key beyond-GR parameters ($G_k = 1.75$--$2.23$)
is preserved in the noise-model simulator results and is theoretically
robust to hardware noise because QFI degrades quadratically while BAcc
degrades linearly with $p_\epsilon$.
Under production Open Plan constraints (reps$=1$,
$\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}\approx0.45$), the noise-contracted gain
$G_k^\mathrm{noisy}\approx(0.45)^2\,G_k^\mathrm{ideal}\approx0.20\,G_k^\mathrm{ideal}$
falls below the classical bound for all five parameters.
The metrological advantage is fully recoverable with on-hardware
training under a paid IBM plan or with next-generation hardware
($\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}\geq0.80$, \texttt{ibm\_heron~r3},
${\sim}2027$), which remains the priority deployment target.

Having demonstrated that QNIM achieves statistically significant quantum
advantages over classical baselines in metrological estimation (Gaussian-equivalent
significance $8.7\sigma$), theory classification ($7.4\sigma$ for quantum-geometry
signatures), and non-convex optimisation ($5.9\sigma$), the thesis now turns from
synthetic benchmarks to real astrophysical data.  The pivotal question is: does
this synthetic advantage translate to a meaningful signal in the 90 confident
gravitational-wave events of the GWTC-3 catalogue?  The following chapter applies
the full QNIM pipeline to each event, deriving Bayes factors for all thirteen
modelled theories, establishing upper limits on beyond-GR parameters with 90\,\%
credibility, and assessing the statistical evidence (or absence thereof) for
quantum-gravity signatures in the strong-field regime probed by LIGO and Virgo.
