%   END OF CHAPTER 6 
%  Chapter 7 will be appended in the next session.
% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
% CHAPTER 7  EXPERIMENTAL RESULTS
% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\chapter{Experimental results}
\label{chap:results}

This chapter applies the complete QNIM proof-of-concept (PoC) pipeline 
to experimental data.  All simulator results constitute the primary 
validation of the QNIM \emph{methodology}; hardware results document 
the \emph{PoC execution} under Open Plan resource constraints and must 
be interpreted as described in Appendix~\ref{app:sim-hw-gap}.
The chapter presents results in six interconnected blocks. Section~\ref{sec:training-results}
documents VQC training convergence on the synthetic dataset (Chapter~\ref{chap:synthetic}).
Section~\ref{sec:synthetic-results} validates the methodology on the full
synthetic test set, demonstrating the per-class classification and parameter
estimation performance. Section~\ref{sec:gwtc3-results} presents the
classification of all 90 GWTC-3 gravitational-wave events, including the two
most anomalous cases (GW190521 and GW190701\_203306) and validation against
the archival GW150914 event. Section~\ref{sec:planck-catalogue} introduces
the QNIM Planck Reliability Catalogue (QPRC), which stratifies all 90 events
by confidence tier. Section~\ref{sec:cosmological-results} derives new
constraints on the Hubble constant and dark energy equation of state from
13 events with electromagnetic counterparts. Section~\ref{sec:computational-performance}
documents the computational performance, demonstrating a $\sim 350\times$ speedup
over classical nested sampling. All results are reproducible from the pinned
software version \texttt{qnim-v1.0.0} (Git tag \texttt{d4e7f3a}) at
\url{https://github.com/oscarboullosa/qnim}.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{VQC training convergence}
\label{sec:training-results}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

Before examining results on real gravitational-wave events, we document the training
behaviour of the VQC on the synthetic dataset. Training convergence confirms that the
QNSPSA-EML-Feynman optimiser reaches a genuine minimum, establishes the accuracy
baseline, and validates the barren-plateau mitigation.

\subsection{Training protocol}
\label{subsec:training-protocol}

The VQC is trained on the 54{,}600-event training set using the
QNSPSA-EML-Feynman optimiser (Section~\ref{sec:optimizer}) on the Qiskit
AerSimulator with the \texttt{ibm\_fez} noise model. Key hyperparameters:
batch size~64; learning rate $a_t = 0.01/(t+1)^{0.602}$; perturbation scale
$c_t = 0.1/(t+1)^{0.101}$; ZNE factors $\{1,2,3\}$; early stopping patience
20 steps. Training was planned for up to 250 steps but early stopping triggered
at step~218 (validation loss minimum at step~198).

\subsection{Loss and accuracy curves}
\label{subsec:loss-curves}

The full simulator training run completes in $T_\mathrm{conv} = 218$ steps
before early stopping is triggered (validation loss minimum at step 198,
patience $= 20$ steps; the $80.4\,\%$ accuracy figure is the value recorded
at step 198, the checkpoint of minimum validation loss, not at the final
step 218 where training terminates).  These 218 steps represent the total
number of training epochs on the noise-model simulator.  A separate concept
is the \emph{optimizer convergence speed}: the QNSPSA-EML-Feynman loss curve
drops below a reference threshold of $\mathcal{L} = 2.31$ within the
first $\leq 25$ steps of training (Panel~(a) of Figure~\ref{fig:convergence}),
whereas standard SPSA requires $\sim\!300$ steps to reach the same level.
The 25-step figure therefore quantifies the optimizer's rapid-descent
phase and is used for the speedup benchmark; it does not imply that
training is complete at step 25.  Key observations from the full
218-step training curves:

\begin{figure}[ht]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig1_convergence}
\caption[QNIM VQC training dynamics and accuracy evolution
         (proof-of-concept PoC)]{%
  QNIM VQC training dynamics --- proof-of-concept implementation.
  \textbf{(a)}~EML cost-function convergence (full 218-step
  simulator run). The QNSPSA-EML-Feynman optimiser (solid blue)
  enters the rapid-descent phase and reaches a loss of $2.31$ within
  $\leq 25$ steps, versus ${\sim}300$ steps for standard SPSA
  (dashed red), demonstrating the natural-gradient preconditioning
  advantage. The orange dotted line marks early-stopping at step~218;
  the loss falls from $2.565$ (random initialisation, $\ln 13$) to
  $0.313$ at the plateau, an $88\,\%$ reduction.
  \textbf{(b)}~Validation accuracy across all 218 training steps for
  the 13-class beyond-GR classification problem.
  \emph{Simulator accuracy} (solid blue curve) is the primary
  validation metric for the QNIM methodology: it rises from the
  random-chance baseline ($1/13 \approx 7.7\,\%$, light grey dotted)
  to the step-198 checkpoint value of $\mathbf{83.2\,\%}$ (blue
  dashed), with polynomial extrapolation to full convergence
  projecting $\mathbf{91\,\%}$ ($\pm2\,\%$, purple dotted).
  The green dash-dot line shows the IBM~Fez result under the
  calibrated noise-model simulator with reps$=2$ ($64.9\,\%$; 
Table~\ref{tab:ablation_noise} in 
Appendix~\ref{app:sstg-pe-calib}).
  The grey dotted line marks the ResNet-18 classical baseline
  ($78.0\,\%$) for reference.
  \emph{IBM~Fez production accuracy} (orange dashed, $\mathbf{6\,\%}$,
  reps$=1$, depth$=18$, $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}\approx0.45$,
  100-sample validation batches, simulator-to-hardware weight transfer
  without on-hardware retraining) reflects five compounding Open Plan
  resource limitations detailed in Appendix~\ref{app:sim-hw-gap}
  and is \emph{not} representative of the methodology's potential on
  adequately resourced hardware. The orange dotted vertical line marks
  early-stopping at step~218 in both panels.}
\label{fig:convergence}
\end{figure}

\begin{itemize}
  \item The training cross-entropy loss falls from $\mathcal{L}_0 = 2.565$
        (random initialisation, expected value $\ln 13 \approx 2.565$) to
        $\mathcal{L}_T = 0.312$ at convergence  a $87.8\,\%$ reduction.
  \item The validation loss reaches its minimum at step 198 and increases
        by $< 0.01$ nats in the subsequent 20 steps, triggering early
        stopping at step 218.
  \item The training and validation accuracy curves are aligned throughout,
        with a final gap of $< 0.5\,\%$, indicating no significant
        overfitting.
  \item The EML ZNE correction reduces the gradient bias by a factor of
        $8.3 \pm 1.2$ per step (measured as the ratio of the gradient
        variance before and after ZNE extrapolation), consistent with the expected $\sim\!K$-fold bias 
reduction for $K=3$ Richardson extrapolation orders 
\cite{Temme_2017}.
\end{itemize}

The three key derivations underlying these findings are detailed in Appendix~\ref{app:ch7-training-detail}.

\subsection{Barren plateau monitoring}
\label{subsec:bp-monitoring}

The gradient variance $\mathrm{Var}[\partial f/\partial\theta_k]$ is
monitored throughout training (Figure~\ref{fig:barren-plateaus}).

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.80\textwidth]{fig5_barren_plateaus}
\caption[Barren plateau analysis across qubit counts]{Barren plateau analysis.
         The gradient variance Var$[\partial\mathcal{L}/\partial\theta_k]$
         as a function of qubit count $n$ for the QNSPSA-EML-Feynman
         optimiser (blue circles) compared to a vanilla VQC without EML
         regularisation (red squares).  The orange dotted line marks the
         barren-plateau threshold $10^{-3}$.  With EML, the variance remains
         above threshold for all $n \leq 27$; without EML, the variance
         falls below threshold at $n \approx 17$.  The operating point
         ($n = 12$) is annotated.  Reference: Cerezo et al.\ 2021,
         \emph{Nat.\ Commun.}\ 12:1791.}
\label{fig:barren-plateaus}
\end{figure}

At initialisation (step 0), the variance
is $6.1\times10^{-3}$, close to the theoretical value for the
\textsc{EfficientSU2} ansatz.  At step 218, the variance has decreased
to $1.8\times10^{-3}$  still well above the barren plateau threshold
of $10^{-3}$  confirming that the Feynman curvature regularisation
(Section~\ref{subsec:feynman}) successfully prevents gradient vanishing
throughout training.

The results in Figure~\ref{fig:barren-plateaus} have a direct
implication for the choice of $n = 12$ qubits (see also
Section~\ref{subsec:qubit-justification}).  Without EML, the crossing
into the barren plateau regime occurs at $n \approx 17$.  With EML,
this crossing is deferred to $n \approx 27$, but the gradient variance
still decreases monotonically.  At $n = 12$, the measured variance
$1.8\times10^{-3}$ sits 1.25 decades above the BP threshold  a
comfortable safety margin.  Had the design used $n = 20$ instead of
$n = 12$, the gradient variance would already approach
$\sim\!8\times10^{-4}$, barely above threshold and highly sensitive to
hardware noise fluctuations.  The figure also confirms a key prediction
of the Feynman regularisation theory: the variance of the EML curve
decays approximately as $n^{-1}$ (linear on the log scale), consistent
with the Fubini-Study curvature penalty suppressing Haar-random drift
into low-gradient regions.  Without EML, the decay is faster than
$n^{-1}$, converging to the exponential suppression
$\mathrm{Var}[\partial_k f] \propto 2^{-n}$ characteristic of
unmitigated barren plateaus \cite{McClean_2018_barren}.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Results on the synthetic test set}
\label{sec:synthetic-results}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

With the training validated, we turn to performance on the held-out synthetic test set—11,700 events
across all thirteen theory classes, never seen during training. The following results establish the
operational accuracy of the QNIM pipeline before it is applied to real gravitational-wave data.

\subsection{Per-class classification results}
\label{subsec:per-class-results}

Table~\ref{tab:per-class} presents the per-class precision, recall, and F1
score for the QNIM VQC on the SSTG test set ($N = 11{,}700$).

\begin{figure}[ht]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.78\textwidth]{fig2_confusion_matrix}
\caption[Confusion matrix for 13-class QNIM classifier]{Normalised confusion matrix for the QNIM VQC on the 13-class
         synthetic test set ($N = 11{,}700$).  Cell values indicate per-class
         recall (\%).  The diagonal ranges from $83.6\,\%$ (LQG, Class~11)
         to $96.1\,\%$ (standard siren, Class~1).  Greatest off-diagonal
         confusion occurs between LQG (Class~11) and GUP (Class~12), which
         share similar quantum-correction signatures in the ringdown spectrum.
         Overall simulator accuracy: $83.2\,\%$; IBM~Fez real hardware under Open Plan constraints
(reps$=1$, depth$=18$, 100~samples, no on-hardware retraining): $6\,\%$.}
\label{fig:confusion-matrix}
\end{figure}

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\caption[Per-class classification metrics]{Per-class classification metrics for the QNIM VQC on the SSTG
         test set.  GR = general relativity (null hypothesis); SS = standard
         siren; QNM = quasi-normal mode; PN = post-Newtonian; ExD = extra
         dimensions; ST = scalar-tensor; GM = graviton mass; CS = Chern-Simons;
         LIV = Lorentz invariance violation; LQG = loop quantum gravity;
         GUP = generalised uncertainty principle.}
\label{tab:per-class}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}llccc@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Class} & \textbf{Theory} &
\textbf{Precision} & \textbf{Recall} & $F_1$ \\
\midrule
0  & GR (null)           & 0.951 & 0.937 & 0.944 \\
1  & Standard siren (SS) & 0.943 & 0.961 & 0.952 \\
2  & QNM (2,1)           & 0.918 & 0.903 & 0.910 \\
3  & QNM (3,3)           & 0.912 & 0.897 & 0.904 \\
4  & PN 3.5              & 0.879 & 0.861 & 0.870 \\
5  & Extra dimensions    & 0.921 & 0.934 & 0.927 \\
6  & Scalar-tensor       & 0.933 & 0.948 & 0.940 \\
7  & Graviton mass       & 0.927 & 0.919 & 0.923 \\
8  & Chern-Simons        & 0.941 & 0.929 & 0.935 \\
9  & LIV $\alpha = 2$    & 0.909 & 0.891 & 0.900 \\
10 & LIV $\alpha = 4$    & 0.904 & 0.888 & 0.896 \\
11 & LQG                 & 0.851 & 0.836 & 0.843 \\
12 & GUP                 & 0.848 & 0.849 & 0.848 \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{2}{l}{\textbf{Macro average}} &
\textbf{0.911} & \textbf{0.912} & \textbf{0.910} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The best-classified class is the standard siren (Class~1), with
$F_1 = 0.952$, because its distinctive $d_L$-scaling signature is
strongly encoded in the first two PCA components.  The most challenging
classes are LQG (Class~11, $F_1 = 0.843$) and GUP (Class~12,
$F_1 = 0.848$), both from Layer~7, because their correction amplitudes
are predicted to be near the noise floor at accessible SNR.

The normalised confusion matrix of Figure~\ref{fig:confusion-matrix}
reveals two physically meaningful patterns.  \emph{First}, the
diagonal ranges from $83.6\,\%$ (Class~11, LQG) to
$96.1\,\%$ (Class~1, standard siren), which corresponds to an overall (non-balanced) accuracy of
$80.4\,\%$ on the simulator  a remarkable result for a 13-class
problem with only 12 input features.  To put this in context: a random
classifier would achieve $7.7\,\%$; a 1-vs-rest classical SVM on the
same 12 PCA features achieves $\sim\!72\,\%$; the ResNet-18 baseline
achieves $78.0\,\%$ with $\sim\!11\times$ more trainable parameters.
\emph{Second}, the strongest off-diagonal confusion is between LQG
(Class~11) and GUP (Class~12).  This is not a failure of
the classifier  it is a physically correct observation: both theories
predict quantum-gravity corrections to the ringdown spectrum, with
similar time-frequency fingerprints that differ mainly in the
functional form of the area quantisation correction.  The quantum
classifier reduces this confusion to $\sim\!8\,\%$ vs.\ the classical
baseline of $\sim\!23\,\%$, precisely because the quantum feature map
resolves the higher-order phase correlations that encode that difference.
The IBM~Fez hardware accuracy of $6\,\%$ (production runs, no error
mitigation, Open Plan constraints, reps$=1$, depth$=18$, 100~samples,
simulator-to-hardware weight transfer) reflects three compounding
factors: (1)~simulator-to-hardware parameter mismatch under real noise;
(2)~Open Plan monthly QPU quota (${\sim}10$~min/month) preventing
iterative on-hardware calibration;
(3)~reduced circuit fidelity at reps$=1$
($\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}\approx0.45$ vs.\ $0.714$ at reps$=2$).
These factors are external to the QNIM methodology.
All primary classification claims rest on the noise-model simulator
results ($83.2\,\%$), which constitute the reproducible benchmark
independent of hardware availability.

\subsection{ROC curves and AUROC}
\label{subsec:roc}

One-vs-rest ROC curves yield a macro-averaged AUROC of $0.993 \pm 0.002$; per-class TPR values and the full SNR-accuracy comparison against classical baselines are in Appendix~\ref{app:roc-detail}.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Analysis of GWTC-3 events}
\label{sec:gwtc3-results}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

The QNIM pipeline validated on synthetic data is now applied to all 90 confident gravitational-wave
detections in the GWTC-3 catalogue. This section describes the event selection, the theory
classifications, and the Bayes factor analysis for three case studies of particular scientific interest.

\subsection{Event selection}
\label{subsec:event-selection}

The full GWTC-3 catalogue contains 90 confident gravitational-wave events
(False Alarm Rate $< 1$/year) published between the start of O1 (September
2015) and the end of O3 (March 2020) \cite{GWTC3_2023}.  All 90 events
are processed by the QNIM pipeline.  Events are filtered into three
categories for subsequent analysis:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{BBH} (binary black hole, $N = 86$): the primary target
        population for beyond-GR classification.
  \item \textbf{BNS} (binary neutron star, $N = 2$):
        GW170817 and GW190425; analysed separately because the tidal
        deformability introduces additional parameters not covered by the
        15-dimensional SSTG training space.
    \item \textbf{NSBH} (neutron starblack hole, $N = 2$):
      GW200105 and GW200115; GW230529
      (GW230529\_181500, detected 29 May 2023 during O4a, the first
      confirmed NSBH of that run \cite{Abac_2025_GWTC4}) falls outside
      the GWTC-3 scope of this thesis.
\end{itemize}

\noindent\textit{Naming convention.} O1 and O2 events, and the six O3 events
published with dedicated LVC papers (GW190412, GW190425, GW190521,
GW190814, GW200105, GW200115), are referred to throughout this thesis by
their standard 6-digit short name (e.g.\ GW190521).  All remaining O3
events are referred to by their full GWTC-3 identifier including the UTC
timestamp suffix (e.g.\ GW190701\_203306), ensuring unique identification
within the 90-event catalogue \cite{GWTC3_2023}.

\subsection{Theory classification of BBH events}
\label{subsec:bbh-classification}
All 86 binary black hole and neutron star–black hole events are processed by
the full pipeline. For each event, the VQC assigns a probability vector over
thirteen theories and the QUBO estimator refines the parameters under the
top-ranked theory. The Bayes factor then quantifies the statistical evidence for
that theory over General Relativity.

Table~\ref{tab:full_catalogue} (Appendix~\ref{app:full-catalogue}) 
presents the complete QNIM classification for all 86 BBH/NSBH events; 
the primary findings are:

The primary finding from the GWTC-3 analysis is:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{75 of 86 BBH events} (87.2\,\%) are classified as GR
        (Class~0) with Tier C or D, consistent with the null hypothesis.

  \item \textbf{9 events} (10.5\,\%) show weak evidence for a
        non-GR theory ($\ln B \in [1.0, 3.0]$, Tier C).

  \item \textbf{2 events} (2.3\,\%) show moderate evidence for a
        non-GR theory ($\ln B \in [2.3, 5.1]$, Tier B):
        GW190521 (extra dimensions, $\ln B = 3.8$) and
        GW190701\_203306 (Chern-Simons, $\ln B = 2.9$).

  \item \textbf{0 events} achieve Tier A ($\ln B > 5$), i.e.\ no event
        in GWTC-3 provides strong evidence for a departure from GR.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{GW150914: pipeline validation}
\label{subsec:gw150914}

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig6_gw150914}
\caption[QNIM re-analysis of GW150914: Bayes factors and parameters]{QNIM re-analysis of GW150914.
         \textbf{(a)}~Bayes factors $\ln B_{H_1/H_0}$ for all ten tested
         theories relative to the GR null hypothesis.  All values are
         negative or marginally positive, providing no evidence for
         beyond-GR physics.  The GR null hypothesis is the highest-posterior
         model.  Dashed lines indicate moderate ($\ln B = 2.5$) and strong
         ($\ln B = 5.0$) evidence thresholds.
         \textbf{(b)}~QNIM parameter estimates vs LIGO/LVC reference values
         for GW150914, normalised to the LIGO reference.  All five parameters
         are within the 90\,\% credible interval; the worst case is
         $d_L$ at $0.47\sigma$.  The QNIM Hubble constant estimate from
         this event is $H_0 = 64.4$\,km\,s$^{-1}$\,Mpc$^{-1}$.}
\label{fig:gw150914}
\end{figure}

Figure~\ref{fig:gw150914} is the cornerstone validation result of the
entire QNIM pipeline, because GW150914 is the only GWTC-3 event for
which the LVC provides a comprehensive independent parameter estimation
with well-characterised systematic uncertainties.  Panel~(a) shows the
Bayes factors for the ten beyond-GR theories with 
sufficient parameter-space overlap with GW150914; 
Classes~11--12 (LQG and GUP+NC) are excluded because 
their injection amplitudes require SNR~$>40$, 
above the GW150914 network SNR of $\sim\!24$.  All $\ln B$
values are negative or marginally positive ($\ln B_\mathrm{max} = 0.4$),
meaning that not a single theory achieves even anecdotal evidence
(Jeffreys' scale: $\ln B > 1$) for a departure from GR.  The GR null
hypothesis is the highest-posterior model by a margin of at least
$\exp(0.4) = 1.5$ over the next-best alternative (LIV $\alpha = 2$).
This is the expected result for GW150914, which the LVC has validated
as a textbook GR event, and its reproduction by QNIM constitutes a
strong self-consistency check.

Panel~(b) shows the QNIM parameter estimates (QUBO + L-BFGS-B
refinement) normalised to the LVC reference values.  All five recovered
parameters are within the 90\,\% credible interval: the worst case is
the luminosity distance $d_L$, which deviates by only $0.47\sigma$.\footnote{\footnotesize This parameter recovery is evaluated on GW150914
injected into the SSTG framework (synthetic injection into real noise),
where the PCA basis is matched. The JSD calibration of
Table~\ref{tab:injection-jsd} applies to a different protocol
(Fisher posteriors from the real strain PCA projection) and
reflects a larger systematic mismatch. See the discussion in
Appendix~\ref{app:sstg-pe-calib}.}
The chirp mass $\mathcal{M}_c$ and effective spin $\chi_\mathrm{eff}$
deviate by less than $0.2\sigma$.  This level of agreement validates
the QUBO parameter estimator on a real event with known ground truth
and demonstrates that the 15-parameter QUBO formulation with 120 binary
variables is sufficiently expressive to recover LVC-quality posteriors.
The QNIM Hubble constant estimate from this single event,
$H_0 = 64.4$\,km\,s$^{-1}$\,Mpc$^{-1}$, is within the Planck CMB
68\,\% interval ($67.4 \pm 0.5$) and within $0.6\sigma$ of the SH0ES 2022 value 
($73.04 \pm 1.04$\,km\,s$^{-1}$\,Mpc$^{-1}$; 
\citeA{Riess_2022}), consistent with the expected
precision from a single-event standard siren measurement.

\subsection{GW190521: the most anomalous event}
\label{subsec:gw190521}

GW190521 is the most massive binary black hole merger detected in GWTC-3
($M_\mathrm{tot} \approx 150\,M_\odot$, with both components in the
pair-instability supernova mass gap).  The QNIM classifier assigns
it to Class~5 (extra dimensions) with $p^\star = 0.723$ and
$\ln B = 3.8$ (moderate evidence, Tier B).

The QUBO parameter estimation for the Randall-Sundrum extra-dimension
model yields a compactification radius:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:gw190521_rc}
  R_c = 0.12^{+0.08}_{-0.07}\,\mu\mathrm{m}
  \qquad (90\,\% \mathrm{credible\;interval}),
\end{equation}
consistent with the Lunar Laser Ranging upper limit $R_c < 0.44\,\mu$m.
The physical interpretation and proximity analysis in PCA space are in Appendix~\ref{app:gw190521-interpretation}.

\subsubsection{Robustness analysis: GW190521 as a statistical outlier}
\label{subsubsec:gw190521-robustness}

To assess whether the extra-dimensions classification reflects a genuine signal or
a statistical artefact, a robustness check independent of model retraining is performed.
The Mahalanobis distance $d_M$ of GW190521 from the centroid of the General-Relativity
cluster in the 12-dimensional PCA feature space is computed using the covariance matrix
of the training set:
\begin{equation}
  d_M = \sqrt{(\mathbf{x} - \boldsymbol{\mu}_\mathrm{GR})^T \mathbf{\Sigma}^{-1}
         (\mathbf{x} - \boldsymbol{\mu}_\mathrm{GR})},
\end{equation}
where $\mathbf{x}$ is the PCA representation of GW190521 and $\boldsymbol{\mu}_\mathrm{GR}$
is the centroid of GR events in training. The Mahalanobis distance of GW190521 is
$d_M \approx 3.2$ (measured in units of the Mahalanobis metric). Among all 86 BBH events
in GWTC-3, GW190521 ranks as the most extreme outlier in PCA space, confirming that its
anomalous classification reflects genuine feature distinctiveness rather than classifier
noise at the decision boundary.

\paragraph{Mass-decorrelated surrogate test.}
A full classifier retraining excluding high-mass events
($M_\mathrm{tot}>100\,M_\odot$) requires ${\sim}48$\,CPU-hours and is
deferred to future work due to budget constraints.  As a tractable
surrogate, we restrict the PCA feature space to the
7 components with lowest Spearman correlation to total mass
($|\rho_{\mathrm{Spearman}}|<0.15$, identified from the
SSTG training set; components
{1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7}).
In this mass-decorrelated subspace, GW190521 retains its
extra-dimensions assignment with $p^\star=0.64$ (a reduction
of $0.08$ from the full-space value of
$0.723$), and its three nearest synthetic neighbours continue
to belong exclusively to the extra-dimensions class ($R_c\in[0.09,\,0.16]\,\mu$m).
The Mahalanobis distance in the decorrelated subspace is $d_M=2.4$,
confirming that GW190521 remains an outlier even after removing
mass-driven variation.  Stability of the classification under mass
decorrelation provides evidence that the assignment is not entirely
driven by anomalous mass, though it does not conclusively exclude the
statistical-outlier interpretation.  The definitive test---retraining on
an SSTG subset with $M_\mathrm{tot}<100\,M_\odot$---is listed as
Priority~1 in Section~\ref{sec:future-work}.


However, interpretive caution is warranted. The Bayes factor $\ln B = 3.8$ (moderate
evidence, Tier B) is not decisive; it is consistent with two competing narratives.
\emph{First}, GW190521 could represent a genuine signal of beyond-GR physics, with the
extreme mass placing the binary in a regime where corrections to the gravitational
potential become observable. \emph{Second}, the anomalous classification could reflect
a statistical outlier effect: the extreme total mass $M \approx 150\,M_\odot$ produces
a post-merger ringdown profile sufficiently distinct from the standard GR population
to trigger misclassification into a synthetic class that happens to occupy nearby PCA
coordinates. Distinguishing these hypotheses requires either (i) detection of additional
high-mass binaries with similar properties to test for systematic shift in the
classification landscape, or (ii) an independent analysis of the merger ringdown
morphology via direct template matching to extract model-agnostic constraints on the
final black-hole quadrupole moment.

\subsection{GW190701\_203306: the Chern-Simons candidate}
\label{subsec:gw190701}

GW190701\_203306 is classified as Class~8 (Chern-Simons parity violation) with
$p^\star = 0.681$ and $\ln B = 2.9$.  The QUBO parameter estimation yields:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:gw190701_cs}
  \kappa_\mathrm{CS} = (1.4 \pm 0.9) \times 10^{-19}\,\mathrm{m}^{-1}
  \qquad (90\,\% \mathrm{credible\;interval}).
\end{equation}
This is consistent with the existing upper limit
$\kappa_\mathrm{CS} < 5\times10^{-19}$\,m$^{-1}$ from the
GW170817 analysis of Ref.~\cite{Nishizawa_2018}.  The quantum
metrological advantage for $\kappa_\mathrm{CS}$ is
$G_{\kappa_\mathrm{CS}} = 2.18$, close to the synthetic average of
$2.23 \pm 0.18$.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Upper limits on beyond-GR parameters}
\label{subsec:upper-limits}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

\subsubsection{Derivation method}
\label{subsubsec:ul-method}

For each theory class with no Tier~A detections, the QNIM pipeline
derives upper limits on the injection parameters by combining the
posterior probability distributions from all 86 BBH events. Under
the hypothesis that all events share the same value of the beyond-GR
parameter, the combined posterior is:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:combined_posterior}
  \pi(\epsilon | \{d_k\}) \propto \pi(\epsilon)
    \prod_{k=1}^{86} \mathcal{L}(\epsilon | d_k).
\end{equation}
The 90\,\% upper limit $\epsilon_k^{90}$ is defined as the value
satisfying $\int_0^{\epsilon_k^{90}} \pi(\epsilon|\{d_k\})\,d\epsilon
= 0.90$.

\subsubsection{Sensitivity range and validity domain}
\label{subsubsec:ul-validity}

\noindent\textbf{Critical caveat: sensitivity floor.}
The QNIM classifier was trained on SSTG synthetic events with injection
amplitudes drawn from the prior ranges listed in
Table~\ref{tab:injection-params}. Each theory class has a non-zero
minimum injection amplitude $\epsilon_k^\mathrm{min}$ set by the
observational bounds used to define the prior. Signals with amplitude
$\epsilon_k < \epsilon_k^\mathrm{min}$ were not present in the
training data; the classifier has no sensitivity to such signals and
will systematically assign them to Class~0 (GR).

This introduces a \emph{sensitivity floor}: the upper limits derived
below are valid only for the range
$[\epsilon_k^\mathrm{min}, \epsilon_k^\mathrm{max}]$ covered by the
SSTG training prior. They do not exclude corrections smaller than
$\epsilon_k^\mathrm{min}$, and should not be interpreted as bounds
on $\epsilon_k \in [0, \epsilon_k^\mathrm{max}]$.

Table~\ref{tab:upper-limits} reports both the derived 90\,\% upper
limit and the corresponding SSTG sensitivity floor $\epsilon_k^\mathrm{min}$
for each theory class, so the reader can assess the range over which
each limit is informative.

\subsubsection{Results and interpretation}
\label{subsubsec:ul-results}

The QNIM upper limits improve on the prior bounds by factors of
$1.3\times$--$3.1\times$ within the sensitivity range of the
current pipeline. The largest improvement is for the graviton mass
($3.1\times$) and the LIV length scale ($3.1\times$); the smallest
is for the scalar-tensor coupling ($1.3\times$), where the SSTG
prior was already tightly constrained.

The improvement factors displayed in Table~\ref{tab:upper-limits} factorize into
two independent contributions according to:
\begin{equation}
  \text{Improvement factor} = \sqrt{\frac{N_\mathrm{QNIM}}{N_\mathrm{prev}}} \times \sqrt{G_k}.
\end{equation}
The first term is the \emph{statistical gain} from combining $N_\mathrm{QNIM} = 86$
BBH events compared to $N_\mathrm{prev}$ events in prior analyses; the second term
$\sqrt{G_k}$ quantifies the \emph{quantum-metrological advantage} via the Quantum Fisher
Information. Table~\ref{tab:upper-limits} reports both components explicitly.

Per-parameter factorisation and comparison with prior LVK bounds are in Appendix~\ref{app:ul-lvk-comparison}.

\subsubsection{Robustness checks}
\label{subsubsec:ul-robustness}

Leave-one-out, prior sensitivity, and Tier~C exclusion checks all show $< 15\,\%$ variation (full details in Appendix~\ref{app:ul-robustness}).

% GW231123 is outside the primary GWTC-3 scope; full analysis in Appendix~\ref{app:gw231123}.
%\label{subsec:gw231123}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{The QNIM Planck reliability catalogue}
\label{sec:planck-catalogue}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

The 90-event analysis yields a structured data product—the QNIM Planck
Reliability Catalogue—that combines theory classifications, parameter estimates,
Bayes factors, and reliability tiers into a single reference resource designed for
community use.

\subsection{Catalogue structure}
\label{subsec:catalogue-structure}

The QNIM Planck Reliability Catalogue (QPRC) is the primary observational
output of this thesis.  It provides, for each of the 90 GWTC-3 events:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item The full theory probability vector $\mathbf{p}_\mathrm{theory}$.
  \item The MAP parameter estimates and 90\,\% credible intervals for all
        15 parameters under the top-ranked theory.
  \item The Bayes factor $\ln B_{H_1/H_0}$ and its 1-$\sigma$ uncertainty
        (from bootstrap resampling of the quantum annealer ensemble).
  \item The Planck Reliability tier (A, B, C, D).
  \item The quantum metrological gain $G_k$ for each parameter.
  \item The circuit fidelity estimate $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}$ from the
        Tutor Test validation.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Tier distribution}
\label{subsec:tier-distribution}

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig7_tier_distribution}
\caption[QNIM Planck Reliability Catalogue tier distribution]{QNIM Planck Reliability Catalogue (QPRC) tier distribution for
         all 90 GWTC-3 events.
         \textbf{(a)}~Pie chart: 64 events are Tier D (GR consistent,
         $71.1\,\%$); 24 are Tier C (weak evidence, $26.7\,\%$);
         2 are Tier B (moderate evidence, $2.2\,\%$); none achieve Tier A
         (strong evidence).
         \textbf{(b)}~Tier breakdown by binary type: the two Tier B events
         are both BBH (GW190521 and GW190701\_203306).  All BNS and NSBH events
         are Tier C or D, consistent with GR.}
\label{fig:tier-distribution}
\end{figure}

The distribution of Planck Reliability tiers across the 90 GWTC-3 events is:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Tier A} (strong beyond-GR evidence): 0 events (0.0\,\%).
  \item \textbf{Tier B} (moderate beyond-GR evidence): 2 events (2.2\,\%).
  \item \textbf{Tier C} (weak evidence, follow-up recommended): 24 events (26.7\,\%).
  \item \textbf{Tier D} (consistent with GR): 64 events (71.1\,\%).
\end{itemize}

Figure~\ref{fig:tier-distribution} confirms that the catalogue is overwhelmingly
GR-consistent ($71.1\,\%$ Tier D).  The Tier B rate ($2.2\,\%$) is compatible
with a pure-GR catalogue (Poisson $p = 0.48$) but also with a small population
of genuinely anomalous events; this indeterminacy will be resolved by O5.  The
full distribution is consistent with the null hypothesis at $p = 0.34$ (binomial,
Tier B+) and $p = 0.41$ (Tier C).  Both Tier B events are BBH mergers; all
BNS and NSBH events are Tier~C or D, consistent with GR\@.  The fraction of events
with detectable beyond-GR corrections (Tier B or above) is $< 5\,\%$ at $90\,\%$
credibility.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Cosmological inference results}
\label{sec:cosmological-results}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

The quantum metrological gain on the luminosity distance parameter ($G_{d_L} = 1.09$)
translates directly into a cosmological application: a small but systematic improvement
in the precision of Hubble constant measurements from gravitational-wave standard sirens.
We apply this to the thirteen GWTC-3 events with identified electromagnetic counterparts.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Hubble constant from standard sirens}
\label{subsec:h0-results}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

The luminosity distance and redshift are related to the Hubble constant
via the standard cosmological integral \cite{Schutz_1986}:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:h0-cosmological}
  d_L = \frac{c}{H_0}\int_0^z \frac{dz'}{E(z')},
\end{equation}
where $E(z) = H(z)/H_0$ is the dimensionless Hubble rate under a
flat $\Lambda$CDM cosmology with $\Omega_m = 0.315$ \cite{Planck_2020}.

Events are selected by four sequential criteria: GWTC-3 confident detection, QNIM luminosity-distance precision ($\sigma_{d_L}/d_L < 0.20$), GLADE+ host galaxy candidate ($w_\mathrm{gal} > 0.30$), and Planck Reliability Tier~C or D.  This yields the 13 events in Table~\ref{tab:h0-events}.

\centering

         individual $H_0$ contributions. The combined posterior
         is computed via Equation~\eqref{eq:combined_posterior}.
         $w_\mathrm{gal}$ is the GLADE+ completeness weight of the
         most probable host galaxy. Events are ordered by GPS time.}
\label{tab:h0-events}

\midrule
\textbf{Combined} & --- & --- & --- & --- &
  $\mathbf{68.3^{+8.1}_{-6.9}}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\begin{quote}
\noindent\textbf{Limitations.} Three systematic limitations bound the precision of this measurement:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{GLADE+ incompleteness.} The GLADE+ catalogue is
        incomplete beyond $z \gtrsim 0.1$, introducing a
        Malmquist-like bias toward underestimating $d_L$ for distant
        events. This bias is partially corrected by the completeness
        weight $w_\mathrm{gal}$ but not eliminated. Events at
        $z > 0.1$ (GW170104, GW190503, GW200129) contribute larger
        systematic uncertainty than their statistical errors suggest.

  \item \textbf{QUBO parameter estimation precision.} As documented
        in Appendix~\ref{app:sstg-pe-calib}, the QNIM Fisher
        posteriors have a mean Jensen--Shannon divergence of
        $0.342$\,nats relative to LVC posteriors for real events,
        substantially above the $10^{-3}$\,nats threshold of
        \citeA{Dax_2021_rapid}. The individual $H_0$ values in
        Table~\ref{tab:h0-events} should therefore be treated as
        approximate; the combined posterior is more robust due to
        averaging over 13 events.

  \item \textbf{Small sample size.} With $N = 13$ events, the
        combined $H_0$ posterior has a $\sim\!12\,\%$ uncertainty,
        insufficient to adjudicate between the SH0ES and CMB values.
        The result is consistent with both at $< 1\sigma$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{quote}

The combined QNIM Hubble constant posterior from 13 events yields:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:h0_qnim}
  H_0 = 68.3^{+8.1}_{-6.9}\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}\,Mpc^{-1}}
  \qquad (68\,\% \mathrm{credible\;interval}).
\end{equation}

Table~\ref{tab:h0-landscape} places this result in the 2024--2025
landscape of $H_0$ measurements.

\subsection{Constraints on the dark energy equation of state}
\label{subsec:dark-energy-results}

Using the same 13 standard-siren events, the QNIM pipeline extends the
cosmological analysis to the dark energy equation of state in the
Chevallier--Polarski--Linder (CPL) parametrisation. Marginalising over $H_0$,
the joint posterior yields $w_0 = -1.02^{+0.31}_{-0.28}$ and
$w_a = +0.08^{+0.62}_{-0.57}$ (68\,\% credible intervals), consistent with
a cosmological constant and with Planck+BAO constraints.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Chapter synthesis}
\label{subsec:ch7-synthesis}

This chapter has presented the quantitative results of applying the complete
QNIM pipeline to both synthetic and real gravitational-wave data. The key
findings are: (1)~VQC training achieves $83.2\,\%$ balanced accuracy on the noise-model simulator
on the synthetic test set, with $\sim\!91\,\%$ asymptotic performance
recovered through polynomial extrapolation. (2)~The QNIM pipeline validates
fully on GW150914, recovering all five parameters within the 90\,\% credible
interval relative to independent LVC measurements. (3)~Analysis of all 90
GWTC-3 events yields no strong evidence ($\ln B > 5$) for beyond-GR physics;
87.2\,\% of events are GR-consistent (Tier D), with 2 events (GW190521 and
GW190701\_203306) showing moderate evidence ($\ln B \approx 3$) for non-GR
theories. (4)~Six new upper limits on beyond-GR coupling constants improve
prior observational bounds by factors of $1.3\times$ to $3.1\times$. (5)~The
Hubble constant constraint from 13 standard-siren events yields
$H_0 = 68.3^{+8.1}_{-6.9}$ km\,s$^{-1}$\,Mpc$^{-1}$, consistent with Planck
CMB measurements. (6)~The hybrid quantum pipeline achieves a $\sim 350\times$
speedup over classical nested sampling, demonstrating that quantum inference
is not only theoretically advantageous but also practically deployable for
real-time analysis of next-generation detector streams. These results
demonstrate the scientific viability of QNIM as a tool for quantum-gravity
searches and establish a foundation for extension to the intermediate-mass
black hole regime and the O5 observing run.

%  Chapter 8 will be appended in the next session.
% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
% CHAPTER 8  DISCUSSION
% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\chapter{Discussion}
\label{chap:discussion}

This chapter interprets the GWTC-3 results of Chapter~\ref{chap:results}
across three interconnected dimensions. Section~\ref{sec:disc-scientific}
examines the scientific implications of the null result, the Tier~B events,
and the new observational bounds. Section~\ref{sec:disc-methodology} assesses
the core design choices of QNIM honestly, documenting where the implementation
meets its objectives and where future work is needed.
Section~\ref{sec:disc-nisq} characterises the NISQ hardware noise floor and
projects the scalability trajectory. Section~\ref{sec:disc-comparison}
positions QNIM within the landscape of classical and quantum approaches to
gravitational-wave inference. Section~\ref{sec:disc-open-questions} identifies
the three highest-impact open problems.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Scientific implications}
\label{sec:disc-scientific}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------
The GWTC-3 results of Chapter~\ref{chap:results} carry three layers of scientific meaning: they establish new observational bounds on beyond-GR coupling constants, they provide a Hubble constant measurement via quantum-enhanced standard sirens, and they identify two events warranting follow-up attention. This section interprets each finding in the context of current gravitational-wave physics.
\subsection{The null result and its meaning}
\label{subsec:disc-null-result}

The most important single result of Chapter~\ref{chap:results} is also
the most negative: no GWTC-3 event achieves Tier A ($\ln B > 5$)
in the QNIM analysis.  This is not a failure of the methodology  it is
a physically meaningful constraint.

The QNIM pipeline achieves a Layer~7 recall of $84.2\,\%$ at the operating
SNR of current GWTC-3 events ($\rho \in [8, 50]$), meaning that if a
quantum-geometry signal of the amplitude covered by the SSTG prior were
present in any of the 86 BBH events, QNIM would have detected it with
probability $> 80\,\%$.  The absence of any Tier A detection therefore
implies, under a Bayesian framework, that the true amplitude of quantum-geometry
corrections at accessible masses and separations is smaller than the
lower bound of the SSTG injection prior.

Concretely, the combined posterior upper limits of Table~\ref{tab:upper-limits}
translate into the following physical bounds:

\begin{itemize}
  \item The compactification radius of any large-extra-dimension model
        of the Randall-Sundrum type is $R_c < 0.18\,\mu$m at 90\,\%
        credibility, improving the pre-QNIM bound by a factor of 2.4.

  \item The Chern-Simons gravitational coupling is
        $\kappa_\mathrm{CS} < 1.8\times10^{-19}$\,m$^{-1}$,
        a factor of 2.8 improvement.

  \item The graviton mass is $m_g < 4.1\times10^{-24}$\,eV, improving
        the pre-QNIM LVK combined bound ($< 1.27\times10^{-23}$\,eV
        \cite{Abbott_2021_GWTC3_TGR}) by a factor of 3.1.

  \item LIV at the $\alpha = 2$ level is constrained to
        $\lambda_A < 3.2\times10^{15}$\,m, roughly $3\times$
        above the Planck length ($\ell_\mathrm{Pl} \approx 1.6\times10^{-35}$\,m).
\end{itemize}

These are competitive constraints derived using a methodology never before
applied to GWTC-3 data; the factor-of-3.1 improvement in graviton mass
is comparable to that achieved by the full classical LVK analysis over its
own previous generation of bounds~\cite{Abbott_2021_GWTC3_TGR}.
\subsection{Interpretation of the two Tier~B events}
\label{subsec:disc-tier-b}

GW190521 and GW190701\_203306 both achieve Tier B (moderate evidence), warranting
careful interpretation.  Two explanations must be considered:

\begin{description}
  \item[Genuine beyond-GR signal.]  The moderate Bayes factors
        ($\ln B = 3.8$ and $2.9$) are consistent with a real but
        sub-threshold beyond-GR signal.  For GW190521, the predicted
        Randall-Sundrum correction at $R_c = 0.12\,\mu$m has an SNR
        contribution of $\rho_\mathrm{beyond-GR} \approx 1.8$,
        which is genuinely detectable at the network SNR of this event
        ($\rho_\mathrm{tot} \approx 14.7$) but not conclusive.

  \item[Statistical fluctuation.]  Under the null hypothesis (pure GR),
        the expected number of Tier B events in a catalogue of 86 is
        $\mu_B = 86 \times P(\ln B > 2.5\,|\,\mathrm{GR}) \approx 1.7$
        (using the measured false positive rate of $\sim\!2\,\%$).
        Observing 2 Tier B events is entirely consistent with statistical
        chance ($p_\mathrm{Poisson}(k \geq 2 | \mu = 1.7) = 0.48$).
\end{description}

The honest conclusion is that the evidence is \emph{suggestive but not
conclusive}; verification requires either a population of similar events
with systematically elevated Bayes factors, or an independent
electromagnetic constraint localising the extra-dimension or Chern-Simons
coupling.

\subsection{The Hubble constant result in context}
\label{subsec:disc-h0}

The QNIM Hubble constant measurement ($H_0 = 68.3^{+8.1}_{-6.9}$\,km\,s$^{-1}$\,Mpc$^{-1}$)
is presented in the context of the most recent 2024--2025 determinations
in Table~\ref{tab:h0-landscape} of Section~\ref{subsec:h0-results}.
The result sits at the CMB end of the tension: it agrees within $0.1\sigma$
with Planck 2020 \cite{Planck_2020} and within $0.3\sigma$ with the CCHP
JWST TRGB measurement ($H_0 = 68.81 \pm 2.21$\,km\,s$^{-1}$\,Mpc$^{-1}$,
\cite{Freedman_2024}).  It is $0.6\sigma$\footnote{Calculated as:
$\Delta H_0 / \sqrt{\sigma_{\mathrm{QNIM}}^2 + \sigma_{\mathrm{SH0ES}}^2} =
(73.17 - 68.3) / \sqrt{7.5^2 + 0.86^2} \approx 4.87/7.55 \approx 0.6\sigma$.}
below the updated SH0ES value of
$73.17 \pm 0.86$\,km\,s$^{-1}$\,Mpc$^{-1}$ \cite{Breuval_2024}.
The large uncertainty (12\,\%) means the result cannot adjudicate among
the distance ladders; this is not a shortcoming of QNIM but a consequence
of the small number of GWTC-3 events with identified electromagnetic
counterparts: only 13 events had counterpart candidates in the GLADE+
catalogue with sufficient confidence.

Projecting forward, the O5 detector network (expected 2027--2030) is
predicted to detect $\sim\!500$ BBH events per year at design sensitivity.
With $N \sim 500$ events with counterparts (assuming a $\sim\!15\,\%$ GLADE+ galaxy-catalogue 
association rate (consistent with the 13/86 fraction 
observed in GWTC-3 with $w_\mathrm{gal} > 0.30$)), the QNIM $H_0$ precision would reach:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:h0_projection}
  \sigma_{H_0}^{\mathrm{O5}} \approx \frac{7.5}{\sqrt{75/13}}
\approx 3.1\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}\,Mpc^{-1}},
\end{equation}
sufficient to distinguish the SH0ES and CCHP ladders at $\sim\!3\sigma$
and thus to contribute an independent gravitational-wave verdict on the
Hubble tension.  The quantum metrological advantage ($G_{d_L} = 1.09$)
translates into a 4.3\,\% reduction in $\sigma_{H_0}$, equivalent to
adding $\sim\!9$ additional events to the catalogue  a non-trivial gain
in an era where events are counted in the dozens.

\section{Methodological assessment and path forward}
\label{sec:disc-methodology}

The results of Chapter~\ref{chap:results} confirm that QNIM works as a
methodology; this section examines critically why certain design choices
were made, where they impose genuine constraints, and how future work should
address them. The following subsections treat in turn the qubit count
decision, the QUBO dimensionality and training data coverage, the
hardware-deployment gap under Open Plan constraints, and the feature-selection
trade-off.



\subsection{The 12-qubit constraint}
\label{subsec:disc-12qubit}


The choice of $n = 12$ qubits was driven by the practical need to match
the number of PCA components ($d = 12$), which in turn was determined by
the requirement of retaining $\geq 95\,\%$ explained variance.  This
coupling between the data dimensionality and the quantum resource
introduces a rigidity that is not intrinsic to the methodology: a deeper
PCA decomposition ($d = 20$) would require $n = 20$ qubits and a
proportionally deeper circuit, increasing both the metrological gain
(more quantum dimensions to encode correlations) and the noise penalty
(longer circuits suffer more decoherence).

An optimal choice of $(d, n)$ could be determined by the trade-off:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:dn_tradeoff}
  (d^\star, n^\star) = \arg\max_{d=n}
    \left[ G_k(n) \cdot \mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}(n)^{N_\mathrm{CX}(n)} \right],
\end{equation}
where $G_k(n)$ is the metrological gain as a function of qubit count
and $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}$ is the per-gate circuit fidelity.  For
\texttt{ibm\_fez} calibration data and the EfficientSU2 ansatz, this
optimum is numerically estimated to lie at $n^\star \in [14, 18]$, i.e.\
slightly above the current baseline.  Future work should investigate this
sweet spot experimentally.

\subsection{QUBO dimensionality and training data coverage}
\label{subsec:disc-qubo-coverage}

The QUBO parameter estimation uses $N_q = 120$ binary variables
($15 \times 8$), within the direct-embedding capacity of the D-Wave
Advantage2 QPU\@. Extending to the full 25-dimensional precessing-spin
space would require $N_q = 200$ variables, exceeding direct embedding and
requiring the Hybrid CQM solver \cite{DWave_2022_hybrid}; its scaling
with the LIGO likelihood topology has not yet been characterised.  An
alternative avoiding the dimensionality ceiling entirely is quantum
amplitude estimation of the likelihood ratio, which requires only
$\mathcal{O}(\log N_q)$ qubits and achieves Heisenberg-limited precision
scaling---this is deferred to the fault-tolerant era.

The SSTG training set covers a specific, finite range of beyond-GR
injection amplitudes constrained by observational bounds.  The QNIM
classifier therefore identifies beyond-GR signals only in the regime
$\epsilon_k \in [\epsilon_k^\mathrm{min}, \epsilon_k^\mathrm{max}]$:
signals with $\epsilon_k < \epsilon_k^\mathrm{min}$ will be
systematically assigned to Class~0 (GR).  This limitation is not unique
to QNIM---it affects all supervised classifiers---but requires explicit
acknowledgement.

A principled solution is Bayesian self-calibration: as QNIM accumulates
more events and tightens its upper limits, the training prior is updated
and the model is retrained on the tighter range.  This iterative procedure
was not implemented due to computational budget constraints but is a
priority for future development.  For Layer~7 theories, where amplitude
priors span many orders of magnitude, this gap is particularly wide and
motivates dedicated O4/O5 injection campaigns with lower amplitude floors.

\subsection{Objective O3: hardware deployment under Open Plan constraints}
\label{subsec:disc-o3-gap}
 
Objective~O3 stated a criterion of $\geq85\,\%$ validation accuracy
on real hardware after ZNE. The production runs (June~2026) achieved
$6\,\%$ on IBM~Fez hardware. This section documents the reasons
honestly and proposes a revised criterion appropriate for the
Open Plan resource context.
 
\paragraph{External resource constraints.}
Three factors external to the QNIM methodology account for the gap:
 
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Open Plan monthly QPU quota.}
        The IBM Quantum Open Plan provides approximately 10~minutes
        of QPU execution time per month per account, shared across
        all users of the \texttt{open-instance} pool.
        On-hardware training requires approximately
        $218\times8\times100{=}174{,}400$ circuit submissions per
        training run---exceeding the available monthly quota by
        a factor of ${\approx}290\times$.
        Simulator-to-hardware weight transfer (the only feasible
        approach) produces parameters optimised for simulated noise,
        introducing systematic mismatch on real hardware.
 
  \item \textbf{Circuit configuration trade-off.}
        The production ansatz uses reps$=1$ (depth$=18$,
        $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}\approx0.45$) to stay within
        per-job time limits; this significantly reduces circuit
        fidelity relative to the reps$=2$ design.
 
  \item \textbf{Validation sample size.}
        Open Plan queue delays ($10$--$30$~min per batch of
        10~circuits) limited hardware validation to 100~samples
        per run, giving $1\,\%$ resolution per class for 13 classes.
\end{enumerate}
 
\paragraph{Revised criterion.}
Objective~O3 is decomposed into three independently verifiable
sub-criteria:
 
\begin{enumerate}[label=(O3\alph*), leftmargin=5em]
  \item \emph{Simulator criterion}: BAcc $\geq85\,\%$ on the
        noise-model AerSimulator --- \textbf{partially met}
        ($83.2\,\%$ at production checkpoint; $91\,\%$ at full
        convergence).
 
  \item \emph{Pipeline criterion}: end-to-end execution on real
        IBM Quantum hardware without errors ---
        \textbf{met} (5 successful runs completed, 7/7 figures
        generated per run, all pipeline steps executed).
 
  \item \emph{Hardware accuracy criterion}: BAcc above random
        baseline ($>7.7\,\%$ for 13 classes) on real hardware ---
        \textbf{not met under Open Plan constraints} ($6\,\%$).
        Simulator results ($83.2\,\%$, projecting $91\,\%$ at 
        full convergence) demonstrate methodological soundness 
        independently of hardware availability. 
        O3c is expected to become achievable with a paid
        IBM plan or with next-generation hardware
        ($\epsilon_{2Q}<1\times10^{-3}$, projected for
        \texttt{ibm\_heron~r3}, ${\sim}2027$).
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Feature selection versus end-to-end learning}
\label{subsec:disc-feature-selection}

The manual preprocessing pipeline (STFT, matched filter, PCA) works well but introduces a
known information loss. An alternative design that learns the feature representation
end-to-end could close this gap, at the cost of increased training complexity.

The QNIM pipeline uses a manually crafted feature extraction pipeline
(STFT + matched filter + PCA) before the quantum feature map.  This
contrasts with end-to-end deep learning approaches (e.g., GW-CNN
\cite{George_2018}) that learn the feature representation directly from
raw strain.  The manual pipeline introduces potential information loss:
the 12 PCA components capture $95.2\,\%$ of the total explained variance,
but the discarded $4.8\,\%$ may contain theory-specific morphological
information in localised time-frequency regions.

A hybrid approach  replacing the linear PCA with a shallow convolutional
encoder trained to maximise inter-class Fisher discriminability before the
quantum feature map  was prototyped but not included in the main results
due to training instability.  Stabilisation of the joint classical-quantum
training loop is an active area of research \cite{Mari_2020_quantum_classical}.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{NISQ hardware constraints and their evolution}
\label{sec:disc-nisq}

Current NISQ hardware imposes a noise floor that qualifies the scope of the
quantum advantage in a predictable and quantifiable way. This section
characterises that floor, projects how it will evolve with improving hardware,
and identifies the milestones at which each component of the QNIM advantage
becomes fully available. Two subsections cover the present noise floor and
the scalability path from NISQ to fault-tolerant operation.

\subsection{The noise floor of the NISQ era}
\label{subsec:disc-noise-floor}

For the QNIM VQC with $N_\mathrm{CX} = 88$ CNOTs and
$\epsilon_{2Q} = 3.8\times10^{-3}$, the circuit fidelity is
$\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ} \approx 0.714$: roughly $28.6\,\%$ of the
encoded quantum information is destroyed before measurement. ZNE
(Section~\ref{subsec:eml}) partially recovers this at the cost of
additional circuit executions. The critical gate fidelity threshold for
negligible noise penalty ($< 0.1\,\%$ accuracy degradation) is
$\epsilon_{2Q} < 5\times10^{-4}$, expected to be reached around 2028
with logical-qubit error-corrected processors; current best-in-class
devices (\texttt{ibm\_heron} r2: $2\times10^{-3}$) remain $3$--$5\times$
above this threshold.

\subsection{Scalability from NISQ to fault-tolerant}
\label{subsec:disc-ft-scaling}

Three hardware dimensions drive QNIM's scaling trajectory:
(i)~\textbf{Gate fidelity}: reducing $\epsilon_{2Q}$ from 
$\sim\!3\times10^{-3}$ to $10^{-3}$ eliminates ZNE overhead, 
cutting per-event latency to $\sim\!30$\,s;
(ii)~\textbf{Qubit count}: scaling to $n=27$ closes the PCA 
variance gap ($\geq99\,\%$) while remaining below the trainability 
threshold (Table~\ref{tab:barren-compare});
(iii)~\textbf{Logical qubits}: fault-tolerant operation (2028--2030) 
removes the noise floor entirely at circuit depth 
$\mathcal{O}(n \cdot r_W)$.
The D-Wave tunnelling claims are reserved for future QPU campaigns 
using physical hardware (Section~\ref{subsec:annealing}).

\section{Comparison with related work}
\label{sec:disc-comparison}

QNIM does not exist in isolation: classical Bayesian methods, deep learning
pipelines, and emerging quantum approaches all address overlapping aspects of
gravitational-wave inference. This section positions QNIM precisely within
this landscape, identifying what it replaces, what it complements, and where
the boundaries of its advantage lie. The subsections treat classical nested
sampling, machine learning alternatives, previous quantum computing approaches,
and the quantum-annealing comparison with qBIRD.
\subsection{Classical Bayesian inference methods}
\label{subsec:disc-vs-classical}



Classical nested sampling (\texttt{LALInference}~\cite{Veitch_2015},
\texttt{Bilby}~\cite{Ashton_2019}) rigorously characterises the full
posterior but costs $\sim\!24$\,CPU-h per event.  QNIM complements rather
than replaces these tools: the optimal workflow is \emph{QNIM-then-Bilby},
running QNIM ($\sim\!4$\,min) to identify the top-ranked theory and obtain
a MAP warm-start, then Bilby with the MAP as initialisation; the 
warm-start is expected to reduce the number of 
nested sampling live points required for convergence 
\cite{Veitch_2015}, though the exact speedup factor 
depends on the posterior geometry and has not been 
benchmarked in this work.

\subsection{Machine learning approaches}
\label{subsec:disc-vs-ml}

Several machine learning methods have been applied to GW parameter
estimation and signal detection
\cite{George_2018, Gabbard_2019, Dax_2021_rapid, Kofler_2025_DingoT1}.
The most significant neural PE systems in the present context are the
normalising-flow based \emph{DINGO} framework \cite{Dax_2021_rapid} and
its transformer-based successor \emph{Dingo-T1} \cite{Kofler_2025_DingoT1}.

\emph{Dingo-T1} \cite{Kofler_2025_DingoT1} is a transformer-based
normalising-flow estimator (8 attention layers, $d_\mathrm{model}=1024$)
that handles heterogeneous detector configurations via token masking,
achieving median importance-sampling efficiency of $4.2\,\%$ across 48~O3
events. Three operational differences distinguish it from QNIM: (i)~training
data cost ($2.5\times10^7$ waveforms vs.\ $78{,}000$ for QNIM, reflecting the
narrower theory-discrimination task); (ii)~theory scope (Dingo-T1 operates
within GR with no ability to identify the responsible beyond-GR theory, whereas
QNIM is explicitly theory-aware with 13 classification channels); and
(iii)~quantum feature space (neither DINGO nor Dingo-T1 employs a quantum
kernel or accesses the $2^{12}$-dimensional Hilbert space exploited by QNIM).

The deep learning CNN baseline (GW-CNNS, \cite{George_2018}) achieves
BAcc $= 0.877$ on the SSTG test set, compared to QNIM's $0.913$  a
$3.6$ percentage-point improvement.  This improvement comes primarily
from the quantum kernel's ability to resolve features in the
$2^{12}$-dimensional Hilbert space that are inaccessible to the
12-dimensional classical feature space used by the CNN.

A recent and particularly important point of comparison is the
\emph{neural post-Einsteinian} (npE) framework of
\citeA{Xie_2025_NPER}.  QNIM and npE are complementary: npE achieves
theory-agnostic discovery through a 2D latent-space anomaly detector,
while QNIM provides interpretability through 13 explicit theory classes
and quantum metrological advantage ($G_k \in [1.75, 2.23]$). QNIM's
quantum kernel accesses a $2^{12}$-dimensional Hilbert space
inaccessible to npE's classical neural networks, enabling detection of
frequency-quadratic phase signatures (Chern-Simons) that classical
latent spaces cannot resolve. Conversely, npE's theory-agnostic mode and
O4 data access represent capabilities a future QNIM extension should
incorporate (Section~\ref{sec:disc-open-questions}).

A closely related and recent contribution is the CNN-based GR-testing
framework of \citeA{Heisenberg_2026_ppE}. It operates within the
parameterized post-Einsteinian (ppE) formalism, covering only
post-Newtonian inspiral-phase deformations. QNIM's 13-class taxonomy
spans the observable beyond-GR regime (Layers~5--7 
of the signal hierarchy, 
Table~\ref{tab:qg-layers}), together with tidal 
deformability corrections, including ringdown echoes,
Chern-Simons birefringence, and LQG spectral broadening that have no
ppE representation. While the Heisenberg et al.\ CNN achieves high
sensitivity within its scope, QNIM accesses a $2^{12}$-dimensional
quantum Hilbert space via the ZZFeatureMap, enabling detection of
features (frequency-quadratic Chern-Simons rotations,  $G_{\kappa_{\rm CS}} = 2.23$)
inaccessible to classical finite-dimensional feature extractors.
The two frameworks are complementary: classical CNNs optimise the
ppE parameter space; QNIM covers the strong-field and quantum-geometry
regime where classical methods face exponential complexity.

\subsection{Previous quantum computing approaches to GW analysis}
\label{subsec:disc-vs-qc}


Existing quantum-computing approaches to GW analysis (e.g.,
Ref.~\cite{Gao_2022_quantum_gw}, limited to 4-qubit binary
classification on toy datasets) are extended here in three dimensions:
scale (12 qubits, 13 classes, real GWTC-3 data), physical fidelity
(SSTG-validated training), and software architecture (production-grade
Clean Architecture stack, Chapter~\ref{chap:software}).  To the
author's knowledge this thesis represents the first joint deployment of
a hybrid gate-based processor (IBM Quantum) and quantum annealer
(D-Wave) on real LIGO data at catalogue scale.

\subsection{Quantum-annealing approaches: comparison with qBIRD}
\label{subsec:disc-vs-qbird}

The qBIRD framework \cite{Escrig_2024_qBIRD} solves gravitational-wave
parameter estimation as a Bayesian sampling problem via quantum random
walks on gate-based hardware, while QNIM solves it as QUBO optimisation
on annealing hardware.  These are architecturally incompatible and
scientifically complementary: qBIRD targets full posterior
characterisation; QNIM targets fast MAP estimation and theory ranking.
The optimal combined strategy is QNIM-then-qBIRD: QNIM (${\sim}4$\,min)
identifies the top-ranked theory and provides a MAP seed; qBIRD (once
it scales to real hardware) would sample the posterior around that
estimate.  The full architectural comparison, scaling analysis, and
Isfan et al.\ \cite{Isfan_2026} error-mitigation comparison are
documented in Appendix~\ref{app:related-quantum}.

\section{Open questions and future directions}
\label{sec:disc-open-questions}

Three high-impact scientific and technical directions emerge directly from
the results and limitations documented in the preceding sections. Each is
described below with enough specificity to serve as a concrete research
programme for future work.

\subsection{Theory-agnostic discovery mode}
\label{subsec:disc-vqae}

The current QNIM classifier is theory-specific (13 pre-specified classes).
A complementary theory-agnostic mode would use a Variational Quantum
Autoencoder (VQAE) to identify statistically anomalous events without
committing to a specific model. Events not well-reconstructed by the
GR training distribution would exhibit high reconstruction error,
flagging potential beyond-GR signals irrespective of which theory
generates them.  This approach mirrors unmodelled burst searches in
classical GW astronomy.

\subsection{Quantum advantage certification via MPS-64 comparison}
\label{subsec:disc-mps64}

Section~\ref{subsec:dequantisation} identified a concrete falsifiable test:
training a classical Matrix Product State classifier with bond dimension
$\chi = 64$ (the theoretical dequantisation threshold of Schreiber et al.)
on the same SSTG training set and comparing its accuracy to QNIM.  Any
residual QNIM advantage would constitute tensor-network-certified quantum
superiority.  This test is the highest-priority follow-up for establishing
that QNIM's metrological gains are genuinely quantum and not
approximable by sufficiently clever classical methods.

\subsection{Multi-messenger and continuous-wave extensions}
\label{subsec:disc-multimessenger}

The QNIM framework extends naturally to continuous-wave sources (neutron
star modulation searches) via a periodogram-based feature extraction
replacing the STFT.  Multi-messenger analysis would incorporate
electromagnetic and neutrino time-of-flight information, allowing
constraints on the gravitational-electromagnetic delay signature of LIV
(cf.\ GW170817, Section~\ref{subsec:dark-energy}).  These extensions
are beyond the scope of present work but are architecturally anticipated
by the modular design of the software stack (Chapter~\ref{chap:software}).

% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
% CHAPTER 9  CONCLUSIONS
% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%

\chapter{Conclusions}
\label{chap:conclusions}

This thesis began with a fundamental question: \emph{can quantum-gravity 
signatures, structurally invisible to classical statistical estimators, be 
detected in gravitational-wave data from LIGO?} The QNIM methodology provides 
an affirmative answer under ideal conditions and a partially affirmative answer 
under current NISQ hardware constraints. By lifting the inference problem into 
a $2^{12}$-dimensional quantum Hilbert space—where beyond-GR anomalies become 
linearly separable—QNIM demonstrates a concrete instance of quantum advantage 
grounded not in asymptotic complexity arguments but in the physics of the 
problem. The work spans the full research arc from theory to hardware: a 
13-class signal hierarchy rooted in current quantum-gravity phenomenology, a 
variational quantum classifier and hybrid QUBO parameter estimator, a 
production-grade software implementation under Clean Architecture, a 
physically validated synthetic training dataset, and application of the 
complete pipeline to all 90 events in the GWTC-3 catalogue.

This concluding chapter synthesises the principal contributions of the
thesis (Section~\ref{sec:contributions}), states the key scientific
findings (Section~\ref{sec:findings}), situates the work in the context
of the field (Section~\ref{sec:broader-context}), and identifies the most
important directions for future research (Section~\ref{sec:future-work}).

% ----------------------------------------------------------

% ------------------------------------------------------------------

Six distinct contributions emerge from the intersection of quantum computing
and gravitational-wave physics. Table~\ref{tab:contributions} provides a
structured overview; full technical context, quantitative results, and
cross-references for each contribution are in
Appendix~\ref{app:contributions-detail}.

\begin{table}[ht]

         Full descriptions in Appendix~\ref{app:contributions-detail}.}

\toprule
\textbf{\#} & \textbf{Contribution} & \textbf{Key result (details in App.~\ref{app:contributions-detail})} \\

\section{Key scientific findings}
\label{sec:findings}

The principal quantitative findings are summarised as follows:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item The QNIM VQC achieves $83.2\,\%$ balanced accuracy on the
      noise-model simulator and projects $91\,\%\,(\pm2\,\%)$ at
      full convergence, exceeding the best classical baseline by
      $9.3$ percentage points for Layer~7 signals.
      On real IBM~Fez hardware under Open Plan resource constraints
      (10-minute monthly QPU quota, 100-sample validation,
      simulator-to-hardware weight transfer without on-hardware
      retraining), the measured production accuracy is $6\,\%$,
      providing end-to-end pipeline validation while quantifying
      the resource requirements for competitive hardware performance.
      

  \item The quantum metrological gain for beyond-GR parameters ranges
        from $G_k = 1.75$ (LIV length scale) to $G_k = 2.23$
        (Chern-Simons coupling), yielding a $(1-\sqrt{1/G_k})\times100\,\%$ reduction in 
parameter uncertainty relative to matched filtering, 
ranging from $24\,\%$ (for $G_k=1.75$) to $33\,\%$ 
(for $G_k=2.23$).

  \item The QUBO quantum annealer finds log-posterior values within
        $0.3\pm0.4$\,nats of the global maximum on the 15-parameter
        LIGO likelihood surface, versus $1.4\pm1.2$\,nats for
        classical basin hopping, using $11\times$ fewer likelihood
        evaluations.

  \item No GWTC-3 event achieves Tier~A ($\ln B > 5$) in the QNIM
        analysis, constraining the prevalence of detectable
        quantum-gravity corrections at O3 sensitivity to $< 10\,\%$
        of the BBH population.

  \item The combined GWTC-3 analysis yields six new upper limits on
        beyond-GR parameters, including $m_g < 4.1\times10^{-24}$\,eV
        (graviton mass, $3.1\times$ improvement) and
        $\kappa_\mathrm{CS} < 1.8\times10^{-19}$\,m$^{-1}$
        (Chern-Simons, $2.8\times$ improvement).

  \item The Hubble constant is constrained to
        $H_0 = 68.3^{+8.1}_{-6.9}$\,km\,s$^{-1}$\,Mpc$^{-1}$ from
        13 GWTC-3 standard-siren events, consistent with both CMB
        and distance-ladder values.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Broader scientific context}
\label{sec:broader-context}

Theoretical predictions of Planck-scale corrections of order
$\ell_\mathrm{Pl}/r$ are vastly smaller than current experimental
sensitivity, yet QNIM demonstrates that quantum computers can extract
more information per event from existing data than classical algorithms.
As detector sensitivity improves from O4 to O5 to the Einstein Telescope
era, the accessible parameter volume grows and Planck-scale corrections
become comparatively larger.
QNIM provides an analytical infrastructure ready to exploit this improved
sensitivity as soon as the data arrive, without requiring methodological
changes.

A recurring objection to quantum machine learning is that a classical
algorithm can always be found that matches any specific quantum advantage;
the QNIM results provide a counterexample by demonstrating that the
quantum kernel genuinely resolves frequency-quadratic Chern-Simons phase
rotations that are inaccessible to any classical kernel in the same
12-dimensional feature space.
This is a concrete instance of quantum advantage grounded not in
asymptotic complexity arguments but in the physics of the problem,
supported by the tensor-network dequantisation analysis of
Appendix~\ref{app:dequantisation}.
The $5.9\sigma$--$8.7\sigma$ Wilcoxon significances of
Table~\ref{tab:significance} establish that these gains are not
marginal but represent a substantive improvement over classical baselines.


% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Future research directions}
\label{sec:future-work}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

The discussion of Chapter~\ref{chap:discussion} identified several open
questions. Table~\ref{tab:scalability-roadmap-ch9} summarises the hardware
milestones; the scientific priorities are:

\paragraph{Near-term (2026--2027).}
Apply QNIM to the full O4 dataset; validate the optimal $(d^\star,n^\star)$
configuration empirically; implement CDR error mitigation; run the Tutor Test
on an IBM~Heron~r2 surface-code patch; and retrain on SSTG events with
$M_\mathrm{tot}<100\,M_\odot$ to confirm or refute the GW190521
extra-dimensions assignment (Priority~1,
Section~\ref{subsubsec:gw190521-robustness}; estimated $\sim48$\,CPU-hours).

\paragraph{Medium-term (2027--2029).}
Develop the theory-agnostic VQAE anomaly-detection branch
(Section~\ref{sec:disc-open-questions}); extend the QUBO estimator to
25-dimensional precessing-spin BNS events via the D-Wave Hybrid CQM solver;
implement a joint classical-quantum convolutional encoder to replace linear PCA;
and apply quantum Shapley interpretability to the two Tier~B events.

\paragraph{Long-term (2030+).}
Redesign the VQC for fault-tolerant processors using quantum phase estimation
(QPE), which saturates the QCRB by construction; implement Grover-based
amplitude estimation for Heisenberg-limited scaling $\sigma_\theta\propto1/N$;
extend to the Einstein Telescope band ($1$--$10{,}000$\,Hz); and develop
multi-messenger integration with Fermi/Swift and Rubin Observatory.
The Berry--Keating--Connes conjecture \cite{Berry_Keating_1999,Connes_1999}
and its potential connection to QNM spectra is discussed in
Appendix~\ref{app:bkc-speculative}; a falsifiable empirical test would require
$\gtrsim10^4$ QNM overtone measurements achievable only with
third-generation detectors.

\subsection{Closing remark}
\label{subsec:closing}

We are at an early and exciting moment in the history of both gravitational-wave
astronomy and quantum computing. LIGO has opened a new observational window
on the universe; quantum processors are beginning to demonstrate capabilities
inaccessible to classical hardware. The QNIM methodology is one attempt to
bring these two revolutions into dialogue. The results presented here are
necessarily limited by the NISQ hardware of 2025--2026 and the 90-event
catalogue of GWTC-3. But the methodology is built to scale: with every
improvement in quantum hardware fidelity, every new gravitational-wave event,
and every tightening of our theoretical priors on quantum gravity, the QNIM
pipeline will extract proportionally more information from the universe.

The next decisive test will come with the O5 network, the Einstein Telescope,
and the first generation of fault-tolerant quantum processors. This thesis
has laid the methodological foundation for that test.

% APPENDIX A  BMS SYMMETRY, SUPERRADIANCE, AND SPECULATIVE PHYSICS
