\chapter{BMS symmetry, superradiance, and speculative physics}
\label{app:bms-superad}

This appendix collects theoretical material that, while physically rich and conceptually important, does not directly modulate the thirteen-class classification pipeline of QNIM. The topics include the infrared structure of asymptotically flat spacetimes, the continuous-wave signatures from bosonic condensates, and zeta-function regularisation techniques. They are presented here for completeness and for readers interested in the broader theoretical context.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Infrared structure of gravity: BMS symmetry and gravitational memory}
\label{app:sec:bms}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------
The asymptotic structure of gravitational radiation encodes symmetries far richer than the Poincaré group. This section reviews the Bondi--van der Burg--Metzner--Sachs group, its connection to soft graviton theorems and gravitational memory, and its relevance to the stochastic background floor modelled in QNIM Layer~7.
\subsection{The BMS group and supertranslations}
\label{app:subsec:bms-group}

The asymptotic symmetry group of asymptotically flat spacetimes at future null infinity $\mathcal{I}^+$ is not the Poincar\'{e} group but the much larger Bondi--van der Burg--Metzner--Sachs (BMS) group. The key discovery \cite{Strominger_2016} is that the spatial translations of the Poincar\'{e} group must be promoted to an infinite-dimensional family of \emph{supertranslations}---angle-dependent shifts of the retarded time $u$:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:supertranslation}
  u \to u + f(\theta,\phi),
  \quad f \in C^\infty(S^2).
\end{equation}
Each supertranslation maps one Minkowski vacuum to a physically inequivalent vacuum, leading to an infinite degeneracy of the ground state---a fact with profound implications for the information paradox.

When extended to include Virasoro \emph{superrotations} generated by $L_m$, the BMS Lie algebra acquires a central extension with central charge $c$:
\begin{align}
  \label{eq:bms_algebra}
  [L_m, L_n] &= (m-n)\,L_{m+n} + \frac{c}{12}\,m(m^2-1)\,\delta_{m+n,0}, \\
  [L_m, T_n] &= \left(\frac{m}{2}-n\right)T_{m+n}, \quad [T_m,T_n] = 0,
\end{align}
where $T_n$ are the supertranslation generators. The non-trivial commutator $[L_m, T_n]$ couples rotations to supertranslations, producing the \emph{spin memory effect} detectable as a net rotation of polarisation axes after a gravitational-wave burst has passed.

\subsection{Strominger's infrared triangle}
\label{app:subsec:infrared-triangle}

Strominger \cite{Strominger_2016} demonstrated an exact equivalence between three apparently unrelated phenomena in gravity and quantum field theory. This ``infrared triangle'' connects:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{BMS supertranslation symmetry}: the Ward identities of supertranslations impose constraints on the soft sector of graviton scattering amplitudes.
  \item \textbf{Weinberg's soft graviton theorem}: in any scattering amplitude, the emission of a graviton with momentum $q \to 0$ factorises from the hard amplitude by a universal factor $\mathcal{S}^{(0)}$ (leading) $+ \mathcal{S}^{(1)}$ (subleading), related to the angular momentum of the hard particles.
  \item \textbf{Gravitational memory}: a burst of gravitational radiation produces a permanent displacement of test masses after the wave has passed:
        \begin{equation}
          \label{eq:memory}
          \Delta h_{ij} = h_{ij}(+\infty) - h_{ij}(-\infty) \neq 0.
        \end{equation}
\end{enumerate}

The gravitational memory effect, predicted independently by Christodoulou \cite{Christodoulou_1991}, is a non-linear effect sourced by the energy flux carried to infinity by the gravitational waves themselves. It produces a DC offset in the detector output that grows with the total radiated energy and is the primary observable for QNIM's memory-layer injection protocol.

\subsection{Hawking's soft hair and black-hole information}
\label{app:subsec:soft-hair}

Hawking, Perry, and Strominger \cite{Hawking_2016} proposed that the degeneracy of BMS vacua resolves part of the black-hole information paradox. As matter falls into a black hole, it leaves imprints in the form of \emph{soft hair}---zero-energy ($q\to0$) graviton and photon excitations on the horizon that carry information about the infalling matter. In principle, this information could be retrieved from correlations in the Hawking radiation spectrum, manifesting as subtle corrections to the thermal spectrum detectable as anomalies in the late-time ringdown tail. QNIM injects these sub-Hz corrections as a DC floor in the stochastic background model of Layer~7.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Superradiance and continuous-wave signatures}
\label{app:sec:superradiance}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------
Rotating black holes can amplify bosonic field perturbations through superradiance, and when the boson mass places its Compton wavelength near the gravitational radius, a quasi-bound condensate forms that radiates monochromatic gravitational waves. This section reviews the instability mechanism, the resulting continuous-wave signal, and the spin-gap constraints encoded in the GWTC-3 population.
\subsection{The Penrose process and superradiant instability}
\label{app:subsec:penrose}

For a Kerr black hole with angular velocity $\Omega_H = ac/(2Mr_+)$ (where $r_+ = M + \sqrt{M^2-a^2}$ is the outer horizon radius), a bosonic wave with frequency $\omega$ and azimuthal number $m$ is amplified by the black hole---\emph{superradiance}---if:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:penrose_condition}
  \omega < m\,\Omega_H.
\end{equation}
When the boson has a mass $\mu_b$ such that its Compton wavelength $\bar{\lambda}_C = \hbar/(m_b c)$ is comparable to the gravitational radius $r_g = GM/c^2$, it becomes quasi-bound. The dimensionless coupling $\alpha = r_g / \bar{\lambda}_C = G M m_b / (\hbar c)$ governs the instability rate \cite{Arvanitaki_2011}:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:superradiance_rate}
  \Gamma_\mathrm{SR} \approx
    \left(\frac{m+1}{n+l+1}\right)^{4l+5}\alpha^{4l+5}\omega_R,
\end{equation}
where $l$ and $n$ are the angular momentum and radial quantum numbers of the quasi-bound state. The growing bosonic occupation number extracts angular momentum from the black hole until either the Penrose condition is saturated or the boson annihilates.

\subsection{Axion clouds and monochromatic gravitational waves}
\label{app:subsec:axion-clouds}

If the boson is an axion---a pseudo-Nambu--Goldstone boson arising from the Peccei--Quinn symmetry-breaking mechanism---the axionic cloud radiates monochromatic gravitational waves at twice the boson mass \cite{Brito_2015}:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:cw_frequency}
  f_\mathrm{CW} = \frac{2 m_b c^2}{h}
    \left(1 - \frac{\alpha^2}{2n^2} + \ldots\right),
\end{equation}
where the correction terms arise from gravitational self-interaction. For LIGO/Virgo sensitivities ($f \in [10, 2000]$\,Hz), the sensitive mass window is $m_b \in [10^{-13}, 10^{-11}]$\,eV. The accumulated duration of the signal can reach $10^4$--$10^6$ years, making continuous-wave (CW) searches with long coherent integration times the primary detection strategy.

\subsection{Spin gaps in the GWTC-3 catalogue}
\label{app:subsec:spin-gaps}

If axion superradiance is efficient for a given mass $m_b$, it will spin down all Kerr black holes with $\alpha \sim 0.1$--$0.5$ to below the Penrose threshold. Observationally, this predicts \emph{spin gaps} in the distribution of black hole spins: bands in the $(M, \chi_f)$ plane where rapidly rotating black holes should be absent \cite{Arvanitaki_2011}. Analysis of the GWTC-3 catalogue reveals tentative evidence for such gaps near $m_b \approx 3\times10^{-13}$\,eV. QNIM's Layer~5 population module incorporates these spin-gap priors as a Bayesian weight in the theory ranking, promoting axion models that are compatible with the observed spin distribution.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Scattering amplitudes and zeta-function regularisation}
\label{app:sec:amplitudes-zeta}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------
Two mathematical tools drawn from quantum field theory underpin the speculative Layer~7 physics of QNIM: the Veneziano amplitude, which encodes the string-theoretic extension of graviton scattering, and zeta-function regularisation, which provides the rigorous foundation for the Casimir energy floor injected into the stochastic background model.
\subsection{Veneziano amplitude and graviton scattering}
\label{app:subsec:veneziano}

At trans-Planckian energies, point-particle quantum field theory breaks down and must be replaced by a framework in which the fundamental objects are extended. String theory provides such a framework; its four-graviton scattering amplitude in the $s$--$t$ channel is given by the \emph{Veneziano amplitude}:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:veneziano}
  \mathcal{M}(s,t) \propto
    \frac{\Gamma\!\left(-\alpha' s/4\right)
          \Gamma\!\left(-\alpha' t/4\right)}
         {\Gamma\!\left(2 + \alpha'(s+t)/4\right)},
\end{equation}
where $\alpha'$ is the Regge slope (inverse string tension) and $s,t$ are the Mandelstam variables. The poles of the Gamma functions correspond to the excitation spectrum of the underlying quantum string: a tower of massive resonances at $M_n^2 = 4n/\alpha'$. In the QNIM quantum feature map, this resonance structure is used as a calibration template for the Chebyshev feature encoding of the ringdown spectrum.

\subsection{Zeta-function regularisation of the energy-momentum tensor}
\label{app:subsec:zeta-regularisation}

In quantum field theory in curved spacetime, the vacuum expectation value $\langle T_{\mu\nu}\rangle$ is formally divergent and requires regularisation. Zeta-function regularisation \cite{Hawking_1975} defines the functional determinant of a differential operator $\hat{A}$ with positive eigenvalues $\{\lambda_n\}$ through the spectral zeta function:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:spectral_zeta}
  \zeta_{\hat{A}}(s) = \sum_{n=1}^\infty \lambda_n^{-s},
  \qquad \ln\det\hat{A} = -\zeta'_{\hat{A}}(0).
\end{equation}

The critical special values of the Riemann zeta function are:
\begin{align}
  \label{eq:zeta_values}
  \zeta(-1) &= -\tfrac{1}{12} & &\text{(1D Casimir / string tension),} \\
  \zeta(-2) &= 0,             & &                                        \\
  \zeta(-3) &= \tfrac{1}{120} & &\text{(4D vacuum energy correction).}
\end{align}

The renormalised Casimir energy for a scalar field with UV cutoff $\omega_c$ therefore evaluates to:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:casimir_reg}
  E_0^\mathrm{reg} = \frac{\hbar\omega_c}{2}\,\zeta(-1)
    = -\frac{\hbar\omega_c}{24},
\end{equation}
providing the stochastic-background floor injected in Layer~7.

\textit{Note.} A formal mathematical analogy between the non-trivial zeros of the Riemann zeta function and quasi-normal mode spectra---based on the Berry--Keating--Connes conjecture \cite{Berry_Keating_1999,Connes_1999}---has been removed from the theoretical framework and placed in Appendix~\ref{app:bkc-speculative}, where its speculative status and lack of experimental grounding are explicitly flagged. No result in this thesis depends on that analogy.

The third-order WKB approximation relates the complex frequency to the
peak $V_0$ and curvature $V_0''$ of the effective potential:
\[
\omega^2 \approx V_0 - i\left(n+\frac{1}{2}\right)\sqrt{-2V_0''}
  + \mathcal{O}\!\left((n+\tfrac{1}{2})^2\right).
\]

For the dominant $(l=m=2,n=0)$ mode, the Berti--Cardoso--Will fitting
relations are
\[
f_\mathrm{QNM} = \frac{1}{2\pi t_M}

\qquad
Q_\mathrm{QNM} = 0.700 + 1.4187\,(1-\chi_f)^{-0.4990},
\]
with $t_M = GM_f/c^3$.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Auxiliary PN and EOB expressions}
\label{app:pn-eob-tools}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

This section gathers the spin-orbit, tidal, and Pad\'e-resummation
expressions referenced compactly in
Chapter~\ref{chap:theoretical-framework}.

The 1.5PN spin-orbit coefficient is
\[
\beta_\mathrm{SO} = \frac{1}{12}\sum_{i=1}^{2}
  \left(113\frac{m_i^2}{M^2} + 75\eta\right)
  \hat{L}\cdot\boldsymbol{\chi}_i.
\]

The binary tidal deformability is
\[
\tilde{\Lambda} = \frac{16}{13}\frac{(m_1+12m_2)m_1^4\Lambda_1
  + (m_2+12m_1)m_2^4\Lambda_2}{(m_1+m_2)^5}.
\]

The EOB Pad\'e potential used in the main text is
\[
A^{P^4_1}(u) = \frac{1 - 2u + \nu_1 u^3 + \nu_2 u^4}
  {1 + \nu_3 u + \nu_4 u^2},
\qquad u = \frac{GM}{rc^2}.
\]

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Chapter 3 mathematical supplements}
\label{app:chap3-math}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

The full design rationale for $n=12$ qubits combines five simultaneous
constraints: PCA dimensionality matching ($d=12$), NISQ coherence and
circuit-depth limits, trainability under barren-plateau constraints,
IBM hardware topology and routing overhead, and negligible marginal
variance beyond the twelfth PCA component.

The chain-rule bridge between circuit-space and physical-parameter QFI
follows the preprocessing pipeline:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:preprocessing_chain}
  \theta_k \; \longrightarrow \; \tilde{h}(f;\theta_k) \; \longrightarrow \;
  \text{STFT} \; \longrightarrow \; \text{PCA} \; \longrightarrow \;
  \mathbf{x}_\mathrm{PCA}(\theta_k),
\end{equation}
and the physical-parameter QFI is obtained by the contraction:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:qfi_physical}
  \mathcal{F}_{Q,k}^\mathrm{phys} = \sum_{i,j=1}^{12}
    \mathcal{F}_{Q,ij}^\mathrm{VQC}
    \frac{\partial x_i}{\partial \theta_k}
    \frac{\partial x_j}{\partial \theta_k},
\end{equation}
with finite-difference Jacobians:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:jacobian_fd}
  \frac{\partial x_i}{\partial \theta_k} \approx
    \frac{x_i(\theta_k + \delta\theta_k) - x_i(\theta_k - \delta\theta_k)}
         {2\delta\theta_k},
  \qquad \delta\theta_k = 0.05 \times \theta_k^{\mathrm{MAP}}.
\end{equation}

Table~\ref{tab:qfi-jacobian} provides numerical values for all fifteen
parameters at a representative event (GW150914 MAP). The full
$15\times15$ quantum geometric tensor $\mathcal{F}_{Q,ij}^\mathrm{VQC}$
is plotted in Figure~\ref{fig:qgt-matrix} as a heatmap; its eigenvalue
spectrum is dominated by $\sim\!4$ large eigenvalues ($>10^{-2}$)
corresponding to the physically meaningful parameter directions (chirp
mass, mass ratio, effective spin, primary beyond-GR coupling).

$\ln d_L$ (lum.\ distance)        & 0.031 & $1.18\times10^2$ & $1.24\times10^2$ & 0.95 \\
$\cos\iota$ (inclination)         & 0.028 & $8.63\times10^1$ & $9.17\times10^1$ & 0.94 \\
$\alpha$ (RA)                     & 0.022 & $4.21\times10^0$ & $4.18\times10^0$ & 1.01 \\
$\delta$ (Dec)                    & 0.020 & $3.88\times10^0$ & $3.91\times10^0$ & 0.99 \\
$\Lambda_\mathrm{tidal}$          & 0.015 & $1.62\times10^{-1}$ & $1.72\times10^{-1}$ & 0.94 \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{5}{c}{\textbf{Beyond-GR parameters}} \\
$\kappa_\mathrm{CS}$ (Chern--Simons)     & \textbf{0.198} & \textbf{$3.47\times10^2$} & \textbf{$1.56\times10^2$} & \textbf{2.23} \\
$\beta$ (Lorentz inv.\ violation)        & 0.082 & $6.81\times10^1$ & $4.24\times10^1$ & 1.61 \\
$\delta_Q$ (quadrupole deviation)        & 0.091 & $8.12\times10^1$ & $5.93\times10^1$ & 1.37 \\
$R_c$ (compactification radius)          & \textbf{0.156} & \textbf{$2.18\times10^2$} & \textbf{$9.78\times10^1$} & \textbf{2.23} \\
$\lambda_A$ (axion mass scale)           & 0.087 & $7.34\times10^1$ & $4.89\times10^1$ & 1.50 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The parameters in \textbf{bold} ($\kappa_\mathrm{CS}$ and $R_c$) achieve
the maximum metrological gain ($G_k = 2.23$). Their large Jacobian norms
reflect the fact that their characteristic phase modulations
(frequency-quadratic and frequency-linear, respectively) concentrate power
in a small subset of the STFT bins, which project strongly onto the leading
PCA components. Standard GR parameters show modest gains
($G_k \in [1.01, 1.10]$) because their phase corrections spread across all
frequencies and matched filtering is already near-optimal for linearly
encoded parameters.

\begin{figure}[ht]
\centering

  (darker $\Rightarrow$ larger $|\mathcal{F}_{Q,ij}|$).
  (\emph{Right}) Pearson correlation coefficients

Positive-semidefiniteness checks and degeneracy-verification diagnostics
confirm that the contracted tensor preserves the standard
gravitational-wave degeneracy structure while improving sensitivity to
orthogonal beyond-GR parameter directions.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{The Fisher information matrix}
\label{app:fim}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------
The Fisher Information Matrix is the fundamental object connecting the
geometry of parameter space to the precision of any unbiased estimator.
This section collects the classical and quantum definitions used throughout
the thesis, together with the Cram\'{e}r--Rao hierarchy that establishes
the quantum bound as provably tighter than its classical counterpart.

\subsection{Classical FIM}
\label{app:cfim}

Let $p(\mathbf{d}|\boldsymbol{\theta})$ be the likelihood of data
$\mathbf{d}$ given parameters $\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^p$.
The classical Fisher Information Matrix (FIM) is:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:cfim_def}
  [\mathcal{F}_C]_{jk} = \mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{d}|\boldsymbol{\theta}}\!\left[
    \frac{\partial\ln p}{\partial\theta_j}
    \frac{\partial\ln p}{\partial\theta_k}
  \right]
  = -\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{d}|\boldsymbol{\theta}}\!\left[
    \frac{\partial^2\ln p}{\partial\theta_j\partial\theta_k}
  \right].
\end{equation}
For a Gaussian likelihood with noise-weighted inner product
$(a,b)=a^\top\Sigma^{-1}b$:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:cfim_gaussian}
  [\mathcal{F}_C]_{jk} = \left(
    \frac{\partial h}{\partial\theta_j},
    \frac{\partial h}{\partial\theta_k}
  \right).
\end{equation}
In the frequency domain with one-sided PSD $S_n(f)$:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:cfim_freq}
  [\mathcal{F}_C]_{jk} = 4\,\mathrm{Re}
    \int_0^\infty \frac{\tilde{h}_{,j}^*(f)\,\tilde{h}_{,k}(f)}{S_n(f)}\,df.
\end{equation}

\subsection{Quantum FIM and the Symmetric Logarithmic Derivative}
\label{app:qfim}

For a parametric density matrix $\rho(\boldsymbol{\theta})$, the Quantum
FIM is defined via the Symmetric Logarithmic Derivative (SLD) $L_k$:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:sld_def}
  \frac{\partial\rho}{\partial\theta_k} =
    \frac{1}{2}(L_k\rho + \rho L_k),
  \qquad
  \mathcal{F}_{Q,k} = \mathrm{Tr}(\rho L_k^2).
\end{equation}
For a pure state $\rho=|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|$, this reduces to
Eq.~(\ref{eq:qfi_vqc}).

\subsection{Cram\'{e}r--Rao bound hierarchy}
\label{app:crb-hierarchy}

\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:crb_hierarchy}
  \mathrm{Var}(\hat{\theta}_k) \geq
  \underbrace{\left[\mathcal{F}_C^{-1}\right]_{kk}}_\text{classical CRB}
  \geq
  \underbrace{\mathcal{F}_{Q,k}^{-1}}_\text{QCRB}.
\end{equation}
The left inequality is the classical Cram\'{e}r--Rao inequality; the right
is the Holevo bound, tight when the SLD operators commute. For the
beyond-GR parameters of the SSTG, $[L_j,L_k]\neq0$, so the QCRB is not
simultaneously achievable for all parameters; the achievable bound is the
Holevo--Cram\'{e}r--Rao bound.
The bipartite von Neumann entropy used to characterise entanglement 
capacity is:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:vne}
  \bar{S}_\mathrm{vN} = \mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\!\left[
    -\mathrm{Tr}\!\left(\rho_A \ln\rho_A\right)
  \right],
  \quad
  \rho_A = \mathrm{Tr}_B\!\left(|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|\right).
\end{equation}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Fubini--Study metric and quantum geometry}
\label{app:fubini-study}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

The Fubini--Study metric on the space of pure quantum states is:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:fs_metric}
  ds^2 = \sum_{j,k} g_{jk}^{FS}\,d\theta_j\,d\theta_k,
  \qquad
  g_{jk}^{FS} = \mathrm{Re}\!\left(
    \langle\partial_j\psi|\partial_k\psi\rangle
    - \langle\partial_j\psi|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|\partial_k\psi\rangle
  \right).
\end{equation}
The Quantum Geometric Tensor (QGT) is the complex extension:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:qgt_def}
  T_{jk} = \langle\partial_j\psi|\partial_k\psi\rangle
          - \langle\partial_j\psi|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|\partial_k\psi\rangle,
\end{equation}
with $g_{jk}^{FS}=\mathrm{Re}(T_{jk})$ and Berry curvature
$F_{jk}=-2\,\mathrm{Im}(T_{jk})$. The QFI relates to the metric by
$\mathcal{F}_{Q,k}=4\,g_{kk}^{FS}$.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Bures metric and density matrices}
\label{app:bures}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

For mixed states $\rho$, the Bures metric is:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:bures}
  ds_\mathrm{Bures}^2 = \frac{1}{2}\sum_{j,k}
    \mathrm{Tr}\!\left(L_j\rho\,L_k + L_k\rho\,L_j\right)
    d\theta_j\,d\theta_k
  = \frac{1}{2}\sum_{j,k}\mathcal{F}_{Q,jk}\,d\theta_j\,d\theta_k.
\end{equation}
Under the NISQ noise model
$\rho(\boldsymbol{\theta})=\mathcal{E}(|\psi(\boldsymbol{\theta})\rangle
\langle\psi(\boldsymbol{\theta})|)$,
the Bures QFI is strictly smaller than the ideal pure-state QFI,
explaining the accuracy degradation documented in
Section~\ref{subsec:fair-comparison}.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Quantum kernel theory}
\label{app:kernel-theory}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

\subsection{Mercer representation}
\label{app:mercer}

The quantum kernel $k_Q(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}')$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:qk_inner})
is continuous, symmetric, and positive semi-definite. By Mercer's theorem:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:mercer}
  k_Q(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}') = \sum_{m=1}^\infty
    \mu_m\,\phi_m(\mathbf{x})\,\phi_m(\mathbf{x}'),
\end{equation}
with non-negative eigenvalues $\{\mu_m\}$ and eigenfunctions
$\{\phi_m\}\subset L^2(\mathcal{X})$.

\subsection{VC dimension and generalisation}
\label{app:vc-dim}

The VC dimension of the QNIM classifier is bounded by $p+1=49$.
By the PAC-learning bound:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:pac_bound}
  \epsilon_\mathrm{gen} \leq \sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{VC}\cdot\ln(2N/\mathrm{VC})
    + \ln(1/\delta)}{N}},
\end{equation}
with probability $\geq 1-\delta$. For $N=54{,}600$, $p=48$, $\delta=0.05$:
$\epsilon_\mathrm{gen}\leq 0.029$ (2.9\,\%), consistent with the measured
training--validation gap of $<0.5\,\%$.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Classical evaluation costs for the quantum kernel}
\label{app:kernel-cost}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

This section provides the formal cost analysis replacing the informal
$\mathcal{O}(2^n)$ statement of Section~\ref{sec:qfm}. We derive the cost
of three classical strategies for evaluating
$k_Q(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}')=|\langle\mathbf{0}|
\mathcal{U}_\phi^\dagger(\mathbf{x}')\mathcal{U}_\phi(\mathbf{x})
|\mathbf{0}\rangle|^2$
for the QNIM ZZFeatureMap ($n=12$, $r_\phi=2$).

\subsection{Strategy 1: statevector simulation}
\label{app:cost-statevector}

Full statevector simulation costs $\mathcal{O}(r_\phi n\cdot 2^n)$ per
kernel pair. For $n=12$, $r_\phi=2$: $C_\mathrm{SV}(12)\approx10^5$
FLOP per pair. The full $N\times N$ Gram matrix ($N=78{,}000$) requires
$\approx3\times10^{14}$ FLOP and 49\,TB memory---memory-bound for any
single node.

\subsection{Strategy 2: MPS simulation}
\label{app:cost-mps}

MPS overlap evaluation costs $\mathcal{O}(n\chi^3)$ per pair
\cite{Schreiber_2024_PRR}. At the critical bond dimension
$\chi_\mathrm{crit}=2^{n/2}=64$:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:cost_mps_n12}
  C_\mathrm{MPS}(12,64) = \mathcal{O}(12\times64^3)
    \approx 3.1\times10^6~\text{FLOP per pair},
\end{equation}
giving $\approx9\times10^{15}$ FLOP and 49\,TB for the full Gram matrix.
At $n=20$, $\chi_\mathrm{crit}=1024$ and the cost grows by $\sim\!6000\times$.

\subsection{Strategy 3: Fourier random features}
\label{app:cost-rff}

The RFF approximation \cite{Rahimi_2007} approximates the kernel as
$\hat{k}_M(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}')=\mathbf{z}(\mathbf{x})^\top
\mathbf{z}(\mathbf{x}')$ with $M$ random features. Exact approximation
to error $\varepsilon=0.01$ over the training set requires:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:rff_M_qnim}
  M \geq \frac{4}{\varepsilon^2}\ln\frac{N(N-1)}{\delta}
    \approx 1.02\times10^6,
\end{equation}
costing $\approx6\times10^{15}$ FLOP and 624\,GB memory---comparable to
MPS and not tractable on a single workstation. The C-QK baseline uses
$M=10{,}000$, which guarantees error $\varepsilon\leq10.1\,\%$ across
the full dataset (Eq.~\ref{eq:rff_error_m1e4}).

\subsection{Comparative summary}
\label{app:cost-summary}

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\small
\caption[Cost comparison of classical kernel strategies]{Cost comparison of three classical strategies for computing
         the QNIM quantum kernel ($N=78{,}000$, $n=12$, $r_\phi=2$).}
\label{tab:kernel-cost}
\resizebox{\textwidth}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{@{}lllll@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Strategy} & \textbf{Per-pair cost} &
\textbf{Gram FLOP} & \textbf{Memory} & \textbf{Exact?} \\
\midrule
Statevector & $\mathcal{O}(n\cdot2^n)$ & $3\times10^{14}$ & 49\,TB & Yes \\
MPS $\chi=64$ & $\mathcal{O}(n\chi^3)\approx3\times10^6$ & $9\times10^{15}$ & 49\,TB & Yes \\
RFF $M=10^4$ & $\mathcal{O}(Md)=1.2\times10^5$ & $6\times10^{13}$ & $<1$\,GB & No ($\varepsilon\leq10.1\,\%$) \\
RFF $M=10^6$ & $\mathcal{O}(Md)=1.2\times10^7$ & $6\times10^{15}$ & 624\,GB & No ($\varepsilon\leq1.0\,\%$) \\
\midrule

\chapter{Software architecture, implementation, and reproducibility}
\label{app:software-architecture}

This appendix provides the complete technical documentation of the QNIM
software architecture described in Chapter~\ref{chap:software}, including
the domain model, infrastructure adapters, deployment procedures, and
reproducibility protocol.

\section{The QNSPSA-EML-Feynman optimization algorithm}
\label{app:qnspsa}
\label{app:qnspsa-section}
This section provides the complete technical specification of the QNSPSA-EML-Feynman optimiser, including its pseudocode, convergence guarantees under the Robbins--Monro conditions, and the energy efficiency analysis that quantifies the computational cost of hardware execution.
\section{Pseudocode}
\label{app:qnspsa-pseudocode}

Algorithm~\ref{alg:qnspsa} gives the complete pseudocode of the
QNSPSA-EML-Feynman optimiser as implemented in the QNIM codebase
(\texttt{src/domain/services/qnspsa\_eml\_feynman.py}).

\begin{figure}[ht]
\begin{verbatim}
Input: theta_0, schedules {a_t}, {c_t}, ZNE factors, lambda_R
Output: theta*

For t = 0, 1, ..., T-1:
  Sample Delta_t, Delta'_t in {-1,+1}^p
  For each lambda_k in {1,2,3}:
    f+ = eval_circuit(theta_t + c_t*Delta_t, lambda_k)
    f- = eval_circuit(theta_t - c_t*Delta_t, lambda_k)
  g_t = sum_k c_k*(f+ - f-)/(2*c_t*Delta_t)   % ZNE gradient
  Repeat with Delta'_t to get g'_t
  F_t = (g_t g_t^T + g'_t g'^T_t) / 2
  H^{-1}_{t+1} = H^{-1}_t - H^{-1}_t F_t H^{-1}_t / (1 + Tr(H^{-1}_t F_t))
  R_t = curvature_regulariser(theta_t)
  g_total = g_t + lambda_R * grad(R_t)
  theta_{t+1} = theta_t - a_t * H^{-1}_{t+1} * g_total
Return theta_T
\end{verbatim}
\caption[QNSPSA-EML-Feynman optimiser pseudocode]{QNSPSA-EML-Feynman optimiser pseudocode.}\label{alg:qnspsa}
\end{figure}

\section{Convergence guarantees}
\label{app:qnspsa-convergence}

Under the following standard assumptions:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item $f(\boldsymbol{\theta})$ is $L$-smooth (Lipschitz gradient).
  \item The stochastic gradient estimate is unbiased: $\mathbb{E}[\hat{g}_t]
        = \nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_t) + \mathcal{O}(c_t^2)$.
  \item The schedules satisfy $\sum_t a_t = \infty$,
        $\sum_t a_t^2/c_t^2 < \infty$ (RobbinsMonro conditions).
\end{enumerate}
The QNSPSA-EML-Feynman iterates satisfy:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:qnspsa_convergence}
  \lim_{T\to\infty} \mathbb{E}\!\left[
    \min_{t\leq T}\|\nabla f(\boldsymbol{\theta}_t)\|^2
  \right] = 0,
\end{equation}
i.e.\ the algorithm converges to a stationary point of $f$ almost surely.
The convergence rate under the schedule $a_t = a_0/(t+1)^{0.602}$ is
$\mathcal{O}(T^{-0.398})$ for the gradient norm.

The ZNE correction introduces a bias of order $c_t^{2K}$ in the gradient
(where $K = 3$ is the Richardson order), which decays faster than any
schedule-induced variance term and does not affect the asymptotic
convergence.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering

         for various qubit scales, with and without the
         QNSPSA-EML-Feynman mitigation.}
\label{tab:barren-compare}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
\toprule
$n$ & \textbf{Raw SPSA} & \textbf{QNSPSA-EML-Feynman} &
\textbf{Trainable?} \\
\midrule
8  & $2.5\times10^{-3}$ & $6.2\times10^{-2}$ & Yes \\
12 & $6.1\times10^{-4}$ & $1.6\times10^{-2}$ & Yes \\

27 & $6.7\times10^{-6}$ & $1.7\times10^{-4}$ & Yes (with EML) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Green computing and energy efficiency}
\label{app:sec:green_computing}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

\subsection{Test taxonomy}
\label{subsec:test_taxonomy}

The QNIM test suite follows the standard three-tier taxonomy:

\begin{description}
  \item[Unit tests] (\texttt{test/unit/}): test individual domain objects
    and domain services in complete isolation. No network, no quantum
    hardware, no filesystem. Execution time: $< 30$\,s for the full suite.

  \item[Integration tests] (\texttt{test/integration/}): test the interaction
    between adapters and their external dependencies using locally
    running stubs (e.g., a local SQLite database, the Qiskit
    \texttt{AerSimulator}).

  \item[End-to-end tests] (\texttt{test/e2e/}): test the full pipeline from
    CLI command to output file. Run against the simulator backend by
    default; tagged \texttt{@pytest.mark.hardware} for optional execution
    against real hardware.
\end{description}

\subsection{Mutation testing}
\label{subsec:mutation_testing}

Domain logic is verified using mutation testing with \texttt{mutmut}.
Mutations are applied to the domain and application layers only (not
infrastructure, which is covered by contract tests). The target mutation
score is $\geq 90\,\%$. At the time of writing, the achieved mutation
score for the domain layer is $92.4\,\%$ and for the application layer
$88.7\,\%$.

Quantum processors operate at millikelvin temperatures, requiring dilution
refrigerators with a static power consumption of $\sim\!10$\,kW per unit,
independent of circuit execution. The effective energy per circuit execution,
amortised over a 24-hour operational day with 40\% queue utilisation, is:

\begin{equation}
  E_\mathrm{circuit} \approx \frac{10\,\mathrm{kW} \times 1\,\mathrm{ms}}
                               {10^5 \cdot 0.4} = 0.25\,\mu\mathrm{J/circuit}.
\end{equation}

For a full QNIM training run ($N_\mathrm{step} = 300$ steps, 8 evaluations
per step, 8192 shots per evaluation):

\begin{equation}
  E_\mathrm{train} \approx 300 \times 8 \times 8192 \times 0.25\,\mu\mathrm{J}
  \approx 4.9\,\mathrm{kJ}.
\end{equation}

QNIM implements four mitigations:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Transpilation caching}: pre-compiled circuits reused
        ($\sim\!12\%$ reduction in circuit count).

  \item \textbf{Adaptive shot allocation}: gradient evaluations use fewer
        shots ($\sim\!28\%$ reduction in total shots).

  \item \textbf{Early stopping with patience}: training halts when validation
        loss plateaus ($\sim\!18\%$ fewer steps).

  \item \textbf{Simulator-first policy}: all development and debugging on
        classical simulators; hardware reserved for final validation
        ($\sim\!80\%$ fewer hardware executions).
\end{enumerate}

Combined effect: approximately $56\%$ reduction in energy cost per training run
(from $\sim\!11.2$\,kJ to $\sim\!4.9$\,kJ).

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Domain model and repository interfaces}
\label{app:sec:domain-model}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

The domain layer declares five core value objects:
\texttt{Strain} (frequency-domain strain with metadata),
\texttt{FeatureVector} (12-dimensional PCA projection),
\texttt{QuantumCircuitSpec} (VQC hyperparameters),
\texttt{QuboMatrix} (QUBO problem specification),
and \texttt{InferenceResult} (classifier output with reliability tier).

Three persistent entities track state:
\texttt{GravitationalWaveEvent} (aggregate root for events),
\texttt{ExperimentRun} (execution history for reproducibility),
and \texttt{ModelWeights} (trained parameters with content hash).

Four domain services implement business logic:
\texttt{StrainPreprocessor} (whitening, STFT, feature extraction),
\texttt{QuboBuilder} (QUBO matrix construction from likelihoods),
\texttt{PlanckReliabilityClassifier} (event reliability tiers),
and \texttt{MetrologyAuditor} (quantum advantage quantification).

Repository interfaces (ports) declare contracts:
\texttt{IEventRepository} for event storage,
\texttt{IModelRepository} for weight management,
and concrete implementations (GWOSC HTTP client, local filesystem, SQLite)
live in the infrastructure layer.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Infrastructure adapters}
\label{app:sec:adapters}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------
The hexagonal architecture of QNIM isolates scientific logic from technology through adapter implementations. This section documents the three primary adapters---IBM Quantum, D-Wave, and GWOSC---together with the polyglot persistence strategy that manages model weights, results, and metadata.
\subsection{IBM Quantum adapter via Qiskit}
The adapter uses Qiskit \texttt{EstimatorV2} primitives with:
(1) session batching (one session per training run),
(2) transpilation caching (compile once, reuse),
(3) adaptive shot allocation (8192 shots for function evaluations,
1024 for gradients, 512 for ZNE copies),
(4) error mitigation via T-REX delegated to IBM Runtime.

Circuit compilation to \texttt{ibm\_fez} (133-qubit Heron) achieves:
CNOT count $N_\mathrm{CX} = 88$,
circuit depth $D_\mathrm{native} = 31$,
two-qubit gate error $\epsilon_{2Q} \approx 3.8\times10^{-3}$,
total fidelity $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ} \approx 0.714$.

\subsection{D-Wave adapter via Ocean SDK}
The adapter translates \texttt{QuboMatrix} to Ocean's
\texttt{BinaryQuadraticModel}, solves with
\texttt{neal.SimulatedAnnealingSampler}, and emulates annealer noise via
Gaussian perturbations ($\sigma_Q = 0.03$, 24 noisy realisations per solve).
Outputs include ensemble mode, parameter standard deviations, and
selection stability.

\subsection{GWOSC client}
Downloads strain data from \texttt{gwosc.org} via \texttt{gwpy}, implements
HTTP retry (exponential backoff, max 5 attempts), local disk caching, and
SHA-256 verification against GWOSC checksums.

\subsection{Polyglot persistence}
\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Model weights}: NumPy \texttt{.npy} or HDF5 files,
        content-addressed by SHA-256 hash, stored in \texttt{models/}.
  \item \textbf{Results}: CSV files with fixed schema in \texttt{reports/},
        ASCII-compatible column names.
  \item \textbf{Metadata}: SQLite database at \texttt{data/qnim.db},
        schema managed by \texttt{alembic} migrations.
  \item \textbf{Logs}: JSON-formatted, rotated daily, retained 30 days.
\end{itemize}

\section{Experiment reproducibility protocol}
\label{app:sec:repro}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

Every \texttt{ExperimentRun} stores:
\begin{itemize}
  \item Git commit hash of QNIM source.
  \item SHA-256 hashes of \texttt{ModelWeights}, input data files.
  \item Random seeds for all stochastic operations
        (\texttt{numpy}, \texttt{random}, Qiskit transpiler).
  \item Backend calibration snapshot (hardware runs).
\end{itemize}

This metadata is persisted in the \texttt{ExperimentRun} SQLite record and
reproduced in the HTML dashboard, enabling independent verification.

\textbf{Code availability}: The complete QNIM source, model weights, and
experiment configuration are version-controlled at
\url{https://github.com/oscarboullosa/qnim}, Git tag \texttt{v1.0.0}
(commit \texttt{d4e7f3a}). To reproduce any result:

\begin{verbatim}
git clone https://github.com/oscarboullosa/qnim
git checkout v1.0.0
pip install -r requirements.lock.txt
# Follow README.md and REPRODUCTION.md for execution
\end{verbatim}

Dependencies are fully pinned in \texttt{requirements.lock.txt} (including
transitive and hash verification), and a Docker image with the complete
runtime environment is provided for bit-for-bit reproducibility across
platforms.

% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
% APPENDIX D  IBM QUANTUM DEPLOYMENT GUIDE
% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\chapter{IBM Quantum deployment guide}
\label{app:ibm-guide}

This appendix describes the procedure for deploying and executing the
QNIM pipeline on IBM Quantum hardware.

\section{Prerequisites and account setup}
\label{app:ibm-prerequisites}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item Create an IBM Quantum account at \texttt{quantum.ibm.com} and
        retrieve the API token from the account dashboard.  Do
        \textbf{not} paste the token into any file; store it only as an
        environment variable (see step~3 below).
  \item Install the QNIM dependencies:
        \texttt{pip install -r requirements.lock.txt}.
  \item Set the \texttt{IBM\_QUANTUM\_TOKEN} environment variable
        \textbf{before} running any QNIM command.  Never hard-code the
        token in source files or configuration committed to version control:
        \begin{verbatim}
# bash / zsh  (add to ~/.bashrc or ~/.zshrc for persistence)
export IBM_QUANTUM_TOKEN="paste-your-token-here"
        \end{verbatim}
        On Windows (PowerShell):
        \begin{verbatim}
$Env:IBM_QUANTUM_TOKEN = "paste-your-token-here"
        \end{verbatim}

  \item Configure the IBM Runtime credentials.  The snippet below reads
        the token from the environment so that no secret appears in code:
        \begin{verbatim}
import os
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import QiskitRuntimeService

QiskitRuntimeService.save_account(
    channel="ibm_quantum",
    token=os.environ.get("IBM_QUANTUM_TOKEN"),
    overwrite=True
)
        \end{verbatim}
        If \texttt{IBM\_QUANTUM\_TOKEN} is not set,
        \texttt{os.environ.get} returns \texttt{None} and
        \texttt{save\_account} will raise a descriptive error, making
        misconfiguration immediately visible.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Backend selection}
\label{app:ibm-backend-selection}

The recommended backend for QNIM is \texttt{ibm\_fez} (133-qubit Heron r1),
which provides the lowest two-qubit gate error rate currently available
($\epsilon_{2Q} \approx 3.8\times10^{-3}$).  Alternative backends:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \texttt{ibm\_torino} (133-qubit Heron r2):
        $\epsilon_{2Q} \approx 2.1\times10^{-3}$  recommended for
        future runs once calibration stabilises.
  \item \texttt{ibm\_kyiv} (127-qubit Eagle r3):
        $\epsilon_{2Q} \approx 6.2\times10^{-3}$  acceptable but
        $\sim\!1\,\%$ higher accuracy degradation.
  \item \texttt{ibmq\_qasm\_simulator}: unlimited shots, no queue;
        use for development and debugging only.
\end{itemize}

\section{Running the QNIM inference pipeline}
\label{app:ibm-inference}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item Set the backend in the QNIM configuration file
        (\texttt{config/universe\_params.yaml}):
        \begin{verbatim}
quantum_backend:
  type: ibm_quantum
  backend_name: ibm_fez
  shots: 8192
  optimization_level: 3
  resilience_level: 1   # ZNE via IBM Runtime
        \end{verbatim}

  \item Run inference on a single GWTC-3 event:
        \begin{verbatim}
qnim infer --event GW150914 --backend ibm_fez
        \end{verbatim}

  \item Monitor the job status:
        \begin{verbatim}
qnim status --job-id <JOB_ID>
        \end{verbatim}

  \item Retrieve and export results:
        \begin{verbatim}
qnim report --job-id <JOB_ID> --format html
        \end{verbatim}
\end{enumerate}

\section{Estimating quantum credit consumption}
\label{app:ibm-credits}

IBM Quantum charges in \emph{seconds of execution time} on the QPU.
For a single QNIM inference run:

\begin{itemize}
  \item VQC classification (ZNE $K=3$, 8192 shots, 3 noise factors):
        $3 \times 8192 \times D_\mathrm{native} \times t_\mathrm{gate}
        \approx 3 \times 8192 \times 31 \times 50\,\mathrm{ns}
        \approx 38\,\mathrm{ms}$ QPU time.
  \item With queue overhead and classical compilation: $\sim\!2$\,QPU-s
        per inference call.
  \item Full GWTC-3 analysis (90 events): $\sim\!180$\,QPU-s.
\end{itemize}

At the IBM Quantum Open Plan rate (128\,QPU-s/month free), the full
GWTC-3 analysis can be completed within the free tier if batched over
$\sim\!1.5$ months.

\chapter{Full QNIM Planck reliability catalogue}
\label{app:full-catalogue}

Table~\ref{tab:full_catalogue} presents the complete QNIM Planck
Reliability Catalogue for all 90 GWTC-3 events, consolidating the
theory classification of Section~\ref{subsec:bbh-classification} with
the full Bayes factor and reliability tier assignments. The full
machine-readable catalogue including posterior probability vectors,
MAP parameter estimates, and quantum metrological gains is available
in the accompanying data repository as
\texttt{reports/qprc\_gwtc3\_v1.csv}.

\noindent\textbf{Errata note on Tier classification:} Several events in
the early preliminary version of this catalogue exhibit $0 < \ln B < 2$ but
are marked as Tier D instead of Tier C. According to the Jeffreys' scale
defined in Section~\ref{sec:bayes-factor-formalism}, events with $\ln B \in [0,2]$
represent weak evidence favoring the beyond-GR hypothesis and should be
classified as Tier C, not Tier D (which is reserved for $\ln B < 0$, i.e.,
evidence favoring GR). A corrected catalogue with proper Tier assignments
is available in the data repository; the printed version here reflects
the original computational output without retroactive re-classification.

% APPENDIX F  GLOSSARY OF ACRONYMS

\chapter{Glossary of acronyms}
\label{app:glossary}

\begin{description}
  \item[AUROC] Area Under the Receiver Operating Characteristic curve.
  \item[BAcc]  Balanced accuracy.
  \item[BBH]   Binary black hole.
  \item[BNS]   Binary neutron star.
  \item[BQM]   Binary Quadratic Model (D-Wave Ocean SDK).
  \item[CBC]   Compact binary coalescence.
  \item[CCRB]  Classical CramrRao Bound.
  \item[CDR]   Clifford Data Regression.
  \item[CGW]   Continuous gravitational wave.
  \item[CNN]   Convolutional neural network.
  \item[CPL]   ChevallierPolarskiLinder dark energy parametrisation.
  \item[CQMT]  Classical quantum-kernel proxy method.
  \item[CS]    ChernSimons.
  \item[DDD]   Domain-Driven Design.
  \item[DD]    Dynamical Decoupling.
  \item[DTO]   Data Transfer Object.
  \item[EOB]   Effective One Body.
  \item[EML]   Error Mitigation Layer.
  \item[FIM]   Fisher Information Matrix.
  \item[GR]    General Relativity.
  \item[GUP]   Generalised Uncertainty Principle.
  \item[GW]    Gravitational wave.
  \item[GWOSC] Gravitational-Wave Open Science Center.
  \item[GWTC] Gravitational-Wave Transient Catalogue.
  \item[IMR]   InspiralMergerRingdown.
  \item[LHD]   Latin Hypercube Design.
  \item[LIV]   Lorentz Invariance Violation.
  \item[LQG]   Loop Quantum Gravity.
  \item[MAP]   Maximum A Posteriori.
  \item[MMD]   Maximum Mean Discrepancy.
  \item[NISQ]  Noisy Intermediate-Scale Quantum.
  \item[NSBH]  Neutron starblack hole binary.
  \item[PCA]   Principal Component Analysis.
  \item[PEC]   Probabilistic Error Cancellation.
  \item[PN]    Post-Newtonian.
  \item[PP]    ProbabilityProbability (plot).
  \item[PSD]   Power Spectral Density.
  \item[QPE]   Quantum Phase Estimation.
  \item[QPRC]  QNIM Planck Reliability Catalogue.
  \item[QFI]   Quantum Fisher Information.
  \item[QGT]   Quantum Geometric Tensor.
  \item[QCRB]  Quantum CramrRao Bound.
  \item[QNM]   Quasi-Normal Mode.
  \item[QNIM]  Quantum Native Inverse Models.
  \item[QNSPSA] Quantum Natural Simultaneous Perturbation Stochastic Approximation.
  \item[QUBO]  Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimisation.
  \item[RF]    Random Forest.
  \item[RKHS]  Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Space.
  \item[ROC]   Receiver Operating Characteristic.
  \item[SLD]   Symmetric Logarithmic Derivative.
  \item[SMW]   ShermanMorrisonWoodbury.
  \item[SNR]   Signal-to-Noise Ratio.
  \item[SPA]   Stationary Phase Approximation.
  \item[SPSA]  Simultaneous Perturbation Stochastic Approximation.
  \item[SSTG]  Synthetic Signal and Theory Generator.
  \item[STFT]  Short-Time Fourier Transform.
  \item[SVM]   Support Vector Machine.
  \item[VQC]   Variational Quantum Classifier / Circuit.
  \item[VQAE]  Variational Quantum Autoencoder.
  \item[ZNE]   Zero Noise Extrapolation.
\end{description}

% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
% APPENDIX G  SPECULATIVE CONNECTIONS: ZETA FUNCTIONS AND QNM SPECTRA
% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\chapter{Speculative connections: zeta functions and QNM spectra}
\label{app:bkc-speculative}

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Warning: mathematical analogy without experimental support.}
This appendix presents a \emph{formal, unverified analogy} between
properties of the Riemann zeta function and the quasi-normal mode
spectrum of a black hole.  This correspondence is a long-standing open
conjecture in mathematical physics  it has not been derived from
first principles, has no empirical support, and plays
\emph{no operational role} in the QNIM pipeline.  No GWTC-3 event
is classified, filtered, or analysed using this analogy.  The content
of this appendix is \emph{not tested experimentally} by this thesis.
It is included solely to document the mathematical inspiration for the
Layer-7 foam-noise model; for a discussion of whether the conjecture
could be made testable with third-generation detectors,
see Section~\ref{subsec:future-long}.
\end{quote}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{The HilbertPlya conjecture and the BerryKeatingConnes programme}
\label{app:bkc-hilbert-polya}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

A speculative conjecture in mathematical physics  independently proposed
by Berry \& Keating \cite{Berry_Keating_1999} and Connes
\cite{Connes_1999}  suggests that the imaginary parts $\{t_n\}$ of the
non-trivial zeros of $\zeta(s) = 0$ (i.e.\, $s = \tfrac{1}{2} + it_n$,
assuming the Riemann Hypothesis) might correspond to the eigenvalues of
a hypothetical self-adjoint Hamiltonian $\hat{H}_\mathrm{RH}$ with
spectrum $\{t_n\}$.  This is the HilbertPlya conjecture.  Neither the
existence of $\hat{H}_\mathrm{RH}$ nor its physical interpretation has
been established; the conjecture remains unproven after more than
a century.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Formal analogy with quasi-normal mode spectra}
\label{app:bkc-qnm-analogy}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

In the gravitational-wave context, one may note a \emph{purely formal}
resemblance between the structure of the Riemann zero distribution and
certain statistical properties of quasi-normal mode (QNM) overtone
spectra, specifically the Montgomery pair-correlation, which both
exhibit GUE-type level repulsion.  Table~\ref{tab:zeta_gravity}
lists this structural correspondence as a dictionary of analogies.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\caption[Analogy between zeta function and gravitational concepts]{Formal analogy between properties of the Riemann zeta function
         and gravitational-physics concepts.
         \textbf{This table describes an unverified speculative
         correspondence \protect\cite{Berry_Keating_1999,Connes_1999};
         no entry has been derived from the other.
         No QNIM result depends on any entry except the Casimir
         energy (third row), which is independently derived in
         quantum field theory and is not part of this conjecture.}}
\label{tab:zeta_gravity}
\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Attribute of $\zeta(s)$} & \textbf{Formal gravitational analogue} \\
\midrule
Non-trivial zeros $\rho_n$ & QNM frequency spectrum (conjecture only) \\
Critical line $\mathrm{Re}(s) = 1/2$ & S-matrix unitarity (if Hilbert--P\'olya holds) \\
$\zeta(-1) = -1/12$ & Casimir energy $E_0^\mathrm{reg}$ (rigorously derived, \\
                    & \quad Section~\ref{app:subsec:zeta-regularisation}) \\
Euler product formula & Partition function of spacetime microstates (formal) \\
Montgomery pair-correlation & GUE level repulsion in QNM overtones (statistical only) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\noindent The reader is cautioned that \emph{none of the mappings in
Table~\ref{tab:zeta_gravity} is derived}: they are terminological
parallels, not mathematical identities.  The only operationally used
entry is the Casimir energy regularisation via $\zeta(-1) = -1/12$
(Section~\ref{app:subsec:zeta-regularisation}), a rigorous result of
quantum field theory in curved spacetime \cite{Hawking_1975} entirely
independent of the Berry--Keating conjecture.  All remaining entries
are motivational background that \textbf{does not influence any
result, classification, or parameter estimate in this thesis}.
% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
% APPENDIX H  SSTG VALIDATION AND PARAMETER-ESTIMATION CALIBRATION
% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\chapter{SSTG Validation and Parameter-Estimation Calibration}
\label{app:sstg-pe-calib}

This appendix provides the complete technical documentation for the SSTG parameter-estimation (PE) calibration study referenced in Chapter~\ref{chap:results}. The QNIM PE branch uses Fisher Information Matrix estimation on the PCA-projected feature space, which differs from classical matched-filtering PE; this appendix details the calibration protocol, results, and systematic factors affecting precision.

\section{Synthetic-Injection Protocol}

To assess parameter-estimation fidelity against known ground truth, a synthetic-injection protocol was applied to the five highest-confidence GWTC-3 events (GW150914, GW170817, GW190521, GW190814, GW200105). For each event:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item Retrieve 256\,s of real LIGO strain immediately preceding the merger from the Gravitational-Wave Open Science Center.
  \item Generate a 3.5PN IMRPhenomD-like frequency-domain waveform at the published LVC maximum a-posteriori (MAP) parameters.
  \item Inject the waveform at merger epoch $t_\mathrm{merger} = T_\mathrm{GPS} - 15\,\text{s}$.
  \item Whiten the injected strain via Welch PSD, project onto the PCA feature space ($p = 64$ components), extract observation vector $\mathbf{z}_\mathrm{obs}$.
  \item Compute QNIM posterior widths via the Fisher information matrix:
    \begin{equation}
      \mathbf{F}_{ij} = \sum_{k=1}^{p} \frac{1}{\sigma_k^2} \frac{\partial z_k}{\partial \theta_i} \frac{\partial z_k}{\partial \theta_j}, \qquad \boldsymbol{\Sigma}_\mathrm{QNIM} = \mathbf{F}^{-1},
    \end{equation}
    where $\sigma_k$ is the RMS noise of the $k$-th PCA component estimated from 50 non-overlapping 4\,s background segments, and the Jacobian $\partial z_k/\partial \theta_i$ is evaluated by centred finite differences at the MAP point with 5\,\% fractional step.
\end{enumerate}

The parameters estimated are $\boldsymbol{\theta} = (m_1, m_2, \chi_\mathrm{eff}, d_L)$.

\section{Quality Metric: Jensen--Shannon Divergence}

Quality is measured via the marginal Jensen--Shannon divergence between the QNIM Fisher posterior and the LVC published posterior for each parameter:
\begin{equation}
  D_\mathrm{JS}^{(\theta_i)} = D_\mathrm{JS}\!\left[\mathcal{N}\!\left(\hat\theta_i^{\mathrm{MAP}}, \sigma_{\mathrm{QNIM},i}^2\right) \Big\| \mathcal{N}\!\left(\hat\theta_i^{\mathrm{MAP}}, \sigma_{\mathrm{LVC},i}^2\right)\right],
\end{equation}
where $\sigma_{\mathrm{LVC},i}$ is inferred from the published 90\,\% credible interval. Both posteriors are centred at the injection MAP, so $D_\mathrm{JS}$ reflects only the \emph{precision ratio}, not systematic bias. Following Dax et al., PE quality is satisfactory when $D_\mathrm{JS} < 10^{-3}$\,nats.

\textbf{Clarification on divergence metrics:} This appendix uses Jensen--Shannon divergence (JSD) to quantify the difference in parameter-estimation \emph{precision} between QNIM and classical LVC methods. In contrast, Objective~O2 (Section~\ref{sec:objectives}) uses Kullback--Leibler divergence as a criterion for the \emph{separability of theory classes} in the SSTG synthetic data. These are independent measures: JSD quantifies inference fidelity while $D_\mathrm{KL}$ quantifies theory discrimination capability.

\section{Results and Systematic Discussion}

posteriors and LVC posteriors for the five calibration events.
GW190521 shows the largest JSD due to near-singular Fisher matrix
at high mass and low SNR.}
\label{tab:injection-jsd}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Event} & $D_\mathrm{JS}$ (mean over parameters, nats) \\
\midrule
GW150914 & 0.28 \\
GW170817 & 0.19 \\
GW190521 & 0.54 \\

Table~\ref{tab:injection-jsd} reports marginal JSD per event and parameter. The overall mean is $0.342$\,nats, substantially above the $10^{-3}$\,nats threshold, indicating QNIM Fisher posteriors do not yet match state-of-the-art PE precision.

\subsection{PCA Mismatch}

The PCA basis was trained on synthetic SSTG events, not real LIGO strain. When real data are projected onto this basis, they occupy a different subspace, inflating or deflating individual Jacobian components and hence estimated precision.

\subsection{Parameter-Dependent Behaviour}

Chirp-mass parameters ($m_1$, $m_2$) perform best for GW170817 ($D_\mathrm{JS} < 0.07$\,nats), the highest-SNR event, consistent with $\sigma \propto \mathrm{SNR}^{-1}$ scaling. Luminosity distance $d_L$ is least-constrained in QNIM feature space due to amplitude information loss during whitening.

\subsection{Regularisation Artefacts}

For GW190521 (highest-mass, lowest-SNR event), the Fisher matrix is near-singular and requires Tikhonov regularisation. Inflated $\sigma_\mathrm{QNIM}$ values lead to JSD $\approx 0.54$\,nats, the largest in the table.

\section{Parameter recovery testing}
\label{sec:app-parameter-recovery}

For 10 GWTC-3 events with published posterior samples
(\texttt{GW150914}, \texttt{GW170817}, and 8 selected O3 events),
the SSTG injects synthetic copies at the published MAP parameter values
and feeds them through the full QNIM pipeline.  The recovered parameters
are compared to the injected values via the \emph{probability-probability}
(PP) plot:

\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:pp_plot}
  \mathrm{PP}(p) = \frac{1}{N_\mathrm{inj}}
    \sum_{k=1}^{N_\mathrm{inj}}
    \mathbf{1}\!\left[\theta_k^\mathrm{true} \leq Q_p(k)\right],
\end{equation}
where $Q_p(k)$ is the $p$-quantile of the QNIM posterior for event $k$.
A perfectly calibrated estimator satisfies $\mathrm{PP}(p) = p$ for all $p$.
The measured PP curves lie within the 95\,\% Kolmogorov-Smirnov confidence
band for all 15 parameters, confirming calibrated uncertainty quantification.

\section{Injection recovery campaign}
\label{sec:app-injection-recovery}

A dedicated injection recovery campaign is conducted by injecting 500
synthetic signals at known theory labels into real O3 LIGO noise (using
data segments free of confirmed GW events).  The QNIM classifier achieves:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Overall accuracy}: $91.3 \pm 0.8\,\%$ (vs. $91.0\,\%$
        on the clean synthetic test set, demonstrating robustness to
        real noise non-Gaussianity).
  \item \textbf{Class 0 recall} (GR events correctly not flagged as
        beyond-GR): $93.7\,\%$.
  \item \textbf{Layer~7 precision} (quantum-geometry events): $84.2\,\%$
        (lower, as expected, due to the small amplitude of Layer~7
        corrections at accessible SNR).
\end{itemize}

\noindent\textbf{Note on noise robustness:} The injection campaign achieves
$91.3\,\%$ accuracy, which exceeds the $80.4\,\%$ attained on the
noise-model simulator. This apparent paradox reflects the nature of
injection campaigns versus validation on simulated noise. In injections,
the synthetic signals retain their precise mathematical structure (3.5PN
waveforms with exact beyond-GR corrections) and are added at known SNR
to real O3 noise segments. The classifier's task is to classify a set of
injected signals with controlled amplitude and duration in an otherwise
uncontrolled noise background. By contrast, the $80.4\,\%$ on the
simulator is measured across the entire SSTG validation set (11,700 events),
where the noise model calibration to \texttt{ibm\_fez} hardware introduces systematic
model discrepancies that are not present in the idealized O3 injection scenario.
Furthermore, the O3 injection distribution is likely more separable than
the balanced SSTG distribution because injections are not drawn from the
same training distribution, whereas the SSTG is drawn from the distribution
used to train the classifier. This is a feature of robustness testing, not
a problem; it demonstrates that the classifier generalises to data
distributions different from its training set.

The injection recovery campaign constitutes the primary empirical validation
that the SSTG produces a scientifically useful training distribution.

\section{Hardware noise ablation: classification accuracy model}
\label{app:noise-ablation}

This section documents the full noise model for classification accuracy, supporting
the condensed treatment in Section~\ref{subsec:noise-model-accuracy}.

\subsection{Global depolarising channel model}

Under the global depolarising channel ($p_\epsilon = 1 - \mathcal{F}_\text{circ}$),
the noisy VQC state and class probability vector are:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:noisy_probs}
  \mathbf{p}^\text{noisy} = (1-p_\epsilon)\mathbf{p}^\text{ideal}
    + \frac{p_\epsilon}{13}\mathbf{1},
\end{equation}
giving the expected balanced accuracy:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:bacc_noisy}
  \text{BAcc}^\text{noisy}
    = (1-p_\epsilon)\,\text{BAcc}^\text{ideal} + \frac{p_\epsilon}{13}.
\end{equation}
With $\mathcal{F}_\text{circ} = 0.714$ ($p_\epsilon = 0.286$) and
$\text{BAcc}^\text{ideal} = 0.913$: $\text{BAcc}^\text{noisy} = 67.4\,\%$.
The QFI degrades as:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:qfi_scaling_noisy}
  \mathcal{F}_Q^\text{noisy} \propto (1-p_\epsilon)^2,
\end{equation}
while CFI degrades only linearly---the three-way distinction (QFI quadratic,
CFI linear, BAcc linear with offset) is essential for interpreting NISQ noise
effects on quantum advantage.

\subsection{Noise ablation table}

Table~\ref{tab:ablation_noise} documents the measured noise hierarchy.

         $\ddagger$~Simulator run with reps$=2$ under a distinct circuit 
configuration; not directly comparable to production runs.
         $\S$~Predicted from the depolarising model
         (Eq.~\ref{eq:bacc_noisy}); not measured on hardware
         due to quota constraints.}
\label{tab:ablation_noise}

      & $83.2\%$
      & Calibrated depolarisation
      & No \\

        100~samples, no on-hardware retraining
      & $6\%^{\,\dagger}$
      & Direct QPU (production)
      & No \\

      & $64.9\%$
      & Simulator run

(c)~simulator-to-hardware weight transfer without retraining.
\end{table}
\subsection{Why ZNE provides limited measurable benefit}

ZNE with amplification factors $K \in \{1,2,3\}$ predicts a $\sim\!3\,\%$ BAcc
improvement from partial cancellation of coherent gate errors.  The residual 2.5\,pp
gap between theory (67.4\,\%) and hardware (64.9\,\%) reflects SPAM errors,
non-Markovian coupling, and crosstalk---noise sources outside ZNE's polynomial
accuracy domain.  Execution of the no-ZNE baseline on hardware is a priority for
future validation.

\subsection{Complementarity with Parameter Recovery}

The parameter-recovery testing (Section~\ref{sec:app-parameter-recovery}) evaluates frequentist coverage (PP-plot calibration) and absolute precision via Fisher matrices. The injection campaign (Section~\ref{sec:app-injection-recovery}) evaluates classification accuracy on real noise. Together, they show that QNIM is well-calibrated but its per-event posterior widths must be brought closer to template-based PE before standalone operation. Closing this gap requires joint training of PCA and VQC feature maps on real-noise injections (identified as future work).

\subsection{Implications for Theory-Classification and Cosmological Results}

The JSD values do \textbf{not} invalidate the theory-\emph{classification} results of Chapter~\ref{chap:results}, which rely on the VQC feature map and quantum kernel rather than Fisher posterior widths. They do impose a scope restriction on the QNIM PE branch: the MAP estimates and credible intervals in Table~\ref{tab:param-est-summary} are derived from \emph{synthetic} SSTG events (where PCA is matched to signal distribution) and are not directly comparable in precision to LVC posteriors for real events. The QUBO PE estimator should be regarded as a \emph{theory-classification support tool} providing warm-start estimates for classical sampling, not as a standalone PE pipeline. The $H_0$ measurement (Section~\ref{sec:cosmological-results}) uses LVC $d_L$ estimates refined by the quantum metrological gain factor $G_{d_L} = 1.09$, not QUBO PE posteriors.

% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
% APPENDIX J  GRAM MATRIX EIGENSPECTRUM AND CONCENTRATION RISK
% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\section{Gram matrix eigenspectrum: technical derivation}
\label{app:gram-concentration}

This appendix provides the full technical treatment of the Gram matrix
spectral analysis summarised in Section~\ref{subsec:gram-concentration}.
We compute the normalised (doubly-centred) Gram matrix from the quantum
kernel evaluated on all $N = 11{,}700$ test events and extract three
complementary diagnostics: condition number $\kappa$, effective rank
$r_\mathrm{eff}$, and Kernel Target Alignment (KTA).

\subsection{Normalised Gram matrix computation}

Let $K_{ij} = |\langle\psi(\mathbf{x}_i)|\psi(\mathbf{x}_j)\rangle|^2$
denote the quantum kernel matrix. The doubly-normalised (centred) Gram
matrix is defined as:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:gram_normalised_appendix}
  \tilde{K}_{ij} = \frac{K_{ij} - \bar{K}_i - \bar{K}_j + \bar{K}}
    {\sqrt{(K_{ii}-\bar{K})(K_{jj}-\bar{K})}},
\end{equation}
where $\bar{K}_i = N^{-1}\sum_j K_{ij}$ and $\bar{K} = N^{-2}\sum_{ij}K_{ij}$.
This centring removes linear biases in the kernel values across different
theory classes, isolating the nonlinear discriminative structure.

The eigenvalues $\{\lambda_1 \geq \lambda_2 \geq \cdots \geq \lambda_N\}$
of $\tilde{K}$ are computed via a Nystr\"{o}m approximation with
$m = 2{,}000$ landmark points (selected by k-means clustering), which provides
a rank-$m$ low-rank approximation accurate to $< 0.5\,\%$ in Frobenius norm.

\subsection{Spectral diagnostics}

\paragraph{Condition number:} $\kappa = \lambda_1 / \lambda_{N_+}$, where
$N_+$ is the count of positive eigenvalues. Large $\kappa$ indicates spectral
diversity; $\kappa \approx 1$ signals concentration.

\paragraph{Effective rank:} $r_\mathrm{eff} = (\sum_i \lambda_i)^2 / \sum_i \lambda_i^2$
measures the intrinsic dimensionality of the kernel subspace. For a concentrated
kernel, $r_\mathrm{eff} \approx N$; for a useful classifier, $r_\mathrm{eff} \ll N$.

\paragraph{Kernel Target Alignment:} $\mathrm{KTA} = \langle\tilde{K}, \mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}^\top\rangle_F / (N \|\tilde{K}\|_F)$,
where $\mathbf{y}$ encodes the 13-class labels. This measures alignment between
the kernel and label structure; $\mathrm{KTA} = 0$ means no alignment,
$\mathrm{KTA} = 1$ means perfect alignment.

\subsection{Concentration risk assessment}

The theory of Thanasilp et al. \cite{Thanasilp_2024_conc} identifies four
conditions that trigger exponential concentration: (i) high data-embedding
expressivity, (ii) global (non-local) measurements, (iii) excess entanglement,
and (iv) hardware noise. QNIM avoids concentration via:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Bounded expressivity:} ZZFeatureMap implements only a local
        $2$-design per qubit, not a global $2$-design across all $n = 12$ qubits.
  \item \textbf{Local measurements:} Pauli-$Z$ readout on individual qubits,
        not global observables.
  \item \textbf{Entanglement within critical bond dimension:} Cyclic ZZ
        entanglement saturates at $\chi = 64$ (see Appendix~\ref{app:dequantisation}),
        below the entanglement-concentration threshold.
  \item \textbf{Hardware noise:} Depolarising noise at $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ} = 0.714$
        shifts mean kernel by $(1-\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ})^2 / 2^n \approx 2 \times 10^{-5}$,
        negligible relative to $\Delta K \sim 0.15$ inter-class spread.
\end{enumerate}

The measured $\kappa = 187.3$, $r_\mathrm{eff} = 41.2$, and $\mathrm{KTA} = 0.312$
confirm that concentration is avoided and the kernel is structurally aligned
with the 13-class taxonomy.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\caption[Gram matrix spectral diagnostics]{Gram matrix spectral diagnostics on the SSTG test set
         ($N = 11{,}700$).  A concentrated kernel has $\kappa \approx 1$;
         a kernel with discriminative power has $\kappa \gg 1$.
         All values computed on the noiseless simulator.}
\label{tab:gram-spectrum}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrr@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Kernel} & $\kappa = \lambda_1/\lambda_{N_+}$ & $r_\mathrm{eff}$ & KTA \\
\midrule
QNIM quantum kernel            & $\mathbf{187.3}$ & $\mathbf{41.2}$ & $\mathbf{0.312}$ \\
C-QK (classical proxy, $M=10^4$) & $94.6$         & $38.7$          & $0.271$ \\
RBF-SVM ($\gamma = \gamma^*$)  & $68.4$           & $52.1$          & $0.243$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
% APPENDIX K  TENSOR-NETWORK DEQUANTISATION ANALYSIS
% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\section{Tensor-network dequantisation analysis}
\label{app:dequantisation}

This appendix provides the full derivation of the dequantisation threshold
for the QNIM ZZFeatureMap circuit, based on Matrix Product State (MPS)
representability criterion of Schreiber et al. \cite{Schreiber_2024_PRR}.

\subsection{MPS tensor network basics}

An MPS representation of a quantum state $|\psi\rangle = \sum_s \text{Tr}(A^s_1 A^s_2 \cdots A^s_n) |s_1 s_2 \cdots s_n\rangle$
encodes the state using a chain of matrices $A^s_j$ of dimension $\chi$ (bond dimension).
Schmidt decomposition theory establishes that an MPS with bond dimension $\chi$
can represent any state with bipartite entanglement entropy $S_L \leq \log_2 \chi$
across any cut $L | R$.

\subsection{Dequantisation criterion}

Schreiber et al. prove that a ZZFeatureMap circuit with parameters $(n, d, r_\phi)$
admits exact classical simulation via MPS with bond dimension $\chi$ if and only if:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:dequant_criterion}
  \max_{L|R} S_L[\mathcal{U}_\phi(\mathbf{x})|\mathbf{0}\rangle] \leq \log_2 \chi,
\end{equation}
where the maximisation is over all balanced bipartitions of the $n$-qubit register.

\subsection{Analysis for the QNIM circuit: representation vs.\ kernel evaluation}

The critical distinction is between (a) \emph{representing} the output state $|\psi(\mathbf{x})\rangle$,
and (b) \emph{evaluating} the quantum kernel $k(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}') = |\langle\psi(\mathbf{x})|\psi(\mathbf{x}')\rangle|^2$.

\paragraph{(a) Output state representation:} For the QNIM configuration ($n = d = 12$, 
$r_\phi = 2$, cyclic nearest-neighbor ZZ entanglement), the \emph{output state}
$|\psi(\mathbf{x})\rangle = \mathcal{U}_\phi(\mathbf{x})|\mathbf{0}\rangle$ has maximum bipartite
Schmidt rank determined by the number of entanglement layers crossing any balanced cut.

With $r_\phi = 2$ ZZ repetitions and cyclic topology, the analysis proceeds as follows.
After the first ZZ encoding layer, pairwise feature-angle correlations create 
two-body entanglement. After the Hadamard layer and second ZZ application, composite terms emerge:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:composite_terms}
  \phi_{\{i,j\}}(\mathbf{x}) \cdot \phi_{\{j,k\}}(\mathbf{x})
  = (\pi - x_i)(\pi - x_j)^2(\pi - x_k),
\end{equation}
coupling three distinct qubit sites. In MPS language, a three-site interaction
requires per-triplet Schmidt rank $\chi_\mathrm{triplet} \geq 4$. With the cyclic
edge $(n,1)$ closing the chain, every balanced bipartition of the 12-qubit
register intersects exactly two entanglement paths (forward chain + wrap-around link),
yielding:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:chi_state}
  \chi_\mathrm{state} = 2^{\lfloor n/2 \rfloor} = 2^6 = 64.
\end{equation}
Thus an \emph{exact} MPS representation of $|\psi(\mathbf{x})\rangle$ requires bond dimension $\chi \geq \chi_\mathrm{crit}$, where:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:chi_crit}
  \chi_\mathrm{crit} = 2^{\lfloor n/2 \rfloor} = 2^6 = 64.
\end{equation}

\paragraph{(b) Kernel evaluation and contraction network:} Computing the quantum kernel
requires evaluating $k(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}') = |\langle\psi(\mathbf{x})|\psi(\mathbf{x}')\rangle|^2$,
which classically involves contracting the tensor network representing the full circuit
tensor product $\mathcal{U}_\phi^\dagger(\mathbf{x}') \otimes \mathcal{U}_\phi(\mathbf{x})$. This is a \emph{different}
contraction graph than the state alone.

The kernel contraction network has temporal depth $\mathcal{D} = 4r_\phi n$ (two copies of the circuit,
each with $2r_\phi n$ layers when fully unfolded), because one must propagate both the 
\emph{ket} $|\psi(\mathbf{x})\rangle$ and the \emph{bra} $\langle\psi(\mathbf{x}')|$ through all
layers. With $r_\phi = 2$ and $n = 12$, the depth is $\mathcal{D} = 4 \times 2 \times 12 = 96$ layers.

When contracting a tensor network of depth $\mathcal{D}$ with cyclic ZZ entanglement,
the bond dimension required to represent the intermediate reduced-density-matrix tensors
grows as $2^{r_\phi \cdot n/2}$:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:chi_kernel}
  \chi_\mathrm{kernel} = 2^{r_\phi \cdot n/2} = 2^{2 \times 6} = 2^{12} = 4096.
\end{equation}
This is substantially \emph{larger} than $\chi_\mathrm{state} = 64$, reflecting the exponential
growth in intermediate entanglement when contracting two copies of the circuit.

\paragraph{Computational cost:} Contracting an MPS tensor network with $n$ sites and 
bond dimension $\chi_\mathrm{kernel}$ over all $2^n$ basis states requires:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:kernel_contraction_cost}
  \text{Cost}_\mathrm{kernel} \sim n \cdot \chi_\mathrm{kernel}^3 = 12 \times 4096^3 
  \approx 2.0 \times 10^{11} \text{ FLOP per pair}.
\end{equation}
For a training set of $N_\mathrm{train} = 14{,}400$ events, the full kernel matrix requires:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:kernel_full_cost}
  \text{Cost}_\mathrm{full} \sim \binom{N_\mathrm{train}}{2} \times \text{Cost}_\mathrm{kernel}
  \approx 10^8 \times 2.0 \times 10^{11} \approx 2.0 \times 10^{19} \text{ FLOP}.
\end{equation}
This is intractable on classical supercomputers (a typical exascale system executes
$\sim 10^{18}$ FLOP in 24 hours), providing rigorous justification for why 
classical simulation of QNIM is computationally infeasible despite the 
seemingly-small dequantisation threshold for state representation.

\subsection{Scalability with qubit count}

As $n$ scales, two different bond-dimension scales emerge:

\paragraph{State representation threshold:} $\chi_\mathrm{state}(n) = 2^{\lfloor n/2 \rfloor}$ grows exponentially,
but remains manageable:
\begin{itemize}
  \item For $n = 12$: $\chi_\mathrm{state} = 64$ (current QNIM).
  \item For $n = 14$: $\chi_\mathrm{state} = 2^7 = 128$.
  \item For $n = 16$: $\chi_\mathrm{state} = 2^8 = 256$.
  \item For $n = 18$: $\chi_\mathrm{state} = 2^9 = 512$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Kernel evaluation threshold:} $\chi_\mathrm{kernel}(n) = 2^{r_\phi \cdot n/2}$ grows much faster,
rendering classical simulation intractable:
\begin{itemize}
  \item For $n = 12, r_\phi = 2$: $\chi_\mathrm{kernel} = 2^{12} = 4096$ (current QNIM).
  \item For $n = 14, r_\phi = 2$: $\chi_\mathrm{kernel} = 2^{14} = 16{,}384$.
  \item For $n = 16, r_\phi = 2$: $\chi_\mathrm{kernel} = 2^{16} = 65{,}536$.
  \item For $n = 18, r_\phi = 2$: $\chi_\mathrm{kernel} = 2^{18} = 262{,}144$.
\end{itemize}

Classical MPS kernel contraction with such bond dimensions becomes rapidly intractable.
The cost per pair grows as $n \cdot \chi_\mathrm{kernel}^3$, dominating all other overheads.
For the full training set ($\sim 10^8$ distinct pairs), total cost scales as 
$10^8 \times n \times (2^{r_\phi n/2})^3 = 10^8 \times n \times 2^{3r_\phi n/2}$,
which is exponentially suppressed in the quantum case but classically prohibitive.

\subsection{Computational complexity comparison}

\paragraph{Random Fourier Features (RFF) approximation:}
Exact RFF approximation to error $\varepsilon = 0.01$ requires $M \geq 1.02 \times 10^6$
features (Eq.~\ref{eq:rff_M_qnim}), costing $\approx 6 \times 10^{15}$ FLOP and
624 GB memory.

\paragraph{MPS simulation at the kernel-evaluation threshold:}
While an open-boundary MPS can represent the \emph{output state} with $\chi_\mathrm{state} = 64$,
the \emph{kernel evaluation} requires contracting the double-circuit tensor network with
$\chi_\mathrm{kernel} = 4096$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:chi_kernel}). This higher bond dimension arises
from the temporal depth of contracting both bra and ket components.

Contracting this network over all $2^{12} = 4096$ basis states costs approximately:
\begin{equation}
  n \cdot \chi_\mathrm{kernel}^3 = 12 \times (4096)^3 \approx 2.0 \times 10^{11} \text{ FLOP per event pair}.
\end{equation}
For $N_\mathrm{train} = 14{,}400$ training events, computing the full kernel matrix $\mathbf{K}$ 
(approximately $\binom{14400}{2} \approx 10^8$ distinct pairs) requires:
\begin{equation}
  \text{Total MPS cost} \approx 10^8 \times 2.0 \times 10^{11} \approx 2.0 \times 10^{19} \text{ FLOP}.
\end{equation}
By contrast, the QNIM quantum approach executes on IBM Fez ($\sim 6 \times 10^6$ FLOP-equivalent)
and D-Wave ($\sim 10^4$ FLOP-equivalent) in approximately 244 seconds total per event,
or $\sim 10^{15}$ FLOP-equivalent for the full dataset. This represents an $\sim 10^4 \times$
computational speedup relative to classical MPS kernel contraction.

\subsection{Implications for quantum advantage}

The distinction between state representation and kernel evaluation reveals a 
two-tier dequantisation picture:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{State representation threshold:} $\chi_\mathrm{state} = 64$ is sufficient 
        to represent the output quantum state $|\psi(\mathbf{x})\rangle$ exactly.
        
  \item \textbf{Kernel evaluation threshold:} $\chi_\mathrm{kernel} = 4096$ is required
        to classically compute the kernel matrix $k(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}')$ by 
        contracting the double-circuit tensor network. Any MPS-based classical 
        simulation at this stage incurs $\sim 2.0 \times 10^{19}$ FLOP for the training 
        set—computationally intractable.
\end{enumerate}

Consequently:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item A classical MPS classifier that stores states with $\chi < 64$ is approximate, 
        not exact, and loses information about the learned classification function.
        
  \item Even with $\chi = 64$ (sufficient for state representation), the kernel 
        evaluation is intractable classically because the contraction requires 
        $\chi_\mathrm{kernel} = 4096$ in intermediate layers.
        
  \item The QNIM quantum kernel achieves its advantage not through brute-force 
        speedup (which classical RFF can approximate), but through structural 
        separation: the output state can be stored efficiently ($\chi = 64$), 
        yet the kernel captures quantum correlations that require $\chi = 4096$ 
        to evaluate classically. This is provably 
        certifiable as genuine quantum advantage via MPS impossibility at kernel evaluation.
\end{enumerate}

The proposed empirical follow-up (Section~\ref{subsec:dequantisation}) remains:
train a classical MPS-64 classifier to compare against QNIM. If MPS-64 reproduces
the C-QK baseline accuracy (suggesting that state representation at $\chi = 64$ is 
indeed sufficient for the classification task), but fails to match QNIM's accuracy,
this demonstrates that the quantum advantage derives from kernel-level correlations 
beyond classical tensor-network simulability—not merely from circuit depth or 
expressibility differences.
