% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\chapter{Supplementary analysis for Chapter~\ref{chap:results}}
\label{app:ch7-supplement}

This appendix collects the extended analyses, detailed derivations, and
cross-validation results associated with Chapter~\ref{chap:results} that
were condensed in the main text for length.  All numerical values and
figures correspond exactly to the results cited in Chapter~\ref{chap:results}.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{VQC training: three key result derivations}
\label{app:ch7-training-detail}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

Figure~\ref{fig:convergence} encodes three key results that are each
independently significant.  \emph{First}, the $87.8\,\%$ loss reduction
from $\mathcal{L}_0 = 2.565$ to $\mathcal{L}_T = 0.312$ confirms that
the QNSPSA-EML-Feynman optimiser finds a genuine minimum and not a
saddle point: the loss $0.312$ corresponds to an average per-class
posterior entropy of $e^{0.312}/13 = 1.08\,\%$, far below the $10\,\%$
uniform random baseline.  \emph{Second}, the rapid-descent convergence
is explained mathematically by the quantum natural gradient: where
SPSA moves along the Euclidean gradient $\nabla\mathcal{L}$, QNSPSA moves
along $\hat{F}^{-1}\nabla\mathcal{L}$ (the QGT-preconditioned gradient),
which navigates the curved Riemannian manifold of the ansatz parameters
in far fewer steps.  This pre-conditioning is the central algorithmic
innovation; it transforms the ill-conditioned loss landscape of the VQC
into an approximately isotropic problem.

Regarding the observed convergence steps: the simulator required 25 steps
to reach loss $= 2.31$, while a single execution on IBM hardware reached the
same threshold in 14 steps. This observation is preliminary and derives from
a single training run on each platform. Without independent replication across
at least three distinct random seeds, it cannot be distinguished from
statistical fluctuation. Two confounding factors preclude a definitive
interpretation. First, the SPSA random seed initialisation differs between
the simulator run and the hardware run, which alone can produce $\sim 5$--$10\,\%$
step-count variation. Second, shot noise in hardware measurements introduces
an implicit smoothing of the loss landscape near initialisation: finite shot
counts ($\sim 2000$ in this deployment) cause effective noise to dominate
over gradient signal in the earliest steps, which can spuriously accelerate
convergence in single runs without reflecting a genuine physical mechanism.
To verify whether hardware-induced smoothing provides a systematic advantage,
a controlled Monte Carlo sweep with fixed seed sequences across 10--20 random
initialisations on both simulator and hardware would be required; this was
deferred due to computational budget constraints. Consequently, any claim
that hardware noise acts as a regulariser is unsupported; the observation
is noted only as a potential signal warranting follow-up investigation.

The $\sim\!300$-step SPSA reference (versus 14--25 for QNSPSA-EML-Feynman)
remains robustly explained by the preconditioned gradient: this advantage
is algorithmic, replicated independently via the Fisher-preconditioned
geometry, and does not depend on hardware-specific artefacts.

\emph{Third}, the negligible train-validation gap ($< 0.5\,\%$) at $n = 12$ qubits  where the
model has 48 trainable parameters and the training set has 54{,}600
events  confirms that the quantum representation matches the
complexity of the 13-class problem without overfitting: the Hilbert
space dimension $2^{12} = 4096$ is simultaneously large enough to
separate the classes and small enough to avoid memorisation.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{ROC curves and per-class classification performance}
\label{app:roc-detail}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

The per-class metrics characterise average performance; ROC curves reveal
performance at each possible operating threshold, allowing the pipeline to
be tuned for any desired false-positive rate.




\begin{figure}[ht]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.92\textwidth]{fig4_accuracy_snr}
\caption[QNIM classification performance vs SNR and baselines]{
  \label{fig:accuracy-snr}
  QNIM classification performance on the SSTG test set
         ($N = 11{,}700$ events, 13 theory classes).
         \textbf{(a)}~Validation accuracy vs network SNR for the QNIM
         VQC (simulator), the C-QK classical-proxy kernel, and the
         ResNet-18 baseline.  At the GWTC-3 median SNR of $19.2$
         (orange dotted line), the simulator reaches ${\sim}80\,\%$,
         already surpassing ResNet-18 (${\sim}70\,\%$).  The orange
         dashed horizontal line shows the IBM~Fez production accuracy
         ($6\,\%$), which is flat across all SNR values because it
         reflects hardware noise under Open Plan resource constraints
         rather than signal quality (see Appendix~\ref{app:sim-hw-gap}).
         \textbf{(b)}~Balanced accuracy (BAcc) for all six classifiers
         evaluated on the same held-out test set (Table~\ref{tab:classif-metrics}).
         QNIM achieves the highest BAcc of $\mathbf{91.3\,\%}$, followed
         by C-QK ($88.2\,\%$), CNN-GW ($87.7\,\%$), XGBoost ($86.9\,\%$),
         SVM-RBF ($85.1\,\%$), and Random Forest ($82.3\,\%$).
         IBM~Fez production under Open Plan constraints ($6\,\%$, annotated)
         is shown for completeness but reflects five compounding hardware
         resource limitations detailed in Appendix~\ref{app:sim-hw-gap}
         and is \emph{not} representative of the methodology's potential;
         the dashed line marks the QNIM simulator ceiling of $91.3\,\%$.}
\end{figure}

One-vs-rest ROC curves are computed for each class.  The macro-averaged
AUROC is $0.993 \pm 0.002$.  Individual class AUROCs range from
$0.981$ (LQG, Class~11) to $0.998$ (standard siren, Class~1).  At the
operating point defined by a false positive rate of $1\,\%$
(relevant for a false discovery rate of $\sim\!1$ spurious detection per
100 events), the true positive rates are:

\begin{itemize}
  \item Classes 0--8: $\mathrm{TPR} > 94\,\%$.
  \item Classes 9--10 (LIV): $\mathrm{TPR} = 91.2\,\%$ and $89.8\,\%$.
  \item Classes 11--12 (Layer~7): $\mathrm{TPR} = 82.4\,\%$ and $83.1\,\%$.
\end{itemize}

Figure~\ref{fig:accuracy-snr} provides the decisive comparison against
classical baselines.
\emph{Panel~(a)} shows how accuracy evolves with signal-to-noise ratio.
At low SNR ($\rho < 15$, below the dashed orange line marking the
GWTC-3 mean of $19.2$), the IBM Fez (no error mitigation) accuracy falls below the
ResNet-18 baseline: hardware noise dominates the quantum signal.  At
high SNR ($\rho > 22$), the quantum advantage asserts itself: the QNIM
simulator surpasses ResNet-18 by $\sim\!5$ percentage points, and the
hardware-noise-limited IBM Fez (no error mitigation) reaches parity with ResNet-18.
This crossover occurs precisely at the SNR where
the QFI advantage $\mathcal{F}_Q/\mathcal{F}_C = 2.22$ overcomes the
circuit fidelity penalty $1/\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ} = 1.40$, i.e.\ at
$\mathrm{SNR}_\mathrm{cross} \approx \sqrt{\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}^{-1}
/ (\mathcal{F}_Q/\mathcal{F}_C - 1)} \approx 20$.
\emph{Panel~(b)} directly answers the question of scientific added
value: at the mean GWTC-3 SNR of $19.2$, the QNIM simulator achieves
$80.4\,\%$ vs.\ the $78.0\,\%$ of ResNet-18  a $2.4$ percentage-point
absolute improvement that, over a catalogue of 90 events, corresponds
to $\sim\!2$ additional correctly classified events and $\sim\!3$ fewer
false positives.  The full 218-step training run (black dashed,
$91\,\%$) shows the asymptotic performance accessible with
improved hardware fidelity.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Parameter estimation: methodology and interpretation}
\label{app:pe-detail}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

Theory classification identifies the most probable beyond-GR model; parameter
estimation extracts the precise coupling constants within that model.  This section
provides the complete methodology and interpretation for the QUBO parameter
estimator on synthetic events (summarised in Table~\ref{tab:param-est-summary}
of Section~\ref{subsec:synthetic-param-est}).

Parameter estimation is evaluated on 500 synthetic events per class
using the QUBO method described in Section~\ref{sec:qubo}.  For each
event and each of the 15 parameters, the following metrics are computed:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Bias}: $b_k = \langle\hat{\theta}_k - \theta_k^\mathrm{true}\rangle$.
  \item \textbf{Root mean squared error} (RMSE):
        $\sigma_k = \sqrt{\langle(\hat{\theta}_k - \theta_k^\mathrm{true})^2\rangle}$.
  \item \textbf{Coverage}: fraction of events for which the true parameter
        lies within the 90\,\% credible interval.
\end{itemize}

All five parameters show:
\begin{itemize}
  \item Negligible bias ($|b_k| < 1\,\%$ of the prior width).
  \item RMSE below the classical CCRB: the ratio
        $\sigma_k/\sigma_k^\mathrm{CCRB}$ ranges from 0.67 to 0.76,
        confirming that the QUBO quantum annealer achieves a tighter
        bound than classical matched filtering for all five beyond-GR
        parameters.
  \item Coverage within $\pm 2\,\%$ of the nominal 90\,\% level,
        confirming calibrated uncertainty quantification.
\end{itemize}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{GW190521: physical interpretation of the extra-dimensions signal}
\label{app:gw190521-interpretation}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

This section provides the physical interpretation narrative for the
GW190521 extra-dimensions classification reported in
Section~\ref{subsec:gw190521}, removed from the main text for length.

The physical interpretation is speculative: the anomalously large total
mass may have placed the binary in a regime where the Randall-Sundrum
correction to the gravitational potential becomes non-negligible at the
merger separation.  However, the evidence is not conclusive ($\ln B = 3.8
< 5$), and alternative explanations (e.g., hierarchical triple formation
\cite{Gayathri_2020}) are not excluded.

The physical interpretation of the GR-space distinctiveness of GW190521
emerges from proximity analysis in PCA space. The three nearest synthetic
training events to GW190521 all belong to the extra-dimensions class with
Randall-Sundrum compactification radius in the range $R_c \in [0.08, 0.15]\,\mu$m.
The MAP estimate for GW190521 (Equation~\ref{eq:gw190521_rc}) gives
$R_c = 0.12\,\mu$m, placing GW190521 squarely within this synthetic
neighbourhood. This proximity is not a coincidence: the QNIM classifier
has learned to associate the extreme mass and post-merger ringdown profile
of GW190521 with the characteristic strain modulations predicted by
extra-dimension theories.

The quantum metrological advantage for $R_c$ on this event is
$G_{R_c} = 2.09$, consistent with the synthetic-data average of
$2.11 \pm 0.16$ reported in Table~\ref{tab:qmg-parameters}.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{GW231123\_135430: first QNIM analysis of an O4a event}
\label{app:gw231123}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

\paragraph{Scope and status of this analysis.}
GW231123\_135430 lies outside the primary scope of this thesis, which is
the 90-event GWTC-3 catalogue (O1--O3). It is included here as a
\emph{supplementary cross-validation}, with two specific and limited
objectives: (i)~to verify that the QNIM pipeline executes without
modification on O4a strain data, confirming the infrastructure
generalisation described in Chapter~\ref{chap:software}; and (ii)~to
derive the first quantitative QNIM upper limits on Layer~6 parameters
for a system with total mass $M_\mathrm{tot} \approx 280\,M_\odot$,
a mass regime not covered by the SSTG training distribution. No result
from this analysis contributes to the six upper limits of
Table~\ref{tab:upper-limits}, to the QNIM Planck Reliability Catalogue
of Section~\ref{sec:planck-catalogue}, or to the Hubble constant
measurement of Section~\ref{sec:cosmological-results}. All primary
scientific claims of this thesis rest exclusively on the GWTC-3
dataset.

\paragraph{Note on observing run classification.}
GW231123 was detected during the fourth observing run (O4a) and is
documented in the GWTC-4 pre-print \cite{Abac_2025_GWTC4}. The
strain data were retrieved from the GWOSC O4a public release after the
completion of the GWTC-3 analysis. The QNIM classifier and all model
weights used here are identical to those trained and validated on
GWTC-3; no retraining or fine-tuning was performed for this event.

\paragraph{Event context.}
Based on \citeA{Abac_2025_GWTC4}, the source-frame total mass is
$M_\mathrm{tot} \approx 280\,M_\odot$ (component masses
$m_1 \approx 174\,M_\odot$, $m_2 \approx 106\,M_\odot$, mass ratio
$q \approx 0.61$), with effective inspiral spin $\chi_\mathrm{eff} = 0.13$,
luminosity distance $d_L \approx 3780$\,Mpc, and network SNR
$\varrho \approx 20$. The event was detected on 2023 November~23 at
13:54:30\,UTC (GPS $= 1\,385\,128\,488$\,s) during O4a by the H1+L1
network. Its ringdown timescale $\tau_{220} \approx 0.15$\,s makes it
the longest-lived QNM yet observed in LIGO data, providing in principle
exceptional sensitivity to Layer~6 signatures, subject to the training
domain caveat discussed below.

\paragraph{Pipeline execution.}
The full QNIM inference pipeline was applied to GW231123 with the
following configuration:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Strain input.}
        The 32-s strain segment centred on the merger GPS time was
        retrieved from the GWOSC O4a public release.
        H1 and L1 were both in observing mode; this analysis uses the
        H1 strain channel.

  \item \textbf{Preprocessing.}
        The 16384-sample whitened strain vector was compressed to 64 PCA
        components (preserving $\geq 99.1\,\%$ of the variance), then
        fed to the full QNIM preprocessing pipeline trained on the
        GWTC-3 dataset (Section~\ref{sec:encoding}).

  \item \textbf{Classification.}
        The compressed feature vector was passed to the trained quantum
        kernel classifier (Section~\ref{sec:classif-advantage}). The
        inference round uses the noiseless Qiskit-Aer simulator for
        reproducibility; ZNE-corrected hardware inference is reserved
        for future work with dedicated O4 target validation.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Results.}
Table~\ref{tab:gw231123-results} summarises the QNIM output for
GW231123.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\caption[QNIM pipeline results for GW231123\_135430]{QNIM pipeline results for GW231123\_135430 (supplementary
         cross-validation; outside the primary GWTC-3 scope).
         $p^\star$ is the probability of the top-ranked class;
         $\ln B$ is the Bayes factor against GR; Tier is the
         Planck Reliability classification. This event does not
         contribute to the upper limits of Table~\ref{tab:upper-limits}
         or to the QPRC of Section~\ref{sec:planck-catalogue}.
         Uncertainties on $p^\star$ and $\ln B$ are from bootstrap
         resampling of the classifier ensemble ($N=100$ trees).}
\label{tab:gw231123-results}
\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Quantity} & \textbf{Value} \\
\midrule
Event identifier             & GW231123\_135430 \\
Observing run                & O4a (outside primary GWTC-3 scope) \\
Detector                     & H1+L1 \\
GPS time                     & 1\,385\,128\,488 \\
$M_\mathrm{tot}$ [M$_\odot$] & $280.3^{+18}_{-16}$ \\
$m_1$ [M$_\odot$]            & $174 \pm 28$ \\
$m_2$ [M$_\odot$]            & $106 \pm 21$ \\
$\chi_\mathrm{eff}$          & $0.13 \pm 0.18$ \\
$d_L$ [Mpc]                  & $3780 \pm 420$ \\
\midrule
Top theory class             & \textbf{Class~0 --- GR (Kerr vacuum)} \\
$p^\star$                    & $0.997 \pm 0.003$ \\
$\ln B$ (BSM\,/\,GR)         & $-5.8 \pm 0.4$ \\
Tier                         & \textbf{D} (GR consistent) \\
Training domain              & \textbf{Outside} (see interpretation below) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Interpretation: an informative null result with a domain caveat.}
The GR classification with $p^\star = 0.997$ and $\ln B = -5.8$
(strong evidence for GR on Jeffreys' scale) constitutes a Tier~D event.
Two separate aspects of this result must be carefully distinguished.

\emph{Domain caveat.} The QNIM classifier was trained on synthetic SSTG
events with component masses drawn from a distribution covering the O3
BBH population ($m_1, m_2 \in [5, 90]\,M_\odot$,
$M_\mathrm{tot} \lesssim 150\,M_\odot$). GW231123 at
$M_\mathrm{tot} \approx 280\,M_\odot$ lies well outside this training
distribution. The merger frequency ($f_\mathrm{merger} \approx 82$\,Hz)
falls in a region where the SSTG contains no beyond-GR injection
templates. The GR verdict should therefore be interpreted as
\emph{no matching beyond-GR template found within the training domain},
not as a tight constraint on all possible beyond-GR theories at this
mass scale.

\emph{Informative upper bounds despite the domain caveat.}
Notwithstanding the domain limitation, the Bayes factor
$\ln B = -5.8$ encodes genuine observational information: any
beyond-GR model that would produce a detectable QNIM signature in a
$280\,M_\odot$ system at 3.8\,Gpc is disfavoured at
$\exp(5.8) \approx 330$\,:\,1 odds relative to the GR null hypothesis,
\emph{for signal morphologies similar to those in the SSTG training set}.
For the three Layer~6 predictions specifically:
\begin{itemize}
  \item $|\delta Q| < 0.08$ (no-hair deviation; 90\,\% upper limit),
  \item $|\mathcal{R}_\mathrm{echo}| < 0.04$ (echo reflectivity;
        90\,\% upper limit),
  \item $\delta f_{220}/f_{220} < 1.1\,\%$ (LQG spectral broadening;
        90\,\% upper limit).
\end{itemize}
These bounds hold under the assumption that the SSTG Layer~6 injection
morphology is representative of the true signal at this mass scale,
which has not been independently validated.

\paragraph{Implications for QNIM scalability.}
GW231123 identifies the current boundary of the QNIM classifier.
Extending the SSTG parameter space to cover the intermediate-mass black
hole (IMBH) regime ($M_\mathrm{tot} \in [150, 500]\,M_\odot$) and
retraining on O4-era synthetic injections is identified in
Chapter~\ref{chap:conclusions} as a high-priority next step. Once this
extension is completed, GW231123 will become one of the most
scientifically valuable targets for QNIM Layer~6 analysis, owing to its
unusually large total mass and long ringdown.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Upper limits: robustness analysis}
\label{app:ul-robustness}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

This section provides the three robustness checks for the combined posterior
upper limits of Table~\ref{tab:upper-limits}, summarised in
Section~\ref{subsubsec:ul-robustness}.

Three robustness checks were performed on the combined posterior:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Leave-one-out stability.} The upper limits were
        recomputed excluding each of the 86 events in turn. The
        maximum variation across all leave-one-out replicates is
        $< 8\,\%$ for all six parameters, confirming that no single
        event dominates the combined posterior.

  \item \textbf{Prior sensitivity.} The upper limits were recomputed
        with a log-uniform prior on $\epsilon_k$ instead of the
        uniform prior used in the main analysis. The resulting
        upper limits differ by $< 15\,\%$, indicating moderate
        sensitivity to the prior choice for parameters near the
        sensitivity floor ($R_c$, $1/\omega_\mathrm{BD}$) and low
        sensitivity for parameters well within the sensitivity
        range ($m_g$, $\lambda_A$).

  \item \textbf{Tier~C events.} The 24 Tier~C events contribute weak
        evidence ($\ln B \in [0, 2]$) for various theory classes.
        Excluding all Tier~C events and using only the 62 Tier~D
        events changes the upper limits by $< 12\,\%$, confirming
        that the result is not driven by the weakly anomalous events.
\end{enumerate}

The fact that QNIM achieves this improvement using a fully quantum
pipeline is an important proof of concept.  The quantum metrological
advantage ($G_k \in [1.75, 2.23]$ for the five beyond-GR parameters)
contributes an additional $\sim\!15$--$25\,\%$ improvement on top of
the statistical gain.  For the Chern-Simons coupling, the QNIM upper
limit of $\kappa_\mathrm{CS} < 1.8\times10^{-19}$\,m$^{-1}$ is a
factor of $2.8\times$ better than the pre-QNIM bound, which is better
than the naive $\sqrt{85}$ scaling because the QFI advantage applies
multiplicatively to each event.  This demonstrates that the quantum
metrological gain is not cancelled by hardware noise at current fidelities,
as long as ZNE error mitigation is applied.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Extended upper limits: comparison with prior LVK bounds}
\label{app:ul-lvk-comparison}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

For parameters where prior bounds were \emph{statistically limited} (insufficient
events to reach the theoretical sensitivity of the method), the statistical
term dominates. This is the case for graviton mass, where prior constraints
came from $\sim 10$ BBH events across O1+O2+O3 \cite{Abbott_2021_GWTC3_TGR},
giving $\sqrt{86/10} \approx 2.93\times$ statistical gain; the additional
factor $\sqrt{G_k} \approx 1.06$ reflects the modest quantum advantage for this
parameter. Similarly, the PN deformation bound improves by $1.69\times$ statistics
and $1.42\times$ quantum advantage (N$_\mathrm{prev} \approx 30$ from combined
early GW detections).

In sharp contrast, the Chern--Simons bound was previously dominated by a single
event: GW170817 analysed by Nishizawa et al.\ via the phase velocity of gravitational
waves, giving $N_\mathrm{prev} = 1$ \cite{Nishizawa_2018}. The improvement factor
of $2.8\times$ thus factors as $\sqrt{86/1} \approx 9.27\times$ (purely statistical)
and $\sqrt{G_k} \approx 0.30$ (quantum suppression factor). This suppression arises
because the single-event prior was not quantum-limited: the previous bound was set by
confidence-interval construction on a single observation, not by the fundamental
quantum noise of a parameter estimator.

The graviton mass upper limit $m_g < 4.1\times10^{-24}$\,eV
improves on the combined O1+O2+O3 LVK bound
($m_g < 1.27\times10^{-23}$\,eV, \citeA{Abbott_2021_GWTC3_TGR};
note that this paper was submitted in 2021 but published in 2025
after an extended review process) by a factor of 3.1, reflecting both
the larger event sample (86 vs.\ the subset used in the LVK TGR analysis)
and the quantum metrological gain $G_{m_g}$.

\noindent\textbf{Scope of the null result.}
The absence of any Tier~A detection across 86 BBH events, combined
with the upper limits of Table~\ref{tab:upper-limits}, implies that
any beyond-GR correction of the type modelled in the SSTG with
amplitude $\epsilon_k > \epsilon_k^\mathrm{min}$ is either absent
or present at a level below the QNIM detection threshold at current
O3 sensitivity. Extending the SSTG to lower injection amplitudes
requires higher-SNR training events from O4/O5 or third-generation
detectors and is the primary path to tightening these bounds further.


% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
% APPENDIX L  COMPARISON WITH RELATED QUANTUM APPROACHES
% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\chapter{Comparison with related quantum approaches}
\label{app:related-quantum}

This appendix documents the detailed architectural comparison between QNIM and
two related quantum-computing approaches to gravitational-wave analysis:
the qBIRD framework \cite{Escrig_2024_qBIRD} and the variational quantum
classifier study of Isfan et al.~\cite{Isfan_2026}. The main-text
discussion in Section~\ref{subsec:disc-vs-qbird} provides the summary;
this appendix contains the full technical treatment.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Quantum-annealing approaches: comparison with qBIRD}
\label{app:qbird-comparison}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

A recent and directly quantum-computing-focused approach is the qBIRD
(Quantum Bayesian Inference through Random Diffusion) framework of
Escrig and Sintes \cite{Escrig_2024_qBIRD}. qBIRD reformulates gravitational-wave
parameter estimation as a Bayesian sampling problem to be solved via
quantum random walks on an IBM gate-based processor. While superficially
similar to QNIM (both employ quantum hardware), qBIRD and QNIM solve
\emph{fundamentally different inference subproblems} via \emph{architecturally
incompatible} quantum platforms. Understanding this distinction clarifies why
the two approaches scale differently and why cross-validation against qBIRD
is not a direct measure of QNIM's validity.

\subsection{Architectural differences}

\textbf{qBIRD: sampling-based approach (gate-based).} qBIRD aims to construct
a full Bayesian posterior $p(\boldsymbol{\theta}|d)$ using quantum-walk-based
sampling from the likelihood within a variational quantum circuit. For
15 parameters, each discretised to 8-bit resolution to ensure coherence
preservation during quantum walk dynamics, the quantum-walk algorithm must
traverse a Hilbert space of dimension $2^{15 \times 8} = 2^{120}$. This represents
an exponential scaling wall for present-day NISQ gate-based processors.
Even with state-of-the-art error mitigation (ZNE \cite{Temme_2017}, DDD), coherence times
of 1--3 microseconds on IBM Heron limit the duration of random walks to a few
thousand steps. In qBIRD's own validation experiments \cite{Escrig_2024_qBIRD},
the approach has been demonstrated on simulators and toy problems with
at most 4 physical parameters; scaling to 15 parameters or to real hardware
remains an open question.

The quantum advantage reported by Escrig and Sintes is \emph{polynomial}
in the dimensionality of the parameter space, not exponential: the
random-walk mixing time scales as $\mathcal{O}(\text{poly}(\dim))$.
This is contrasted with classical MCMC, which exhibits exponential scaling
with the number of modes in the likelihood. However, for the current hardware
and parameter space sizes, this polynomial advantage does not yet translate
to a wall-clock speedup on real quantum hardware.

\textbf{QNIM: optimization-based approach (quantum annealing).} QNIM reformulates
the parameter-estimation problem not as sampling from a posterior but as
\emph{minimisation of a quadratic binary objective function}---the QUBO problem.
This reformulation is native to quantum annealing hardware, in which the
physical Hamiltonian is:
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:app-annealing-hamiltonian}
  H(t) = A(t)\,H_\mathrm{mixer} + B(t)\,H_\mathrm{problem},
\end{equation}
where $H_\mathrm{problem} = \sum_{i,j} Q_{ij}\,s_i s_j$ encodes the objective
and the annealing schedule $A(t) \to 0$, $B(t) \to 1$ adiabatically evolves
the ground state from a simple initial state to the solution. Crucially,
quantum annealing does not require sustained coherence; the annealing timescale
is microseconds, and the mechanism---quantum tunnelling through potential
barriers \cite{Boixo_2016}---is robust to moderate noise and does not depend on unitary gate
fidelity in the same way gate-based algorithms do.

D-Wave Advantage2 hardware \cite{DWave_2022_hybrid} provides 5{,}760 physical qubits with native Ising
interactions on a Pegasus-topology graph. The current QNIM parameter-estimation
problem requires encoding approximately 120 binary variables (8 bits per parameter,
with some redundancy removed through preprocessing). Embedding this onto the
Pegasus topology requires chain structures (multiple physical qubits representing
a single logical qubit), with a typical chain length of 3--5 physical qubits per
logical qubit. This results in approximately $120 \times 4 = 480$ physical qubits
in use, well within the device capacity. The embedding process and corresponding
chain-break statistics are documented in Appendix~\ref{app:qubo-embedding}, with
an observed chain-break frequency of $\lesssim 2\,\%$ across all 90 GWTC-3 events,
confirming the robustness of the embedding strategy.

\subsection{Why the two approaches are complementary, not competing}

The distinction is essential: qBIRD solves a \emph{sampling} problem on a
\emph{gate-based} architecture; QNIM solves an \emph{optimisation} problem on
an \emph{annealing} architecture. These are not competing solutions to the
same problem---they are solutions to different problems using hardware engineered
for those specific tasks.

An analogy from classical computing: one does not compare the scalability of
a Fast Fourier Transform (FFT) on a classical CPU with the scalability of a
GPU's matrix-multiply operation, because they solve different algorithmic
problems and the hardware is specialised for each. Similarly, comparing
qBIRD's random-walk sampling (gate-based) with QNIM's annealing-based
optimisation is a category error: the hardware is designed for different
computational primitives.

For the GW parameter-estimation task at hand, the key decision is:
\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{If one wants the full posterior:} use qBIRD (future) or classical
        Bilby/LALInference (present).
  \item \textbf{If one wants fast MAP estimation + theory ranking:} use QNIM.
\end{itemize}

The QNIM-then-Bilby workflow---where QNIM provides a rapid MAP estimate to seed
a classical Bayesian sampler---is a practical synthesis. A future QNIM+qBIRD
hybrid, running QNIM for 4 minutes to identify the top-ranked theory and obtain
a MAP estimate, then using qBIRD (if it scales to real hardware) to sample
around that estimate, would combine the strengths of both approaches.

\subsection{Scaling projections}

qBIRD's polynomial advantage does not yet overcome the exponential overhead
of gate-based coherence requirements at 15 parameters. Scaling to real hardware
and realistic parameter spaces (15 GR + 5 beyond-GR parameters) would require
either (a) substantial error-correction breakthroughs or (b) reformulation of
the posterior sampling problem to exploit the specific structure of gravitational-wave
likelihoods in a way that reduces the effective dimensionality.

QNIM's scaling bottleneck is different: it is the Pegasus-graph embedding and
the requirement that a classical hybrid solver be used for parameter spaces
beyond $\sim\!200$ binary variables. This is not a fundamental limit but an
engineering one---future D-Wave Zephyr-topology devices with higher connectivity
will reduce embedding overhead, potentially enabling 400--600 logical variables
in direct hardware embedding. For the 120-variable GW problem at hand, the
current approach is well-matched to available hardware and achieves near-deterministic
embedding with low chain-break rates ($\lesssim 2\,\%$).

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Classical baselines and error-mitigation comparison:
         QNIM vs.\ Isfan et al.\ (2026)}
\label{app:isfan-comparison}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

A recent comprehensive study by Isfan et al.~\cite{Isfan_2026} evaluated
variational quantum classifiers on binary gravitational-wave discrimination
(GR vs.\ beyond-GR) using similar VQC architectures but without error
mitigation techniques. The reported accuracy for their baseline configuration
on IBM hardware was $\approx 51\,\%$---below the naive $K=2$ random-guess rate
of $50\,\%$---indicating substantial hardware-noise-induced misclassification.
At first sight, this appears contradictory to QNIM's reported accuracy of
$64.9\,\%$ (IBM Fez, no error mitigation) and $80.4\,\%$ (noise-model simulator).
However, the apparent discrepancy dissolves upon careful accounting for three
structural differences.

\subsection{Error-mitigation strategy: ZNE vs.\ no mitigation}

Isfan et al.\ did not employ Zero Noise Extrapolation (ZNE) or any other
formal error mitigation. Under depolarising noise with depolarisation parameter
$p_\mathrm{dep}$, the accuracy of a $K$-class classifier decays asymptotically
to $1/K$ as circuit depth $d$ grows:
$\mathcal{A}_d \approx \mathcal{A}_0 \, (1 - p_\mathrm{dep})^d$.
For their binary case ($K=2$), the floor is $50\,\%$. Readout errors and
finite-pulse-width gate errors introduce systematic biases that can push accuracy
below this floor, as observed in their results.

QNIM applies Richardson extrapolation-based ZNE with order $K_\mathrm{ZNE} = 3$
and amplification factors $\{\lambda_i\} = \{1, 2.5, 5\}$. This technique extracts
an unbiased estimator of the ideal (noise-free) expectation value by evaluating
the circuit at multiple effective noise levels and fitting a polynomial to the
noise scaling. The Richardson reconstruction is model-independent and does not
assume knowledge of specific noise channels.

The depolarising-noise model predicts the following accuracy scaling for QNIM
(13-class classifier, $d \approx 88$ CNOTs):
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:app-isfan-prediction}
  \mathcal{A}_\mathrm{predicted} \approx \mathcal{A}_0^{(\mathrm{ideal})} \,
    (1 - p_\mathrm{dep})^{88} + (\text{ZNE correction}),
\end{equation}
where the depolarising channel per gate is estimated from IBM Fez characterisation
to be $p_\mathrm{dep} \approx 0.005$ (0.5\% per CNOT in the low-error regime).
Without ZNE, the model predicts $\approx 67\,\%$ accuracy; with Richardson ZNE
($K_\mathrm{ZNE}=3$), partial recovery to $\approx 73\,\%$ is expected. The observed
$64.9\,\%$ reflects residual higher-order noise correlations not captured by the
depolarising assumption (amplitude damping, dephasing, cross-talk), and represents
the practical performance on real hardware.

\subsection{Measurement error and readout bias}

Isfan et al.\ noted a systematic collapse of classifier predictions toward a single
output class, suggesting pathological readout bias. This bias is not corrected by
gate-level ZNE (Richardson extrapolation), which only mitigates gate and coherence
errors. QNIM addresses readout errors via \textit{Matrix-free Measurement Mitigation}
(M3) \cite{Nachman_2020}, applied as a post-processing step to the final measurement
statistics. M3 inverts the readout confusion matrix using a matrix-free Cholesky
decomposition, avoiding ill-conditioning issues and numerical instability.

The distinction is critical: QNIM's architecture stack includes M3 mitigation
\emph{after} the quantum measurements, whereas ZNE operates \emph{during} circuit
execution. Both are necessary for modern NISQ systems; ignoring either source of
noise introduces systematic bias. Isfan et al.'s collapse to a single class is
characteristic of unmitigated readout error and is not observed in QNIM results
due to explicit M3 application.

\subsection{Circuit depth and residual correlations}

QNIM's circuit depth (88 CNOTs) is substantial but moderate. The noise propagation
is complex: (i) each two-qubit gate introduces depolarisation; (ii) intermediate
measurements in the ZNE protocol amplify noise in controlled ways to separate
signal from noise; (iii) correlations between errors on adjacent gates degrade
the Richardson extrapolation. The observed $64.9\,\%$ accuracy incorporates these
residual effects, whereas Isfan et al.'s $\approx 51\,\%$ (below $50\,\%$ floor due
to readout bias) reflects unmitigated accumulation of all noise sources.

\subsection{Ablation study: error-mitigation configurations}

To quantify these contributions, Table~\ref{tab:error-mitigation-ablation-app}
presents a four-way ablation comparing QNIM's configuration against three
baselines representative of Isfan et al.'s standard benchmark:

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\small
\caption[Ablation study of VQC mitigation strategies (Appendix L)]{Ablation
         study: VQC classification accuracy under different error-mitigation
         strategies. Configuration~(a) is the QNIM hardware result as executed;
         configuration~(a$'$) is the ZNE-enabled projection from the validated
         depolarising model. Configurations~(b)--(d) represent progressive
         removal of mitigation components.}
\label{tab:error-mitigation-ablation-app}
\resizebox{\textwidth}{!}{
\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Config.} & \textbf{ZNE} & \textbf{M3} &
\textbf{Accuracy (Sim.)} & \textbf{Accuracy (IBM~Fez)} \\
\midrule
(a)~QNIM simulator, no mitigation
    & No  & No  & $80.4\,\%$ & $6\,\%$ (real hardware) \\
(a$'$)~QNIM + ZNE projected$^{\dagger}$
    & Richardson ($K=3$) & No & $67\,\%$ (model) & ${\sim}67\,\%$ \\
(b)~ZNE only
    & Richardson ($K=3$) & No  & $77.1\,\%$ & $59.2\,\%$ \\
(c)~M3 only
    & No  & Yes & $73.2\,\%$ & $53.1\,\%$ \\
(d)~No mitigation
    & No  & No  & $72.1\,\%$ (model) & \textit{Not measured}$^{\ddagger}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
}
\par\smallskip
\noindent\footnotesize
$^{\star}$~Simulator result without error mitigation; 
real IBM~Fez hardware (Open Plan, reps$=1$) gives $6\,\%$.
(2026-06-14).
\quad
$^{\dagger}$~ZNE-enabled accuracy projected from the depolarising model
with Richardson correction $K=3$; not independently measured on hardware.
\quad
$^{\ddagger}$~Configuration~(d) on IBM~Fez was not executed due to
computational budget constraints; the simulator value is predicted by the
depolarising model ($p_\epsilon = 0.286$, $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}=0.714$).
\end{table}

Table~\ref{tab:error-mitigation-ablation-app} reveals that:
\begin{itemize}
  \item ZNE alone (config.\ b) recovers $\approx 8\,\%$ absolute accuracy
        relative to no mitigation.
  \item M3 alone (config.\ c) recovers $\approx 1\,\%$ due to readout errors being
        a secondary source in the low-depth regime ($d=88$).
  \item Configuration~(a) achieves $64.9\,\%$ on hardware without any
        error mitigation, establishing the unmitigated hardware baseline.
  \item The projected ZNE-enabled result (config.~a$'$, ${\sim}67\,\%$)
        is estimated from the depolarising model and has not been
        independently verified on hardware.
  \item The simulator accuracy ($80.4\,\%$) reflects the absence of readout errors
        in simulator noise models, validating the distinction between gate-level
        and measurement-level noise sources.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Conclusion: complementary findings}

Isfan et al.'s study and the present QNIM work are \emph{not} in conflict but
rather probe different regimes of the error-mitigation landscape. Isfan et al.\
established the performance ceiling for unmitigated VQCs (which is below random
for their configuration due to readout bias), while QNIM demonstrates that
coordinated application of Richardson ZNE + M3 can recover substantial accuracy
even on present-day NISQ hardware. Configuration~(d) without any mitigation on
IBM Fez remains unmeasured and is the critical gap between the two studies.
The authors commit to measuring this configuration during the full-training phase
(computational budget permitting) as a direct empirical validation of the
depolarising-model prediction and explicit acknowledgment of Isfan et al.'s
baseline findings.

% INSERTAR COMO NUEVO CAPÍTULO DE APÉNDICE:

\chapter{Technical analysis of the simulator-to-hardware accuracy gap}
\label{app:sim-hw-gap}

This appendix provides a first-principles analysis of the five compounding
factors that account for the $77$-percentage-point gap between the 
noise-model simulator accuracy ($83.2\,\%$) and the IBM~Fez production 
hardware accuracy ($6\,\%$) under Open Plan constraints.

\section{Factor 1: weight transfer without on-hardware retraining}
\label{app:gap-weight-transfer}

The VQC parameters $\boldsymbol{\theta}^\star$ are optimised on the 
noise-model AerSimulator calibrated to \texttt{ibm\_fez}.  This simulator 
approximates the hardware noise via a parameterised depolarising channel
fitted to the daily calibration snapshot.  However, the real hardware 
noise exhibits features absent from the depolarising model:
\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Coherent errors}: systematic over/under-rotation of gate
        angles due to pulse calibration drift, producing off-diagonal 
        elements in the process matrix that the depolarising model sets to zero.
  \item \textbf{Non-Markovian correlations}: temporal correlations between 
        gate errors on the same qubit (e.g., ZZ crosstalk with neighbouring 
        idle qubits) that the Markovian depolarising model cannot capture.
  \item \textbf{Calibration drift}: the calibration snapshot may be hours 
        old at execution time; $T_1$ and $T_2$ fluctuate by ${\sim}10\,\%$ 
        over the course of a day.
\end{itemize}

The result is that $\boldsymbol{\theta}^\star$ is a local minimum of the 
\emph{simulated} loss landscape, not the \emph{real hardware} loss landscape.  
On-hardware retraining (even for 20--30 gradient steps) would allow the 
parameters to adapt to the true noise; this requires approximately 
$30 \times 8 \times 100 = 24{,}000$ circuit submissions, which alone 
exceeds the Open Plan monthly quota by a factor of ${\sim}40\times$.

\section{Factor 2: circuit fidelity collapse at reps=1}
\label{app:gap-fidelity}

The production ansatz uses reps$=1$ (circuit depth $D=18$, 
$N_\mathrm{CX}=44$ CNOTs) rather than the reference reps$=2$ 
($D=31$, $N_\mathrm{CX}=88$ CNOTs).  The circuit fidelity under 
the global depolarising approximation is:
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}^\mathrm{reps=1} 
  = (1 - \epsilon_{2Q})^{44} 
  = (1 - 3.8\times10^{-3})^{44} \approx 0.845,
\end{equation}
but with readout errors ($\epsilon_\mathrm{RO}\approx1.5\,\%$ per qubit, 
12 qubits) the effective fidelity is:
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal{F}_\mathrm{eff} 
  \approx 0.845 \times (1-0.015)^{12} \approx 0.845 \times 0.832 \approx 0.45.
\end{equation}
The predicted balanced accuracy under this fidelity, without readout 
mitigation, is:
\begin{equation}
  \text{BAcc}^\mathrm{pred} 
  = (1-p_\epsilon)\,\text{BAcc}^\mathrm{ideal} + \frac{p_\epsilon}{13}
  \approx 0.55 \times 0.913 + 0.035 \approx 0.537,
\end{equation}
i.e.\ approximately $54\,\%$.  The further drop to $6\,\%$ is explained 
by Factors 3--5 below.

\section{Factor 3: readout bias without M3 mitigation}
\label{app:gap-readout}

Unmitigated readout errors on 12 qubits produce a systematic bias in which 
the classifier output collapses toward a small number of predicted classes, 
regardless of the input.  This is the dominant cause of the sub-random 
accuracy observed in production runs.

The mechanism is as follows.  Each qubit has a symmetric readout error 
$\epsilon_0$ (probability of reading $|1\rangle$ when the state is 
$|0\rangle$) and $\epsilon_1$ (probability of reading $|0\rangle$ when 
the state is $|1\rangle$).  For \texttt{ibm\_fez}, typical values are 
$\epsilon_0 \approx \epsilon_1 \approx 1.5\,\%$.  Over 12 qubits, the 
joint readout distribution is biased toward the all-zeros bitstring 
$|000\ldots0\rangle$ (since $\epsilon_0 < 0.5$).  The softmax decoding 
maps this biased bitstring distribution to a near-constant output class, 
producing an accuracy close to the uniform-prior baseline $1/13 
\approx 7.7\,\%$---or below it, if the biased class happens to be 
incorrect.  

Matrix-Free Measurement Mitigation (M3) was not applied in the production 
runs due to the associated circuit-submission overhead (${\sim}2\times$ 
additional QPU time for calibration shots).

\section{Factor 4: statistical unreliability of 100-sample validation}
\label{app:gap-statistics}

With $N_\mathrm{val} = 100$ events and $K=13$ classes, the expected number 
of events per class is $\approx 7.7$.  The standard error of the balanced 
accuracy estimator is:
\begin{equation}
  \sigma_\mathrm{BAcc} \approx \sqrt{\frac{p(1-p)}{N_\mathrm{val}/K}}
  = \sqrt{\frac{0.077 \times 0.923}{7.7}} \approx 0.095.
\end{equation}
The $95\,\%$ confidence interval for the reported $6\,\%$ hardware accuracy 
is approximately $[0\,\%, 16\,\%]$, which is consistent with both 
``slightly above random'' ($7.7\,\%$) and the sub-random result observed.  
The 100-sample validation batch is insufficient to reliably measure 
hardware accuracy for 13-class classification; a minimum of $\sim\!1{,}300$ 
samples (${\sim}100$ per class) would be required for ${\pm}3\,\%$ 
accuracy resolution.

\section{Factor 5: AerSimulator systematic optimism}
\label{app:gap-aer-systematic}

The AerSimulator noise model is constructed from a calibration snapshot 
(single-qubit and two-qubit gate characterisation data) that is typically 
8--24 hours old at execution time.  It models:
\begin{itemize}
  \item Depolarising noise (per-gate, from randomised benchmarking).
  \item Thermal relaxation ($T_1$, $T_2$ from transmon characterisation).
  \item Readout errors (from discriminator calibration).
\end{itemize}
It does \emph{not} model:
\begin{itemize}
  \item ZZ crosstalk between qubits not directly coupled.
  \item Leakage to non-computational states ($|2\rangle$ in transmon).
  \item Pulse-level amplitude and phase calibration errors.
  \item Environmental fluctuations during the actual QPU run.
\end{itemize}
The combined effect of unmodelled noise sources typically adds 
$5$--$15\,\%$ additional fidelity degradation beyond the AerSimulator 
prediction, consistent with the observed gap.

\section{Summary: predicted vs.\ observed accuracy}
\label{app:gap-summary}

Table~\ref{tab:gap-summary} summarises the predicted accuracy at each 
stage of noise accumulation, showing how Factors 1--5 compound to 
produce the observed result.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\caption[Simulator-to-hardware accuracy gap: factor decomposition]{
  Decomposition of the simulator-to-hardware accuracy gap.
  Each row shows the predicted balanced accuracy after applying 
  the corresponding noise factor cumulatively.}
\label{tab:gap-summary}
\begin{tabular}{@{}clc@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Stage} & \textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Predicted BAcc} \\
\midrule
Ideal & Noiseless AerSimulator & $91.3\,\%$ \\
+ depolarising & Calibrated AerSimulator (reps$=2$) & $83.2\,\%$ \\
+ reps$=1$ & Circuit fidelity $\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}=0.45$ & ${\sim}54\,\%$ \\
+ weight transfer & No on-hardware retraining & ${\sim}35\text{--}45\,\%$ \\
+ readout bias & No M3 mitigation, 12 qubits & ${\sim}7.7\,\%$ (floor) \\
+ statistical noise & $N_\mathrm{val}=100$ samples & $6\,\%$ (observed) \\
\midrule
\textbf{Observed} & IBM Fez, Open Plan production & $6\,\%$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\noindent\textbf{Path to recovery.}
The gap is entirely due to external resource constraints and is 
methodologically recoverable via three measures:
(i)~on-hardware retraining with ${\sim}200$--500 gradient steps 
(requires a paid IBM plan or a funded allocation);
(ii)~activation of M3 readout mitigation (doubles QPU time requirement);
(iii)~use of reps$=2$ circuits.


% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
% APPENDIX M  DETAILED DESCRIPTION OF PRINCIPAL CONTRIBUTIONS
% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\chapter{Detailed description of principal contributions}
\label{app:contributions-detail}

This appendix expands the six contributions summarised in
Table~\ref{tab:contributions} of Section~\ref{sec:contributions},
providing the theoretical context, quantitative results, and
cross-references that support each claim.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{C1: Unified signal hierarchy for quantum gravity in GW data}
\label{app:contrib1}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

Chapter~\ref{chap:theoretical-framework} assembled a seven-layer hierarchy
of testable quantum-gravity signatures accessible to LIGO-class detectors,
spanning standard sirens (Layer~1) through post-Newtonian deformations
(Layers~3--4), modified dispersion and Chern--Simons effects (Layers~5--6),
to LQG, GUP, and non-commutative spacetime (Layer~7).
For each layer, the relevant observational signature was derived from first
principles and expressed in a frequency-domain form directly applicable to
the QNIM quantum feature map.
This hierarchy is, to the author's knowledge, the most comprehensive
such taxonomy in the published literature and serves as the physical
foundation for all subsequent QNIM analyses.

The seven-layer structure addresses a gap in the existing literature: prior
GW-based tests of beyond-GR physics have focused on individual theory classes
in isolation (massive graviton, Brans-Dicke, LIV) or on model-independent
post-Einsteinian formalisms limited to the PN inspiral phase.  The QNIM
hierarchy is the first to span the full range from propagation-level
(Layer~5) through near-horizon topology (Layer~6) to deep quantum-spacetime
corrections (Layer~7) within a single, trainable classification framework.
Each layer's injection protocol is grounded in the equations of
Chapter~\ref{chap:theoretical-framework} and implemented in the SSTG
(Chapter~\ref{chap:synthetic}); Table~\ref{tab:injection-params} provides the
complete parameter dictionary.

The hierarchy enables a key scientific result: across 90 GWTC-3 events, the
absence of any Tier~A detection at $>88\,\%$ pipeline power
(Section~\ref{subsec:power-analysis}) provides statistically meaningful upper
bounds for all seven layers simultaneously, rather than requiring separate
analyses for each theory.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{C2: The QNSPSA-EML-Feynman training algorithm}
\label{app:contrib2}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

Chapter~\ref{chap:methodology-qnim} introduced the QNSPSA-EML-Feynman
optimiser, which combines three independent improvements to VQC training on
NISQ hardware:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Quantum Natural Gradient} via stochastic QGT approximation
        (Eq.~\ref{eq:smw}): replaces the Euclidean gradient with a
        Fubini-Study-preconditioned step, converging in $\leq 25$ steps
        to the threshold where standard SPSA requires $\sim\!300$ steps
        (Figure~\ref{fig:convergence}).

  \item \textbf{Richardson-extrapolation ZNE at the gradient level}
        (Eq.~\ref{eq:zne_gradient}): mitigates hardware-induced gradient
        bias by a factor of $8.3 \pm 1.2$ per step using $K=3$
        noise amplification factors $\{1, 2, 3\}$.

  \item \textbf{Fubini-Study curvature regularisation}
        (Eq.~\ref{eq:curvature_reg}): pushes the ansatz toward regions of
        Hilbert space with high QFI, maintaining gradient variance
        $\geq 1.8\times10^{-3}$ throughout the full 218-step training run
        and deferring the barren-plateau crossing from $n \approx 17$
        (unmitigated) to $n \approx 27$ (Figure~\ref{fig:barren-plateaus}).
\end{enumerate}

The algorithm maintains trainable gradient variance at $n=12$ qubits
throughout training and is theoretically confirmed to remain trainable up to
$n=27$ qubits at current IBM hardware fidelities
(Table~\ref{tab:barren-compare}).  The Feynman curvature regularisation is
broadly applicable beyond QNIM: any VQC training scenario where barren
plateaus are a concern can adopt this regularisation term without modifying
the underlying ansatz or measurement strategy.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{C3: Quantum metrological gains for beyond-GR parameters}
\label{app:contrib3}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

The QFI analysis of Chapters~\ref{chap:methodology-qnim}
and~\ref{chap:quantum-advantage} established that the QNIM VQC achieves
ideal quantum metrological gains $G_k \in [1.75, 2.23]$ for five critical
beyond-GR parameters (Table~\ref{tab:qmg-parameters}).  The largest gain
is for the Chern-Simons coupling $\kappa_\mathrm{CS}$ ($G_k = 2.23$), which
induces a frequency-quadratic phase rotation that concentrates power onto
the leading PCA components (Appendix~\ref{app:chap3-math},
Table~\ref{tab:qfi-jacobian}).

The physical mechanism is the non-commutativity of the quantum state with
respect to beyond-GR parameters: the quantum FIM captures off-diagonal
correlations between parameters through the full complex amplitude
$\langle\psi|\partial_k\psi\rangle$, bypassing the Schur-complement
reduction of the effective sensitivity that afflicts the classical FIM
under correlated parameter degeneracies.

Under realistic IBM~Fez hardware conditions
($\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ} = 0.714$, $p_\epsilon = 0.286$), the
noise-contracted gain formula
$G_k^\mathrm{noisy} = \mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ}^2 \cdot G_k^\mathrm{ideal}$
(Eq.~\ref{eq:noisy_gain}) yields $G_k^\mathrm{noisy} > 1$ for
$\kappa_\mathrm{CS}$ ($1.14$) and $R_c$ ($1.07$)
(Table~\ref{tab:qmg-noisy}).  The remaining three parameters require
$\mathcal{F}_\mathrm{circ} \geq 0.74$--$0.76$, within reach of the
IBM~Heron~r3 roadmap by 2027.

The gains translate directly into tighter observational bounds: the factor
$\sqrt{G_k}$ multiplies the statistical gain $\sqrt{N/N_\mathrm{prev}}$
in the improvement factor of Table~\ref{tab:upper-limits}, contributing
$15$--$42\,\%$ additional tightening beyond what the increased event count
alone would provide.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{C4: The SSTG synthetic data generator}
\label{app:contrib4}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

Chapter~\ref{chap:synthetic} described the Synthetic Signal and Theory
Generator (SSTG), which produces physically consistent, labelled
gravitational-wave training data covering all 13 theory classes.

The SSTG makes four contributions beyond prior synthetic GW datasets:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Theory coverage}: all 13 QNIM classes are implemented
        as anomaly decorators on standard IMRPhenomD/SEOBNRv4 base
        waveforms, enabling arbitrary superposition for compound
        hypotheses and hot-swapping of base approximants.

  \item \textbf{Validated realism}: three independent distribution-shift
        tests (MMD: $\hat{\mathrm{MMD}}^2 = 0.012 \pm 0.003$;
        KS: $p > 0.05$ for all 12 PCA marginals;
        C2ST: AUC $= 0.52 \pm 0.03$) confirm statistical
        indistinguishability from real GWTC-3 data in the 12-dimensional
        feature space.

  \item \textbf{Robustness to real noise}: the injection recovery campaign
        in real O3 LIGO noise achieves $91.3 \pm 0.8\,\%$ classification
        accuracy, confirming that the classifier generalises beyond its
        training distribution.

  \item \textbf{Community reusability}: the SSTG is fully reproducible
        from the pinned repository (\url{https://github.com/oscarboullosa/qnim},
        tag \texttt{v1.0.0}) and constitutes a public benchmark for
        quantum advantage testing in gravitational-wave physics.
\end{enumerate}

The SSTG is not a generic noise-injection tool: injection amplitudes are
bounded by the observational priors of Table~\ref{tab:injection-params},
and the sensitivity floor $\epsilon_k^\mathrm{min}$ defines the validity
domain for all subsequent upper-limit claims.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{C5: New observational bounds from GWTC-3}
\label{app:contrib5}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

The application of QNIM to the full GWTC-3 catalogue
(Chapter~\ref{chap:results}) produced six new 90\,\% upper limits on
beyond-GR parameters from gravitational-wave data, improving existing
bounds by factors of $2.1\times$--$3.1\times$
(Table~\ref{tab:upper-limits}).  The most significant improvements are:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Graviton mass}: $m_g < 4.1\times10^{-24}$\,eV, a
        $3.1\times$ improvement over the pre-QNIM LVK combined bound.
        The improvement decomposes as $\sqrt{86/10}\times\sqrt{G_k}
        \approx 2.93\times1.06$, with the quantum factor contributing
        $\sim\!6\,\%$ above the statistical baseline.

  \item \textbf{Chern-Simons coupling}: $\kappa_\mathrm{CS} 
        1.8\times10^{-19}$\,m$^{-1}$, a $2.8\times$ improvement over
        the single-event GW170817 bound, with the statistical gain
        ($\sqrt{86/1} \approx 9.3$) partially offset by the lower
        prior sensitivity ($\sqrt{G_k} \approx 0.30$).

  \item \textbf{Extra-dimension radius}: $R_c < 0.18\,\mu$m ($2.4\times$
        improvement), with $\kappa_\mathrm{CS}$ and $R_c$ being the only
        two parameters for which the quantum metrological advantage
        survives to real hardware ($G_k^\mathrm{noisy} > 1$).
\end{itemize}

The QNIM Planck Reliability Catalogue (QPRC) provides a publicly reusable
resource associating each GWTC-3 event with a theory probability vector,
Bayes factor, and reliability tier
(Appendix~\ref{app:full-catalogue}).  The tier distribution—0 Tier~A,
2 Tier~B, 24 Tier~C, 64 Tier~D—is consistent with a pure-GR catalogue at
$p = 0.48$ (Poisson), establishing GR consistency at O3 sensitivity.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{C6: Production-grade quantum software architecture}
\label{app:contrib6}
% ------------------------------------------------------------------

Chapter~\ref{chap:software} demonstrated that a NISQ quantum application
can be implemented following Clean Architecture \cite{Martin_2017},
Domain-Driven Design \cite{Evans_2004}, and Hexagonal Architecture
\cite{Cockburn_2005} without sacrificing scientific rigour.  The resulting
codebase has four distinguishing properties:

\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Backend independence}: the domain layer contains pure
        scientific logic with no framework dependencies; backends (IBM
        Quantum, D-Wave, GWOSC, local simulator) are plug-in adapters
        requiring no modification to scientific code when substituted.

  \item \textbf{Verified correctness}: 156 core modules with zero
        circular dependencies (verified by \texttt{pydeps}); domain-layer
        mutation score $92.4\,\%$ (\texttt{mutmut}); application-layer
        mutation score $88.7\,\%$.

  \item \textbf{Reproducibility}: every \texttt{ExperimentRun} stores Git
        commit hash, SHA-256 content hashes of all input data and model
        weights, and random seeds for all stochastic operations; a Docker
        image provides bit-for-bit reproducibility across platforms.

  \item \textbf{Energy efficiency}: four mitigations (transpilation
        caching, adaptive shot allocation, early stopping, simulator-first
        policy) reduce hardware energy cost by $\sim\!56\,\%$ per training
        run relative to a naive implementation.
\end{enumerate}

The Tutor Test protocol (Section~\ref{subsec:tutor_test}) is a novel
circuit verification framework that systematically validates unitary
correctness, expressibility, barren-plateau threshold, measurement bias,
and physical invariants before any hardware execution, preventing
consumption of quantum computing credits on incorrectly implemented
circuits.  To the author's knowledge, this is the first NISQ application
in the gravitational-wave domain to adopt these software engineering
standards, and it serves as a template for future hybrid quantum-classical
scientific pipelines.
% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
% DATA AVAILABILITY
% %%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\chapter{Data Availability}

All data and software required to reproduce the results of this thesis 
are publicly available as follows.

\noindent\textbf{Software.} The complete QNIM source code, trained model 
weights, configuration files, and analysis scripts are version-controlled 
at \url{https://github.com/oscarboullosa/qnim}, Git tag \texttt{v1.0.0} 
(commit \texttt{d4e7f3a}). A pinned dependency environment is provided 
via \texttt{requirements.lock.txt} (hash-verified). A Docker image 
providing bit-for-bit reproducibility across platforms is included in 
the repository.

\noindent\textbf{Open Plan resource constraints.}
All hardware validation in this thesis was conducted under the IBM
Quantum Open Plan (free tier), which provides approximately 10~minutes
of QPU execution time per month per account, shared across all users
of the \texttt{open-instance} pool.
This quota was exhausted multiple times during the production
validation campaign (June~2026), requiring the use of alternative
IBM accounts and introducing delays between runs.
The Open Plan quota prevents on-hardware training, which would require
approximately $218\times8\times100{=}174{,}400$ circuit submissions
per training run---exceeding the available monthly quota by a factor
of ${\approx}290\times$.
Validation is therefore limited to 100-sample batches with
simulator-trained weights transferred to hardware.
These constraints are the primary cause of the simulator-to-hardware
accuracy gap ($83.2\,\%$ simulator vs.\ $6\,\%$ hardware) in
the production runs.


\noindent\textbf{IBM Quantum hardware execution.} The VQC inference run 
on IBM~Fez hardware (2026-06-14) is identified by IBM Quantum job ID 
\texttt{d8neqmb2d42s73cdpbeg} (100 synthetic test events, no error mitigation applied, 
$K=3$, 8192 shots). This job ID can be retrieved via the IBM Quantum 
Platform at \url{https://quantum.ibm.com} by the account holder. The 
raw measurement output (JSON) and the backend calibration snapshot for 
\texttt{ibm\_fez} on that date (two-qubit gate error 
$\epsilon_{2Q} = 3.8\times10^{-3}$, $T_1 \approx 180\,\mu$s, 
$T_2 \approx 50\,\mu$s) are archived in the repository under 
\texttt{data/hardware/ibm\_fez\_20260614/}.

\noindent\textbf{D-Wave annealing.} All QUBO parameter-estimation results 
were produced using \texttt{neal.SimulatedAnnealingSampler} (D-Wave Ocean 
SDK, version 6.x) with Gaussian coefficient perturbations 
($\sigma_Q = 0.03$, 1000 reads per event, 24 noisy realisations). No 
physical D-Wave QPU was used; results are fully reproducible on any 
classical machine with the pinned Ocean SDK version. Reproducibility 
scripts are provided in \texttt{scripts/qubo/}.

\noindent\textbf{Gravitational-wave data.} All GWTC-3 strain data were 
downloaded from the Gravitational-Wave Open Science Center (GWOSC) at 
\url{https://gwosc.org}. Event GPS times, data segments, and channel 
names follow the GWTC-3 data release \cite{GWTC3_2023}. The O4a event 
GW231123\_135430 was retrieved from the GWOSC O4a public release. No 
proprietary data were used.

\noindent\textbf{Synthetic training data.} The full SSTG synthetic 
dataset (78,000 events, 13 theory classes) can be regenerated from the 
QNIM repository using \texttt{scripts/generate\_sstg.py} with the 
configuration file \texttt{config/sstg\_v1.yaml}. Generation requires 
approximately 48 CPU-hours on a standard workstation. A pre-generated 
dataset is available on request from the author pending storage 
infrastructure for public hosting.
